9 Performance Engineering

Purpose

Fleet stack focus for this chapter.

Why can a fleet with abundant compute still fail to serve requests fast enough?

Expensive accelerators can spend much of a request waiting for data, software dispatch, or communication while their arithmetic units remain underused. Adding more machines may raise cost without removing the delay, and a faster isolated kernel may leave the request’s critical path unchanged. The engineering stakes are useful serving capacity: how much work the existing fleet can complete while preserving latency and model quality. A configuration that answers one short request quickly may behave differently when many requests arrive with varied lengths and memory demands. Sharing work can improve throughput, but the extra state competes for memory and individual requests may wait longer. Reducing that state can admit more work only if the resulting computation remains accurate and the execution path can use the freed capacity. These interactions make optimization a question of measured constraints rather than a search for the fastest component. On one machine, a local improvement can be tested against a relatively contained execution path. Across a fleet, its value also depends on communication, synchronization, and the distribution of work across devices. The cost of a mistaken diagnosis grows when the same inefficient execution repeats across many replicas. Reaching useful capacity therefore requires matching numerical representations and execution schedules to the workload, then checking whether local savings survive along the complete request path. In C³ terms, performance engineering makes compute gains accountable to the communication and coordination costs that determine sustained fleet throughput.

Learning Objectives
  • Apply roofline analysis and execution traces to distinguish compute, memory, and launch limits
  • Analyze prefill, decode, and batch-size regimes to predict latency-throughput behavior in LLM serving
  • Diagnose serving bottlenecks using reproducible measurements at kernel, replica, and fleet boundaries
  • Evaluate fusion, tiling, and graph replay against memory traffic, launch overhead, and resource limits
  • Select precision, compilation, and runtime optimizations from bandwidth, quality, and deployment constraints
  • Evaluate speculative decoding and sparse expert routing against acceptance, communication, and load-imbalance costs
  • Construct a serving optimization plan that tests compute, communication, coordination, and quality trade-offs

9.1 The Memory Wall and Roofline Diagnosis

An H100 GPU capable of 989 TFLOP/s of dense FP16 Tensor Core compute can leave more than 99 percent of that compute idle during batch-one language-model decode. The processor is not short on arithmetic; it is starving for data. Each new token depends on the token before it, so every decode step must stream the model’s weights from memory again to produce a single token. That is the autoregressive bottleneck (principle 15) in its most direct form, with no other requests sharing the cost of each weight read. Performance engineering operates inside that constraint: the memory wall, where moving bytes from memory to compute units can cap throughput long before Tensor Cores reach their advertised peak.

Partitioning, synchronization, recovery, and scheduling can put the workload on the right hardware and keep it alive. An orchestrator can hand a serving replica eight accelerators, yet nothing in that allocation guarantees that the replica completes enough useful work to justify its capacity. The orchestrator’s own accounting already exposes the shortfall, because allocated capacity, hardware activity, and productive work are measured separately and can diverge widely, but that accounting cannot say where inside the execution path the missing work went. Across the multi-tier hierarchy of The Fleet Stack, performance engineering connects execution inside the Runtime & Serving row of the Serving band to the compute, communication, and coordination costs established across the physical and distribution tiers. A faster kernel matters when it shortens the request’s critical path, releases memory for concurrent requests, or reduces the capacity needed to meet a service objective. The governing question is therefore which execution constraint limits useful fleet throughput, not which kernel can achieve the highest isolated speedup.

↩︎ The allocated, compute, and productive tiers of fleet resource accounting are established in Resource accounting and observability.

The recurring workload is an autoregressive language-model service. Its concrete instance throughout the chapter is a 70B-parameter model served on one node of eight H100 GPUs, a smaller instance of Archetype A (GPT-4/Llama-3) than that archetype’s 175B reference model, sized so that a single tensor-parallel node forms one complete serving replica. It keeps the property that defines the archetype, a dense transformer too large for one accelerator, so even a single replica spans several accelerators through tensor parallelism and exchanges activations among them in every layer; separate replicas serve different requests.

↩︎ Archetype A and its 175B-parameter reference model are established in Archetype A (GPT-4/Llama-3).

A request first processes its input prompt in a prefill pass, then generates output tokens through successive decode passes. The key-value (KV) cache retains attention keys and values from the prompt and generated tokens so later passes can reuse them rather than recomputing them, though this shifts the bottleneck from compute to memory capacity and bandwidth as the cache grows. Time-to-first-token (TTFT) measures the delay from request arrival to the first output token, while inter-token latency (ITL) measures the gaps between subsequent tokens. Batching shares execution across active requests, but each request still consumes cache capacity and must meet its latency objective.

The estimates in this chapter are bounds and constructed budgets, and each names a limit worth testing. Measurement decides whether that limit binds. An end-to-end trace of the fixed workload first locates the exposed interval, whether waiting for input, communication, memory movement, computation, or host dispatch; only then do kernel counters and the roofline choose among fusion, precision, compilation, or algorithmic change, and the same measurement repeated after each change shows whether a local gain survives at the replica and fleet boundaries. The measurement contract in section 9.7 makes that comparison reproducible.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A training step’s wall-clock time is decomposed via the iron law of ML performance (\(T = D_{\text{vol}}/\text{BW} + O/(R_{\text{peak}} \cdot \eta_{\text{hw}}) + L_{\text{lat}}\)) into 60% memory traffic (\(D_{\text{vol}}/\text{BW}\)), 30% compute execution (\(O/(R_{\text{peak}} \cdot \eta_{\text{hw}})\)), and 10% dispatch and latency overhead (\(L_{\text{lat}}\)). A hardware upgrade doubles the GPU’s peak dense FP16 compute (\(R_{\text{peak}}\)) while leaving memory bandwidth (\(\text{BW}\)) and software latency (\(L_{\text{lat}}\)) unchanged. What is the maximum theoretical speedup this upgrade can achieve on this workload?

    1. Approximately \(1.18\times\) speedup (a 15% reduction in step time), because doubling compute only reduces the 30% compute slice to 15% while the 70% non-compute time remains unchanged
    2. \(2.00\times\) speedup, because doubling peak compute throughput halves the overall execution time of matrix multiplication operations
    3. \(1.67\times\) speedup, because the 30% compute fraction and 10% latency fraction are both accelerated by the faster clock frequency
    4. \(1.43\times\) speedup, because the memory-bound portion is compressed by the faster arithmetic execution units

  2. An operation has an arithmetic intensity of 200 FLOP/byte. On an NVIDIA V100 (ridge point \(\approx 139\text{ FLOP/byte}\)) and an H100 (ridge point \(\approx 295\text{ FLOP/byte}\)), how does the tighter roofline ceiling change across generations, and what is the primary systems implication for performance optimization?

  3. Order the following levels of the GPU memory hierarchy from nearest to the streaming multiprocessor arithmetic units to farthest: (1) L2 Cache, (2) Registers, (3) High-Bandwidth Memory (HBM), (4) Shared Memory (SRAM).

  4. True or False: Arithmetic intensity is a fixed, intrinsic property of a neural network architecture that remains invariant regardless of batch size or kernel implementation choices.

  5. What is the primary energy and performance consequence of repeatedly streaming intermediate tensors through off-chip HBM rather than retaining them in on-chip SRAM?

    1. Off-chip transfers bypass the L2 cache entirely, preventing the GPU memory management unit from performing virtual address translation
    2. Off-chip transfers consume memory-interface energy and can limit throughput through bandwidth and latency, whereas retaining reusable intermediates in SRAM reduces HBM traffic
    3. Streaming through HBM causes numerical drift and precision loss because DRAM capacitors lose charge over multi-nanosecond round-trips
    4. SRAM access draws higher total power because operating at higher clock frequencies increases dynamic capacitance dissipation beyond HBM interface PHYs

See Answers →

9.2 The Single-Machine Iron Law

The memory wall is one term in a larger budget. Equation 1 applies the single-machine iron law to local execution inside a replica, whose inter-device costs the Fleet Law accounts for separately. It decomposes execution time into three competing costs:

\[ \Time= \frac{\DataVolume}{\Bandwidth} + \frac{\Operations}{\PeakRate \cdot \EfficiencyHw} + \Latency \tag{1}\]

In overlapped execution, the roofline-style simplification replaces the sum of compute and data movement with the slower exposed term:

\[ \Time\approx \max\left( \frac{\Operations}{\PeakRate \cdot \EfficiencyHw}, \; \frac{\DataVolume}{\Bandwidth} \right) + \Latency \]

Here, \(\DataVolume\) is the data volume crossing the chosen memory boundary, \(\Bandwidth\) is its bandwidth, \(\Operations\) is the operation count, and \(\PeakRate \cdot \EfficiencyHw\) is the realized compute rate after accounting for hardware execution efficiency. The overhead term \(\Latency\) collects the exposed latency outside those compute and memory terms, and it has two parts. Host dispatch, meaning kernel launches and other software costs, belongs to each device’s own execution. Exposed communication and synchronization arise only because the replica spans several devices. Local execution on each device and exposed inter-device delays together supply the service time of a replica; request queueing and routing add time outside that boundary. The roofline approximation asks which exposed term dominates at a given operating point. Increasing compute throughput for a memory-bound workload does not materially improve performance until the memory term is reduced. Eager framework execution can expose a large slice of the overhead term when the host submits many short kernels one at a time, a cost that section 9.8.1 quantifies and that graph compilation and replay later attack.

In batch-one decode, the data-movement term dominates the iron law.

In the Fleet Law’s terms, the data and compute terms together with the dispatch part of \(\Latency\) contribute to distributed local execution, whose unsharded reference time is \(\UnshardedLocalExecutionTime\), while the communication and synchronization part of \(\Latency\) becomes the Fleet Law’s separate \(\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)\) and \(\SynchronizationTime(\DeviceCount)\) terms. For a measured device shard, partitioning has already reduced the local workload; its execution time must not be divided by the device count again. Independent serving replicas process different requests, so adding replicas raises aggregate capacity without dividing the latency of one request. In the rest of the chapter, overhead means host dispatch unless communication is named. A local optimization therefore reaches the fleet through whichever of those terms it shortens.

↩︎ The Fleet Law, which extends this single-machine iron law across \(\DeviceCount\) devices, is established in The Fleet Law.

Model compression changes either the work a model performs or the representation of its values, and this chapter takes up only the representation side. Pruning and distillation change the model itself and are model-design decisions. Quantization reduces the bytes per value without necessarily reducing the operation count, which makes it a performance-engineering lever; the chapter treats it as precision engineering. Either kind of compression saves time only when the implementation can exploit the smaller model or representation.

Several levers map directly to those terms. When memory traffic dominates, operator fusion and tiling reduce the exposed data-volume term by eliminating intermediate high-bandwidth memory (HBM) round-trips. A fused sequence that keeps its intermediates in SRAM can shrink the exposed memory-access term dramatically; for long-sequence attention the traffic reduction can exceed an order of magnitude, as section 9.8.4 works out. Precision engineering attacks the same numerator from a different angle: FP8, INT4, and KV-cache compression (storing the per-token attention keys and values in fewer bytes) represent each value in fewer bytes, so the same physical bandwidth carries more useful model state.

Other levers change the overhead or compute terms. Graph compilation, including torch.compile, Accelerated Linear Algebra (XLA), and TensorRT, can reduce repeated host dispatch, fuse eligible operations, and plan memory across captured regions. Communication-computation overlap makes communication concurrent with independent useful work. Full hiding requires \(\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount) \leq \UnshardedLocalExecutionTime/\DeviceCount\), the per-device compute time of the Fleet Law (equation 1.3), but the duration comparison alone is insufficient. The schedule must make that independent work ready in time, and resource contention must not erase the gain. When the whole communication interval is hidden, \(\OverlappedTime = \CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)\) and communication adds nothing to the step; \(\OverlappedTime\) can never exceed \(\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)\), because only time actually spent communicating can be hidden. When communication outlasts the available compute window, the overlap term credits the part that was hidden. Algorithmic changes affect the work itself. Speculative decoding lets a small draft model propose several tokens that the full model verifies in one pass, and mixture of experts (MoE) activates only part of the model for each token; section 9.11 develops both.

Each technique attacks a different term, and this taxonomy guides optimization strategy: diagnose which term dominates (using the roofline model from section 9.4.1), then apply the technique targeting that term. A technique targeting a smaller exposed term can still help, but its end-to-end benefit is bounded by that term’s share of execution time.

Checkpoint 9.1: The single-machine iron law

Verify understanding of system-level performance diagnosis:

Triage starts outside the kernel. A missed latency or throughput target with low accelerator utilization points first to input, host, or communication stalls, because no kernel optimization helps an accelerator that is waiting for work. Only once the device is busy does the question become whether its kernels are compute-bound or memory-bound (figure 1). A busy GPU running a workload whose arithmetic intensity sits below the machine’s ridge point, the intensity at which its bandwidth and compute bounds meet (section 9.4.1), routes to memory-bound remedies such as operator fusion or weight quantization.

The iterative diagnostic loop in figure 1 reinforces why profiling must precede optimization: every remediation pathway routes back into re-profiling the workload, ensuring that an engineering intervention targets the active limiter rather than an unexposed cost. A technique that changes a small or hidden term has limited influence on end-to-end time even if it produces a large local speedup. For serving, the missed target is a request-latency or throughput objective; for training, it is step time. Misdiagnosis wastes engineering effort and, worse, a performance intervention deployed without rigorous measurement can shift the system to an inferior operating point on its efficiency frontier.

9.3 The Efficiency Frontier

Diagnosis says which term to shrink; it does not say what the shrinking is for. The objective is a position on the efficiency frontier, the Pareto-optimal subset of the feasible configurations for a stated workload and resource budget. A configuration on this frontier cannot improve one measured dimension without sacrificing another within that set. Its most familiar picture is a two-dimensional projection, the curve of model quality against system throughput. Thompson et al. (2021) show why efficiency matters for deep learning: quality improvements have required rapidly increasing compute, the same appetite that the scaling laws quantify. Scaling laws set how much compute a quality target demands; performance engineering sets how much of the purchased compute becomes useful work, expanding the feasible set by making a quality level achievable at higher throughput. The deployment decision then selects an operating point that meets the application’s latency, cost, and quality requirements.

Thompson, N. C., K. Greenewald, K. Lee, and G. F. Manso. 2021. “Deep Learning’s Diminishing Returns: The Cost of Improvement Is Becoming Unsustainable.” IEEE Spectrum 58 (10): 50–55. https://doi.org/10.1109/mspec.2021.9563954.

↩︎ The power-law relation between compute and model quality that drives fleet growth is established in AI Scaling Laws.

The full frontier spans five measurable dimensions (table 1), and each one trades against at least one other.

The dimensions in table 1 interact in nonobvious ways. Increasing batch size can improve throughput and cost efficiency while degrading latency. Reducing precision can lower memory demand and improve throughput but may degrade quality. Speculative decoding can improve latency but may increase per-token cost. The performance engineer’s task is to navigate these trade-offs guided by the application’s specific requirements.

An interactive chatbot might target time-to-first-token under 200 ms and inter-token latency under 50 ms, accepting a higher per-token cost to meet those illustrative limits. An offline document-summarization pipeline may instead prioritize completed work per dollar, subject to a deadline and a quality threshold. A quality-sensitive application can impose a stricter acceptance criterion while retaining its own latency constraints. These requirements select different feasible operating points; performance engineering must make the trade-off explicit before choosing an optimization.

For the recurring 70B deployment, a latency-oriented configuration might use small batches and speculative decoding to reduce the time a request waits for tokens. A throughput-oriented configuration might use larger batches and INT4 weights to amortize weight reads and free memory for concurrent requests. The larger batch raises aggregate throughput while lengthening each request’s token intervals, and reduced precision is usable only if the model still meets its quality criterion. Once batching has been analyzed, section 9.5.1 prices both operating points in time and cost per token.

9.4 The Memory Wall

The efficiency frontier fixes the objective; memory bandwidth sets the first bound on reaching it. The wall itself is a hardware fact the replica inherits. Batch-one decode streams the active weights from HBM for about one multiply-accumulate per weight read, and on an NVIDIA H100 989 TFLOP/s of dense FP16 Tensor Core compute faces only 3.35 TB/s of peak HBM bandwidth. Performance engineering adds the kernel’s view of that wall. Batching, sparse activation, and on-chip reuse all change the traffic, so the diagnostic question is how far a given kernel sits from the bandwidth bound and which of those levers can move it. The answer depends on the constraint each level of the memory hierarchy places on a kernel’s schedule (table 2).

↩︎ The memory wall, HBM bandwidth per generated token, and the accelerator memory hierarchy are established in The Memory Wall.

Two consequences follow for kernel design. Register pressure becomes a first-class constraint in custom kernels, whether written in CUDA or in a tile-level language such as Triton (section 9.10.5), because tile size sets both arithmetic intensity and register demand. Data placement becomes the optimization target whenever HBM traffic limits execution: operator fusion removes intermediate round trips when a legal schedule can keep the values in registers or shared memory, tiling organizes reuse within limited SRAM (which is why on-chip reuse matters so much for attention), and precision engineering shrinks each trip’s payload by representing values in FP8 or INT4 instead of FP16. Graph compilers apply supported versions of these transformations automatically. Each intervention must still be checked against launch overhead, dependencies, and on-chip resource limits, which can stop a low-intensity kernel before it saturates HBM bandwidth.

9.4.1 The roofline model

The memory wall becomes a diagnostic instrument once each kernel is placed on the roofline model1 (Williams et al. 2009). A kernel with arithmetic intensity \(\ArithmeticIntensity\) attains at most \(\min(\PeakRate, \ArithmeticIntensity\times \Bandwidth)\), and the ridge point \(\RooflineRidgePoint = \PeakRate/\Bandwidth\) separates the memory-bound side of this ideal envelope from its compute-bound side. The tighter ceiling bounds performance; a measurement below that ceiling still requires investigation of latency, dependencies, and implementation limits.

1 Roofline model: The original paper framed the model for multicore CPUs (Williams et al. 2009); the same two-ceiling diagram extends directly to accelerators.

Williams, Samuel, Andrew Waterman, and David Patterson. 2009. “Roofline: An Insightful Visual Performance Model for Multicore Architectures.” Communications of the ACM 52 (4): 65–76. https://doi.org/10.1145/1498765.1498785.

↩︎ The roofline model and the H100 ridge point are established in Roofline model.

Definition 9.1: Measured arithmetic intensity

Measured arithmetic intensity is the ratio of the floating-point operations a kernel actually executes to the bytes observed crossing a named memory boundary, such as HBM, during that execution (FLOP per byte).

  1. Significance: It places a profiled kernel on the same roofline as the bandwidth (\(\Bandwidth\)) and compute (\(\PeakRate\)) ceilings, so its distance below the algorithmic estimate exposes traffic that the algorithm did not require.
  2. Distinction: Unlike algorithmic intensity, which counts the data movement an idealized algorithm requires under a stated memory model, measured intensity includes tiling, cache behavior, fusion, padding, and redundant transfers.
  3. Common pitfall: Arithmetic intensity is not a fixed property of a model. Batch size can change algorithmic reuse, while fusion and tiling primarily change measured HBM intensity by keeping intermediates on chip. A roofline point is interpretable only when its FLOP convention and memory boundary match the ceilings on the same plot.

The H100’s FP16 ridge, established in Roofline model, is the reference point for every placement that follows:

\[I_{\text{ridge}}^{\text{H100, FP16}} = \frac{989\text{ TFLOP/s}}{3.35\text{ TB/s}} \approx 295\text{ FLOP/byte}\]

Below that intensity, the HBM bandwidth bound is tighter than the H100’s FP16 compute bound. FP8 doubles the Tensor Core rate to 1979 TFLOP/s while bandwidth stays at 3.35 TB/s, so the ridge rises to about 590.7 FLOP/byte. If all counted operand traffic halves while the operation count stays fixed, arithmetic intensity doubles as well, preserving the kernel’s position relative to the ridge. In that ideal comparison, lower precision raises the bandwidth-limited ceiling by moving more values per second. Actual gains depend on which tensors change precision and on conversion, scaling, and kernel costs.

The ridge also moves across hardware generations, because peak arithmetic has outgrown HBM bandwidth for the physical reasons The Memory Wall develops. For diagnosis, the ridge column of table 3 matters more than the growth trend, because a kernel’s regime can change with the hardware even when its intensity does not.

At 200 FLOP/byte, an operation lies on the compute side of the V100 and A100 envelopes but on the bandwidth side for the H100 and B200, whose ridges sit roughly 2× above the V100’s 139 FLOP/byte. That comparison holds intensity fixed; a different cache hierarchy or kernel can change the measured traffic as well. Reducing exposed memory traffic becomes more valuable when compute grows faster than bandwidth, but the applicable intervention still follows the measured limit. A hardware upgrade therefore calls for re-profiling rather than an assumed speedup.

9.4.2 Workload placement on the roofline

The following operations span three orders of magnitude in estimated arithmetic intensity. Their roofline positions identify candidate limits; the traffic assumptions explain what must be checked against a real implementation.

Large general matrix multiply (GEMM) operations expose substantial opportunities for data reuse. A square matrix multiplication of dimension \(4096{\times}4096\) in FP16 performs approximately 137.4 billion FLOPs. Counting one read of each input matrix and one write of the output gives roughly 100.7 MB of traffic and an arithmetic intensity of approximately 1365.3 FLOP/byte. This idealized intensity sits above the H100’s ridge point. Reaching the compute ceiling still requires a kernel whose tiling, instruction mix, and parallelism exploit that reuse; tile reloads can increase measured HBM traffic.

Element-wise operations expose much less reuse. For a Gaussian error linear unit (GELU) activation applied to a \(4096{\times}4096\) tensor, the illustrative accounting assumes 5 operations per element and one load and store, yielding an arithmetic intensity of approximately 1.2 FLOP/byte. The operation count depends on the activation approximation and is not an instruction count for a specific kernel. The low intensity makes HBM traffic a candidate limit, while launch cost, nonlinear-function execution, and memory-access efficiency determine how closely the kernel approaches that bound.

Autoregressive large language model (LLM) decoding at batch size one is the kernel-level form of the whole-step decode placement in Roofline model. One dense matrix-vector product against a weight matrix of hidden dimension 4096 gives approximately 1 FLOP/byte, and a decode step streams many such matrices before cache reads, communication, and dispatch add time. The estimate covers the weight-streaming component, not every operation in a decode step. Recommendation serving in the style of Archetype B (DLRM at Scale) has a different low-intensity component: embedding lookups gather rows from large tables and perform little arithmetic on them. Memory-access latency, bandwidth, and any remote communication then matter more than Tensor Core peak throughput for that component.

↩︎ Archetype B’s sharded embedding tables and their lookup-dominated serving path are established in Archetype B (DLRM at Scale).

Attention’s intensity depends on phase, implementation, and the traffic boundary. Counting the full score matrix, each head performs \(4\SequenceLength^2 \AttentionHeadDimension\) FLOPs for its two matrix products. For the Llama 2 70B head dimension \(\AttentionHeadDimension =\) 128 and a sequence of \(\SequenceLength=\) 2,048 tokens, the unfused accounting writes and rereads the \(\SequenceLength{\times}\SequenceLength\) score and probability matrices in FP16, \(4\SequenceLength^2\) two-byte transfers beside the four \(\SequenceLength{\times}\AttentionHeadDimension\) operand tensors. Dividing \(4\SequenceLength^2\AttentionHeadDimension\) FLOPs by \(2(4\SequenceLength^2 + 4\SequenceLength\AttentionHeadDimension)\) bytes gives about 60 FLOP/byte, and the quadratic traffic caps the ratio near \(\AttentionHeadDimension/2\) however long the sequence grows. An ideal tiled accounting retains scores on chip and counts one read each of \(\AttentionQueryMatrix\), \(\AttentionKeyMatrix\), \(\AttentionValuesMatrix\), and one output write, four \(\SequenceLength{\times}\AttentionHeadDimension\) tensors of 2-byte values. The ratio is \(4\SequenceLength^2\AttentionHeadDimension / (4 \times 2\SequenceLength\AttentionHeadDimension) = \SequenceLength/2\), about 1,024 FLOP/byte. Finite SRAM and the tile schedule can require additional reads, so this value is an upper bound on intensity under that accounting rather than a prediction for every tiled kernel. For decode, counting one full KV-cache read per query head gives approximately 1 FLOP/byte. Grouped-query attention (GQA), which Llama 2 70B uses, lets 8 query heads share each cached key and value head, so one read of the cache can serve the whole group. If the implementation realizes that reuse, the corresponding estimate rises to about 8 FLOP/byte, still below the ridge.

Under these traffic assumptions, only batched GEMM and tiled prefill attention expose enough reuse to approach the compute-bound regime; every other example has a bandwidth ceiling below peak arithmetic throughput (table 4). That placement identifies where reducing bytes may help; profiling determines whether memory traffic is the exposed cost.

The operations this chapter optimizes fall on both sides of the H100’s FP16 ridge (figure 2). Batch-one decode and unfused attention prefill sit on the HBM bandwidth slope, while tiled prefill and large GEMM operations reach the Tensor Core ceiling. Each position identifies the tighter ideal bound, not measured performance at that bound; a kernel that runs below the envelope may first need better scheduling, locality, or parallelism before either peak resource rate becomes limiting.

The workload comparison explains why peak FLOP/s alone cannot predict small-batch decode performance. When weight streaming dominates, memory bandwidth limits token generation, while memory capacity constrains the requests that can be batched. Larger batches can reuse weights across requests and move the workload toward the compute ceiling; the serving regime determines how far that shift remains useful.

9.5 Serving Regimes, Batch Size, and Prefill-Decode

The weight-streaming estimate from workload placement is the source of the chapter’s opening figure. Decoding LLM requests one at a time performs about one operation per byte of weights streamed, which leaves nearly all of an H100’s FP16 compute idle during each decode step. Batching requests amortizes those reads across many tokens, but combining prompt processing and token generation without accounting for their different costs can increase latency. The serving regime, meaning how a system manages the batch dimension and divides prompt processing from token generation, shapes both weight reuse and interference between requests. These two features establish the workload conditions under which subsequent optimization techniques must be evaluated.

9.5.1 Batch size as the universal control knob

For memory-bound LLM serving, batch size is often the first performance lever to test, and also one of the most constrained. Increasing the batch size raises arithmetic intensity where requests reuse the same weights. For the weight-dominated portion of an LLM decode step, the following estimate is approximately linear in batch size:

\[ \DecodeArithmeticIntensity(\BatchSize) = \frac{2\Parameters \BatchSize}{\Parameters\ElementStorageSize + \BatchSize \HiddenDimension\, \ElementStorageSize} \tag{2}\]

Here, \(\Parameters\) is parameter count, \(\BatchSize\) is batch size, \(\HiddenDimension\) is hidden width, and \(\ElementStorageSize\) is bytes per element, assuming weights and activations share one precision. The numerator counts the whole model’s arithmetic, while the activation term counts only one matrix’s input vectors; even summed over every weight matrix, the activations stay far smaller than the \(\Parameters\ElementStorageSize\) weight bytes at the batch sizes considered here, so the shortcut does not change the estimate. This simplified weight-reuse estimate excludes KV-cache traffic and inter-device communication; it describes why batching helps before those other costs become binding. At batch size 1, the denominator is dominated by the weight term \((\Parameters\ElementStorageSize)\), and \(\DecodeArithmeticIntensity\approx 2/\ElementStorageSize \approx 1\) FLOP/byte for FP16. At batch size 256, the weight term still dominates, but the same weight bytes are amortized across more requests, so \(\DecodeArithmeticIntensity\approx 2 \times 256/\ElementStorageSize \approx 256\) FLOP/byte, approaching the compute-bound regime.

Larger batches raise arithmetic intensity toward the ridge.

Batching moves weight-dominated execution toward the compute ceiling until cache traffic and memory capacity stop it. End-to-end serving reference values put model FLOPs utilization (MFU) at about 5 percent at batch size 1, against an illustrative large-batch value of about 40 percent. Both include prefill work, which is why the batch-one value sits above the pure decode bound that notebook 9.1 computes later in this section. With hardware, price, and useful work per token held fixed, that utilization ratio is a 8× reduction in cost per token, which is the economic reason serving systems are designed around batch size. A deployed service still has to measure its own prompt/decode mix, completed throughput, and latency together before planning capacity on that ratio.

The same economics can be priced on the recurring deployment, for the latency-oriented and throughput-oriented operating points of section 9.3, once the KV cache that the weight-reuse estimate omitted is added back. Take the FP16 weights of the 70B model, sharded across eight GPUs, and let every request hold a full 4,096-token KV cache. At batch one, each GPU streams about 17.7 GB per decode step, so a step takes at least 5.3 ms and the replica emits at most 190 tokens/s. At a batch of 32 requests, each GPU streams 22.9 GB, giving a step-time lower bound of 6.8 ms and an aggregate output upper bound of 4,690 tokens/s. At $2.50 per GPU-hour, the corresponding cost lower bounds per million output tokens are $29 and $1.19. Their ratio is roughly 25×, while the step-time bounds differ by only 1.3×. The batched point buys a large cost reduction for a smaller latency penalty, and that asymmetry is the shape of the frontier trade-off.

This bandwidth-only cost ratio is larger than the 8× of the end-to-end MFU anchors above, mainly because the two comparisons count different work. Both bandwidth bounds count decode alone and assume every step streams at peak bandwidth with no launch, communication, or queueing delay, whereas the anchors’ batch-one value already includes compute-dense prefill, which raises its utilization and so narrows the ratio. Realized ratios come from completed throughput and per-request latency recorded together at a stated offered load. One accounting rule matters when converting those measurements to cost: a tensor-parallel replica produces one shared output stream per request, so its aggregate token rate must not be counted once for every participating GPU.

Memory limits that reuse: active requests need KV cache for prompt and generated tokens, alongside model weights and runtime state. On the recurring deployment, 140 GB of FP16 weights exceed one H100’s memory; sharding across the node leaves a 17.5 GB weight shard per GPU. Remaining capacity must cover cache, workspace, and runtime overhead. INT4 reduces the weight payload to a quarter of its FP16 size before metadata, and section 9.9.3 later prices how that saving and a compressed cache change batch capacity.

A serving scheduler can add new requests as others finish, provided demand and available memory permit admission. Local performance engineering supplies the capacity and execution-time measurements that make those decisions feasible. Weight quantization reduces shared model storage, while cache compression and allocation efficiency reduce the capacity needed for active requests. Continuous batching for large language model (LLM) inference develops the scheduler; the local point here is that memory savings can expand its feasible batch range without guaranteeing that the largest batch meets latency targets.

PagedAttention (Kwon et al. 2023) reduces allocation waste by storing KV cache in fixed-size blocks allocated on demand, rather than reserving contiguous space for the longest sequence each request might reach. Reserving 4,096 tokens for requests that average 500 leaves much of that reserved capacity unused. Block allocation lets other requests use capacity that would otherwise remain reserved, although partially filled blocks and runtime headroom still consume memory. The benefit is a larger feasible active-request set when allocation waste is the limiting factor.

Kwon, Woosuk, Zhuohan Li, Siyuan Zhuang, Ying Sheng, Lianmin Zheng, Cody Hao Yu, Joseph Gonzalez, Hao Zhang, and Ion Stoica. 2023. “Efficient Memory Management for Large Language Model Serving with PagedAttention.” Proceedings of the 29th Symposium on Operating Systems Principles, 611–26. https://doi.org/10.1145/3600006.3613165.

↪ The PagedAttention allocator, its block table, and the KV-cache capacity it recovers from fragmentation are developed in PagedAttention.

PagedAttention and KV cache quantization address complementary sources of memory pressure. PagedAttention reduces memory waste from fragmentation, while quantization reduces memory usage from precision. Their joint batch-size gain depends on the model-weight footprint, request-length distribution, and scheduler headroom, so the two savings do not simply multiply.

9.5.2 The prefill-decode decomposition

Modern LLM serving systems decompose each request into two distinct phases with fundamentally different performance characteristics. The distinction between these phases drives system architecture and optimization strategy.

The prefill phase processes the entire input prompt in parallel. If the prompt contains \(\SequenceLength\) tokens, the prefill phase executes a single forward pass over all \(\SequenceLength\) tokens simultaneously. The GEMM operations have shape \([\SequenceLength,\HiddenDimension]{\times}[\HiddenDimension,\HiddenDimension]\), making the batch dimension equal to \(\SequenceLength\). For a prompt of 1024 tokens, this is arithmetically intensive: the arithmetic intensity is approximately \(2 \times 1024/2 = 1024\) FLOP/byte for FP16 weights, well into the compute-bound regime. This weight-reuse estimate places the example GEMM above the FP16 ridge point; it omits activation and output traffic. Short prompts, kernel shapes, and other operators can produce different limits, so prefill as a whole still requires profiling.

The decode phase generates output tokens one at a time, autoregressively. Each step has a batch dimension of 1 for a single request or the number of requests scheduled together for batched serving. The weight-streaming analysis in section 9.4.2 explains why dense batch-one decode often has a bandwidth limit, while cache traffic, launches, and communication can add further exposed costs.

The prefill-decode decomposition connects serving design to the two operational regimes mapped in figure 2. Long-prompt prefill GEMMs can have enough arithmetic intensity to place their roofline bound on the compute ceiling, while weight-dominated, low-batch decode usually has a bandwidth ceiling; prompt length, batch size, and kernel implementation determine how closely execution approaches each bound. A real serving system must handle both phases, often simultaneously across different active requests, so their competition for accelerator resources also matters.

Disaggregated serving addresses this interference by running prefill and decode on separate hardware pools. The roofline placement tells each pool what to buy. The prefill pool wants arithmetic throughput for its compute-bound GEMMs, while the decode pool wants memory bandwidth and capacity for weight streaming and KV cache, which also makes it the natural home for aggressive weight quantization. Each pool can then choose its own batch size and parallelism strategy for its latency target. The KV cache computed during prefill is transferred to a decode server, which handles the subsequent autoregressive generation. Separation therefore introduces a communication cost as it removes competition between the phases. Its benefit depends on whether the reduced interference and independent tuning outweigh cache-transfer time and waiting for capacity in either pool.

↪ Disaggregated prefill and decode pools, joined by a KV-cache transfer on the critical path, are developed in Prefill-decode disaggregation.

The performance characteristics of each phase guide the choice of optimization. FlashAttention reduces attention’s memory traffic mainly during prefill, because only prefill forms the full \(\SequenceLength{\times}\SequenceLength\) score matrix that a tiled kernel avoids writing to HBM; a decode step has a single query row and no quadratic intermediate. Decode attention benefits from a different fused kernel, one that reads each cached key and value once and computes scores, softmax, and the weighted sum in a single pass rather than in separate kernels with their own launches and intermediate writes. Either kernel’s effect on total latency still depends on how much of the forward pass attention consumes. Speculative decoding targets the decode phase. KV cache quantization reduces decode storage and read traffic, while its encoding cost begins when prefill creates the cache. Precision engineering can help both phases through different mechanisms. Supported FP8 kernels offer a higher compute ceiling for compute-bound prefill, while FP8 or INT4 weights reduce the bytes streamed during weight-dominated decode. The resulting speedup depends on format support, conversion overhead, and the costs left unchanged by the precision reduction; the model must also retain the required output quality.

For the recurring 70B replica, the decode phase’s distance below the ridge can be priced in two steps: its arithmetic intensity, then the token rate that HBM bandwidth alone permits.

Napkin Math 9.1: The Roofline diagnostic
Problem: A 70B parameter LLM is deployed on 8\(\times\) H100 GPUs with tensor parallelism. At batch size 1, each GPU holds approximately 8.75B parameters in FP16 (17.5 GB of weights). Each decode step reads its weight shard to produce one token. What is the achieved arithmetic intensity, and what is the theoretical maximum token generation rate?

Math:

Step 1: Arithmetic intensity. Each decode step per GPU: FLOPs \(=\) \(2 \times 8.75 \times 10^{9}\) \(=\) 17.5B FLOPs. Bytes loaded \(=\) \(8.75 \times 10^{9} \times 2 \text{ bytes}\) \(=\) 17.5 GB.

\(\ArithmeticIntensity = \frac{1.75 \times 10^{10} \text{ FLOP}}{1.75 \times 10^{10} \text{ bytes}} = 1 \text{ FLOP/byte}\)

At 1 FLOP/byte, the operation sits far below the H100 ridge point of ~295 FLOP/byte: deeply memory-bound.

Step 2: Token rate. Since the operation is memory-bound, performance is limited by bandwidth, not compute:

\(\WeightStreamingDecodeTime = \frac{17.5\,\text{GB}}{3.35\,\text{TB/s}} \approx 5.2\,\text{ms per token}\)

Because all eight shards stream their weights in parallel and together emit one token per step, the bandwidth-only ceiling is approximately 191.4 tokens/s for the whole replica, not per GPU. In practice, overheads from KV cache reads and NVLink synchronization reduce this substantially below the ideal bandwidth-only limit.

Systems insight: At batch size 1, the bandwidth-only calculation reaches about 0.3 percent of the H100’s FP16 peak FLOP/s, leaving idle the more than 99 percent of compute that opened the chapter. This is the decode-only bound; the end-to-end batch-one anchor of about 5 percent MFU sits higher because it also counts compute-dense prefill. Larger batches amortize weight reads across requests, quantization reduces the weight payload, and speculative decoding seeks multiple accepted tokens per target-model pass. Each changes the weight-streaming budget, with additional costs to evaluate before claiming a speedup.

The decode calculation identifies weight streaming as a limit, while the prefill analysis exposes a different opportunity in attention intermediates. These are distinct sources of HBM traffic, and which one dominates depends on the deployed service’s prompt and output lengths. Profiling must locate the exposed cost and establish a reproducible baseline before an optimization can be selected.

Self-Check: Question

  1. In modern Large Language Model (LLM) serving systems, why do the prompt prefill phase and the token decode phase exhibit fundamentally different performance bottlenecks on GPU hardware?

    1. Prefill generates tokens sequentially one by one, making it memory-bound by weight reads, whereas decode processes all prompt tokens in parallel, making it compute-bound by Tensor Cores
    2. Prefill relies exclusively on integer arithmetic units, whereas decode requires floating-point matrix multiplication units
    3. Long-prompt prefill GEMMs reuse weights across prompt tokens and can have a compute ceiling, whereas weight-dominated single-request decode has low intensity and a bandwidth ceiling
    4. Prefill requires continuous network AllToAll collective exchanges between GPUs, whereas decode requires no inter-GPU communication

  2. Explain how PagedAttention reduces KV-cache allocation waste compared with contiguous preallocation. What measurements determine whether the recovered capacity improves serving throughput?

  3. The architectural pattern that decouples LLM inference by executing the compute-bound prompt prefill phase and the bandwidth-bound autoregressive token decode phase on separate, specialized hardware pools is known as ____ serving.

  4. A 70B parameter LLM (140 GB FP16 weights) is served across an 8-GPU NVIDIA H100 node with tensor parallelism (TP=8). Each GPU holds 17.5 GB of weights and provides 3.35 TB/s of HBM bandwidth and 989 TFLOP/s peak FP16 compute. At batch size 1, what is the theoretical bandwidth-limited decode step time per token, and why is achieved compute utilization below 0.4%?

    1. Decode takes approximately 120 ms per token because NVLink latency serializes the 8 GPUs, consuming 99.6% of time in network barriers
    2. Decode takes approximately 0.5 ms per token because the 8 GPUs execute in parallel at peak FP16 compute throughput
    3. Decode takes approximately 24.0 ms per token because the GPU must reread the entire 140 GB model across the PCI-e bus on each step
    4. Decode takes approximately 5.2 ms per token (\(17.5\text{ GB} / 3.35\text{ TB/s}\)), and compute utilization is below 0.4% because the arithmetic intensity is only 1.0 FLOP/byte compared to the H100 ridge point of ~295 FLOP/byte

See Answers →

9.6 System Profiling

Suppose the recurring 70B replica misses its inter-token latency objective. Before any kernel is rewritten, the engineer needs to know which interval of the decode step is exposed: weight streaming, attention, element-wise work, collectives, or host dispatch. A kernel made several times faster changes nothing if the interval it shortens was small or already hidden behind other work. System profiling answers that question by showing where execution waits and which intervals an intervention could shorten. The candidate remedies mapped across the branches of figure 1 are hypotheses: fusion removes intermediate transfers, graph replay reduces repeated host submission, and precision reduction moves fewer bytes. Their detailed mechanisms follow the measurement method; selecting one first requires evidence that its target cost is exposed.

Systems Perspective 9.1: The physics of profiling (the Heisenberg effect)
Instrumentation adds work to a distributed ML system. Gathering high-resolution telemetry, such as tracking every CUDA kernel launch or memory allocation, consumes host cycles and memory and can introduce additional transfers or synchronization. In heavily optimized serving pipelines, profiling can induce the very latency spikes the engineer is trying to diagnose. This observer effect favors low-overhead telemetry for continuous monitoring and short, targeted traces for diagnosis. Detailed tracing in production requires a bounded collection window and an overhead budget; isolated runs are useful when they reproduce the relevant workload and contention.

The cost must be measured for the selected workload and collection settings. The PyTorch profiler and Nsight Systems can both perturb execution, and neither has a fixed overhead independent of the events collected; richer options, such as recording tensor shapes or allocation history, perturb more. An engineer should record the enabled options, compare instrumented and uninstrumented runs, and use traces to explain execution structure while measuring production latency with instrumentation overhead controlled.

9.6.1 The profiling hierarchy

Profiling levels are useful only if they guide the drill-down from symptom to intervention. ML system profiling partitions observability across four tiers, trading observational breadth for diagnostic detail. Figure 3 structures this descent from top-level application symptoms down to hardware root causes, tracing how diagnostic scope narrows from fleet-wide metrics over hours to streaming multiprocessor counters over microseconds.

The drill-down starts at the application level, where end-to-end metrics such as tokens per second, time-to-first-token, p99 latency, and GPU utilization reveal whether the user-facing system meets its target. Framework profiling then associates time with model operations and exposes scheduling, data-transfer, and collective-communication dependencies. Kernel profiling narrows the diagnosis to execution intervals, launch gaps, and overlap opportunities. Hardware counters complete the chain: tools such as NVIDIA Nsight Compute report achieved memory bandwidth, occupancy, and instruction mix for a selected kernel. Distributed scope cuts across these levels. Application metrics describe the fleet outcome, while synchronized traces across ranks identify imbalance before an engineer drills into one rank’s kernels.

For example, an application trace may reveal missed token-latency targets; framework traces across ranks may then expose a lagging collective. Kernel timelines distinguish late arrival from slow communication, and hardware counters test whether the preceding computation suffered from memory stalls or register pressure. Tensor Core use is also a measurement question: matrix alignment can affect kernel efficiency, but a misaligned shape does not universally force a fallback to scalar CUDA Core instructions. Library version, datatype, hardware, and kernel selection determine the executed path. Inspecting the instruction mix is more reliable than inferring that path from tensor dimensions alone.

9.6.2 Key performance metrics

Metrics are not interchangeable; each answers a different bottleneck question (table 5). Model bandwidth utilization (MBU), achieved memory bandwidth divided by peak bandwidth, is the decode counterpart of MFU. Together they diagnose useful hardware work, TTFT and ITL diagnose serving latency, and throughput exposes capacity under batching. Reading the wrong one hides the bottleneck: a decode path at a few percent MFU can be running its memory system near saturation.

9.6.3 Using the roofline for diagnosis

The metric choice in table 5 reflects the fundamental bottleneck shift between training and inference. MFU is usually the right training-efficiency lens, while MBU is often the right inference-efficiency lens when decode is limited by HBM traffic. The roofline model from section 9.4.1 becomes a diagnostic tool when combined with profiling data. Diagnosis follows three steps:

  1. Measure the achieved FLOP/s and memory bandwidth for a kernel using Nsight Compute.
  2. Compute measured arithmetic intensity from executed FLOPs and observed bytes at the same memory boundary used for the bandwidth ceiling (FLOP/byte). Compare it with the algorithmic estimate to expose extra traffic.
  3. Plot the measured performance against the roofline ceiling.

The plotted position then sorts the kernel into one of three cases, provided the ceilings match its datatype and operations. A kernel far below both ceilings has an execution problem rather than a resource limit, because dependencies, instruction mix, memory-access latency, or too little parallel work keep it from either peak. A kernel near its bandwidth ceiling gains little from faster arithmetic; only moving fewer bytes, through fusion or lower precision, moves it. A kernel near its compute ceiling can be helped only by doing less arithmetic or by a faster supported execution format or device. The roofline narrows the search, while the trace and counters establish which cost an intervention can actually remove.

Consider an illustrative profiling result: a LayerNorm kernel on an H100 reports 5 TFLOP/s of achieved compute and 2.8 TB/s of achieved memory bandwidth. Its arithmetic intensity is 5 TFLOP/s / 2.8 TB/s \(\approx\) 1.8 FLOP/byte. The H100’s ridge point is approximately 295 FLOP/byte at FP16. Since 1.8 FLOP/byte is far below 295 FLOP/byte, the roofline places the kernel’s bound on the bandwidth slope. Its achieved 2.8 TB/s (83.6 percent of the H100’s 3.35 TB/s peak bandwidth) shows that the kernel is already close to the bound the roofline assigns it. Faster arithmetic cannot help, and even a perfect memory schedule could recover only the small gap between that utilization and peak bandwidth. The material gains come from moving fewer bytes: fusion can eliminate an intermediate HBM round-trip, and a supported lower-precision representation can reduce bytes per element, provided either change preserves the required numerical behavior.

9.6.4 PyTorch profiler workflow

The PyTorch Profiler is most useful when application metrics show a slowdown but the responsible layer is still unknown. Its role is to narrow a global symptom to a layer, kernel family, or synchronization point before a heavier tool is used. It wraps an iteration loop to capture detailed traces with minimal code modification. Here the loop is the replica’s decode loop, one forward pass per step over the active batch; a training loop is profiled with the same schedule:

import torch
from torch.profiler import (
    profile,
    schedule,
    tensorboard_trace_handler,
)

# Profile 2 warmup steps + 3 active steps
with profile(
    activities=[
        torch.profiler.ProfilerActivity.CPU,
        torch.profiler.ProfilerActivity.CUDA,
    ],
    schedule=schedule(wait=1, warmup=2, active=3, repeat=1),
    on_trace_ready=tensorboard_trace_handler("./profiler_logs"),
    record_shapes=True,
    profile_memory=True,
    with_stack=True,
) as prof:
    for step in range(6):  # 1 wait + 2 warmup + 3 active
        with torch.inference_mode():
            # One decode step: a forward pass over the active
            # batch that reads and extends each request's KV cache
            next_tokens = decode_step(model, active_batch, kv_cache)
        prof.step()

The schedule parameter defines a warmup period during which the profiler collects events but discards the results, reducing the influence of profiler startup on the retained trace. Application warmup is a separate requirement: just-in-time (JIT) compilation, memory allocation, and CUDA context initialization must settle before a steady-state measurement. A fixed number of profiler warmup steps does not guarantee that this has happened. Check iteration times and compilation activity before choosing the measurement window; include startup costs separately when they matter to the deployment.

The resulting trace, viewable in TensorBoard or Chrome’s trace viewer, matters only insofar as each view maps to an intervention, and table 6 pairs each view with the experiment it suggests.

9.6.5 Timeline analysis with Nsight Systems

NVIDIA Nsight Systems narrows a profiler’s layer-level finding to the CPU, GPU, and communication timeline. A short trace of training or inference iterations reveals whether host submission leaves launch gaps, whether communication serializes execution, and which kernels warrant deeper inspection:

nsys profile --trace=cuda,nvtx,osrt,cudnn,cublas \
    --output=llm_profile \
    --force-overwrite=true \
    python3 inference_server.py --num-steps=5

The --trace flags control which activities are recorded. The cuda flag captures kernel launches and memory operations. The nvtx flag captures NVIDIA Tools Extension (NVTX) ranges emitted by application instrumentation or an enabled framework profiling context; it does not itself add module annotations. The cublas and cudnn flags capture library-level operations, which helps identify whether a GEMM is using cuBLAS or a custom kernel. The command is useful because these rows expose whether the bottleneck is launch overhead, communication serialization, or kernel implementation.

The resulting .nsys-rep file is opened in the Nsight Systems graphical user interface, which presents a multi-row timeline. Four rows carry the most diagnostic weight.

The CUDA HW row shows the actual kernel execution on the GPU. Each colored bar represents a kernel, with width proportional to execution time. A gap without kernel activity is a reason to inspect the surrounding transfers, host work, dependencies, and request arrivals. It represents a recoverable delay only if useful work could have run during that interval. For a saturated inference pipeline, unexplained gaps can expose an optimization opportunity; under light load, waiting for the next request may be expected.

The CUDA API row shows CPU-side CUDA API calls (kernel launches, memory allocations, synchronization). A long API interval may reflect launch overhead, a blocking synchronization, allocation, or waiting on device work. Identify the API and correlate its interval with GPU activity before diagnosing a CPU launch bottleneck. Graph replay helps when repeated host submission creates exposed launch gaps.

The NCCL row (for distributed workloads) shows collective communication operations. Comparing the NCCL row with compute-kernel intervals reveals whether communication overlaps with useful computation. Concurrent bars establish overlap in time, but not a net speedup: communication can contend with compute for device resources. Compare end-to-end duration with the baseline to establish whether concurrency actually removed exposed time.

The NVTX row shows the ranges emitted by the selected instrumentation. Explicit module-level annotations can associate intervals with names such as Linear, LayerNorm, or Attention. This connects low-level kernel names, cryptic strings that encode the target architecture, data types, and tile shape (an sm90_xmma_gemm_... prefix marks a Hopper GEMM, for example), to the model-level operations that produced them.

Five patterns reveal the most diagnostic information from an Nsight Systems trace. Table 7 maps each visual pattern to the bottleneck question it answers.

9.6.6 Common bottleneck patterns

Recurring trace patterns provide hypotheses that profiling can test. Table 8 connects each visual signature to a likely cause and a candidate intervention. Before applying the intervention, verify that the suspected cost lies on the execution path that determines the missed target; similar-looking traces can arise from different dependencies.

A full decode trace shows how several of these smaller bottlenecks can combine into the dominant loss. The example uses a 7B model on one GPU rather than the recurring 70B replica, so that no collective enters the trace and every interval belongs to a single device.

Example 9.1: The profiler detective
Scenario: A 7B parameter LLM running autoregressive generation on an H100 GPU at batch size 1 achieves 84 tokens/s, falling below the 239 tokens/s memory-bandwidth decode ceiling.

Diagnosis: Nsight Compute shows the primary FP16 GEMM operates efficiently (2.8 TB/s bandwidth), but Nsight Systems reveals that 58 percent of step time is spent in unfused LayerNorm/GELU kernels, sampling, and CPU launch gaps across 120 launches per layer. Some of this interval performs required model work; the trace must distinguish that work from avoidable launches and intermediate transfers.

Systems lesson: Systematic profiling reveals bottlenecks where intuition fails. Optimizing GEMM kernels yields diminishing returns when accumulated launch overhead and unfused element-wise operations consume over half the step duration; kernel fusion via torch.compile or Triton is a candidate for reducing the exposed launches and transfers. The end-to-end measurement must establish how much time it saves, since fusion does not eliminate all non-GEMM work or guarantee attainment of the decode roofline.

9.6.7 The profiling feedback loop

Effective performance engineering follows an iterative cycle: profile, diagnose, optimize, verify. Engineers frequently neglect the verification step. After applying an optimization, reprofiling confirms whether the intervention relieved the active limiter or merely exposed a secondary bottleneck—the classic waterbed dilemma of computer systems.

A common trap is optimizing based on microbenchmarks rather than end-to-end traces. A kernel that achieves a 2\(\times\) speedup in isolation may yield only a 5 percent gain in end-to-end throughput if its interval was not on the critical path, or if data dependencies serialize execution. Rigorous evaluation demands measuring impact at the application boundary (tokens per second, step time, tail latency percentiles) alongside kernel-level metrics. The same measurement discipline governs rollout risk, because a change that looks local can consume a global resource once it reaches production traffic. In ML serving, the analogous risk is an input shape the tests never exercised, such as an unusually long prompt whose prefill occupies a replica’s compute and cache far longer than the typical request. A well-documented case of an execution cost that escaped this verification step and became a fleet-wide failure comes from outside ML.

War Story 9.1: The regex that saturated the edge (2019)
Context: Cloudflare’s web application firewall evaluates rules on HTTP/HTTPS traffic across its global edge (Cloudflare 2019).

Mechanism: A rule deployed on July 2, 2019 caused excessive regular-expression backtracking and drove the CPUs serving HTTP/HTTPS traffic to nearly 100 percent utilization across the network. A previously removed CPU-use protection, missing performance tests, and a global rollout allowed the local computational cost to become a fleet-wide failure.

Impact: The resulting 27-minute outage disrupted proxying, content delivery, and firewall service. Traffic through the network dropped by about 82 percent, and users received 502 errors.

Fix: Disabling the firewall globally restored traffic; the offending change was then removed and the firewall re-enabled after testing. The postmortem reported restoring CPU-use protection and plans for performance tests, staged rule deployment, and a regular-expression engine with runtime guarantees.

Systems lesson: Correct outputs do not establish acceptable execution cost. For ML serving, the analogous discipline is to test expensive input shapes, bound admitted work, and stage performance-sensitive changes so a regression can be detected before it reaches the fleet.

Cloudflare. 2019. Details of the Cloudflare Outage on July 2, 2019. Cloudflare incident report.

Profiling turns optimization from guesswork into a discipline guided by quantitative evidence. A trace from one device, however, cannot show how a fleet behaves.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A production LLM serving decode step on an NVIDIA H100 reports a Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) of only 2.5%, but a Model Bandwidth Utilization (MBU) of 86%. How should the performance engineer interpret these diagnostic metrics?

    1. The GPU hardware is malfunctioning and must be power-cycled to clear thermal throttling
    2. The measurements are consistent with a bandwidth-limited path; low MFU alone is not evidence of poor implementation when MBU is high
    3. The low MFU indicates severe kernel launch latency on the CPU dispatcher that requires rewriting into CUDA Graphs
    4. The high MBU indicates excessive gradient checkpointing overhead that is saturating the PCIe bus

  2. Using the application-to-hardware profiling hierarchy, describe the drill-down an engineer should use to diagnose an LLM serving pipeline that misses its p99 latency objective. Explain where synchronized traces across ranks enter the investigation.

  3. If profiling confirms that a matrix operation uses scalar GPU arithmetic rather than Tensor Core instructions, the scalar units are called ____ Cores; tensor dimensions alone do not establish that execution path.

  4. Why can enabling shape recording in a high-fidelity PyTorch profiler perturb tensor lifetimes and increase memory demand in a workload that runs stably without profiling?

    1. The profiler increases GPU clock frequency beyond thermal limits, triggering emergency memory shutdowns
    2. The profiler converts all FP16 tensors to FP64 double-precision matrices during recording
    3. Shape recording can retain tensor references, inhibit reference-count-dependent optimizations, and introduce extra copies
    4. The profiler overwrites the CUDA driver’s virtual memory page tables with TensorBoard logs

  5. True or False: In an NVIDIA Nsight Systems trace, observing that the CUDA API row on the CPU thread displays wide bars significantly larger than the corresponding CUDA HW kernel bars indicates that the GPU kernels are executing too slowly.
