9.7 Measurement at Scale

When scaling from 8 GPUs to 1,024 GPUs, new sources of overhead emerge that are invisible in local traces. Fleet measurements determine whether optimizing a local kernel is warranted in the first place. A profiler can explain why one kernel stalls; it cannot by itself say whether thousands of accelerators are converting power, memory bandwidth, and network time into useful model progress. Several examples in this section are training jobs rather than serving replicas, for a deliberate reason: every synchronous training step waits for all ranks, so fleet-level loss shows up there directly and at the largest scale. The same accounting applies to the collectives inside the recurring tensor-parallel serving replica, only across eight devices instead of thousands.

Collecting detailed traces from every GPU makes the measurement itself a scaling concern. At a fixed event rate and collection configuration, trace volume grows with the number of GPUs. If a 5-second Nsight Systems trace for one GPU occupies 500 MB, the same window across 1,024 GPUs would produce about 512 GB. This illustrative volume motivates selecting the ranks and intervals needed to answer a diagnostic question rather than collecting every event continuously.

Production systems address this through hierarchical profiling. At the top level, application metrics such as MFU, throughput, and step time detect degradation, while per-rank timing and device telemetry help locate it. Their collection frequency must keep monitoring overhead within budget. Targeted profiling then captures a short window from ranks selected to test a hypothesis, including different pipeline stages or data-parallel groups when relevant. Comparing a suspected straggler with an otherwise similar rank is often more informative than tracing an arbitrary representative GPU.

Another approach is statistical profiling, where a sampling policy selects a small fraction of kernels for detailed timing. Aggregating samples across training steps can estimate the kernel-time distribution with less collection overhead than full tracing. The estimate depends on adequate coverage of ranks and execution phases; rare stalls can remain absent even from a large sample. The approach is analogous to the sampling profilers (like Linux perf) used in traditional systems engineering, adapted for the GPU context.

Intermittent stragglers challenge both short traces and sampled profiles: an accelerator typically runs at expected speed but periodically falls behind due to thermal throttling, hardware degradation, transient network congestion, or host runtime pauses such as memory reclamation and garbage collection. In synchronous execution, a step finishes only when its slowest rank does, so a rank running 10 percent slow during an episode can delay every rank in its communication group by up to that same 10 percent; the fleet-level throughput loss is that synchronization penalty weighted by episode frequency. Continuous per-rank execution timers reveal these tail latency outliers and trigger targeted tracing. Correlating slow intervals with accelerator hardware telemetry, network packet counters, and host runtime event logs then distinguishes the root straggler from the peer ranks that merely idle at collective barriers waiting for it.

Fleet measurement also needs a reproducible measurement contract, one that covers both the local execution boundary and the global efficiency metrics that capture computation, communication, and hardware variability. Without a shared contract, two measurements cannot support an optimization claim. Benchmark suites formalize this principle by fixing workload, quality, and measurement rules before comparing systems (Mattson et al. 2020; Reddi et al. 2019). The contract, whose fields table 9 pairs with the failure each one prevents, fixes the workload envelope, including model, input and output length distributions, batch or concurrency, data distribution, and quality criterion; records hardware topology, accelerator clocks and power limits, software versions, precision, and compiler settings; and states the timing and synchronization boundaries. Cold-start compilation and cache-filling trials must remain separate from warmed steady-state trials. Each reported point needs repeated trials with a dispersion statistic, an output-equivalence or quality check, and latency percentiles at a named offered load. Without those controls, a speedup can come from different work, different machine state, asynchronous timing, or a shifted quality or service-level objective (SLO) rather than from the proposed optimization.

Mattson, Peter, Christine Cheng, Cody Coleman, Greg Diamos, Paulius Micikevicius, David Patterson, Hanlin Tang, et al. 2020. “MLPerf Training Benchmark.” Proceedings of Machine Learning and Systems (MLSys) 2: 336–49.
Reddi, Vijay Janapa, Christine Cheng, David Kanter, Peter Mattson, Guenther Schmuelling, Carole-Jean Wu, Brian Anderson, et al. 2019. “MLPerf Inference Benchmark.” 2020 ACM/IEEE 47th Annual International Symposium on Computer Architecture (ISCA), 446–59. https://doi.org/10.1109/isca45697.2020.00045.

Systems Perspective 9.2: The load generator is part of the measurement
An open-loop load generator schedules requests independently of responses. A closed-loop load generator issues replacements only as earlier requests finish. The modes hold attempted arrivals and outstanding work fixed, respectively.

At 100 requests/s, a 1 s open-loop schedule contains 100 requests. At concurrency one, the closed loop records 1 slow completion but issues no replacements, omitting 99 waiting-time samples. This coordinated omission makes overload look less severe.

The correct mode reproduces the target workload. Reporting intended load, achieved arrivals, completed throughput, concurrency, latency, errors, timeouts, and duration together exposes any generator feedback that conceals the queue.

9.7.1 The fleet efficiency metric

Hardware utilization reports how often GPUs are busy, but it does not distinguish model arithmetic from recomputation or time spent waiting within communication operations. Fleet measurement therefore needs the useful-work metric that table 5 already listed, MFU, which counts only the “useful” FLOPs the model architecture requires. What matters at fleet scale is how that metric aggregates across devices and what it hides. Its comparisons across software stacks and parallelization strategies hold only when the workload, FLOP-counting convention, and hardware peak definition stay fixed.

Definition 9.2: MFU

MFU is the fraction of the hardware’s theoretical peak throughput (\(\PeakRate\)) consumed by FLOPs that directly advance model training or inference, excluding overhead from recomputation, padding, and synchronization.

  1. Significance: At fleet scale, MFU aggregates across all nodes. Communication overhead, load imbalance, and pipeline bubbles tend to lower it relative to a comparable local run, although changes in batch size or memory feasibility can alter that comparison. A one-percentage-point MFU increase across 10,000 identical GPUs adds useful throughput equivalent to 100 GPUs at peak. Realized cost savings depend on whether the recovered capacity displaces hardware or shortens paid runtime.
  2. Distinction: Unlike hardware utilization (which reports how often the accelerator is “busy”), MFU relates the model’s reference FLOP rate to hardware peak. It excludes recomputation and padding from useful work even when they support a feasible or faster implementation; quality and convergence require separate checks.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that high GPU utilization implies high efficiency. A system can show 90 percent hardware utilization while achieving 30 percent MFU when busy intervals include communication or arithmetic that runs far below peak. MFU exposes the gap but does not explain its cause; traces and other metrics must guide the optimization decision.

↩︎ Model FLOPs utilization is established as the compute-axis efficiency measure in Node compute substrate (\OperationalTaxonomyDimension_1).

Fleet MFU falls below the single-node baseline.

With MFU defined as useful work rather than raw busyness, the loss between a single node and the fleet becomes a number, here for the recurring 70B model in training.

Napkin Math 9.2: The scaling tax
Problem: A 70B parameter model achieves a local node baseline of 65 percent MFU on a single 8-GPU node. When training scales to 128 GPUs, the step time increases to 245 ms, dropping fleet performance to 48 percent MFU. The single-node baseline assumes optimizer state offloaded to host memory, since full training state for a 70B model exceeds one node’s HBM. What relative MFU loss appears at fleet scale under these constructed assumptions?

Math: Both MFU figures come from the same ratio, the useful FLOPs a step performs divided by the FLOPs the cluster could execute at peak in that step’s wall time:

\[\ModelFlopsUtilization = \frac{\UsefulModelFlops}{\DeviceCount\times \PeakRate \times \DistributedStepTime}\]

For the fleet case, a step performs 14.9 PFLOP of useful work, while 128 GPUs \(\times\) 989 TFLOP/s per GPU \(\times\) 245 ms of wall time could execute 31 PFLOP at peak; the quotient 14.9 PFLOP ÷ 31 PFLOP \(\approx\) 48 percent. The same substitution on the single node yields 65 percent, and the scaling tax is \(1 - \text{Fleet MFU}/\text{Local MFU}\), giving 26.2 percent.

Systems insight: The 26.2 percent scaling tax measures the relative efficiency change under these assumptions. It does not isolate network cost: workload shape, parallelism, offload, kernel efficiency, and synchronization can all change the ratio. A scaling regression therefore requires a matched measurement contract and trace evidence before attributing the loss to the network.

9.7.2 Detecting scaling regressions

Local resource costs can become exposed delays at scale. A small increase in memory demand may cross an allocation threshold, while a longer host pause on one rank can hold up a synchronized step across the fleet. Communication changes can separately increase network contention. These mechanisms require different evidence, so a local memory regression alone does not diagnose a network-buffer problem. Table 10 organizes the tests that connect local changes to distributed behavior before a full-fleet rollout.

The gray-failure row of table 10 applies the same straggler arithmetic, since a synchronous data-parallel step runs at the pace of its slowest node. The same measurements also explain why benchmark numbers cannot be copied directly into production capacity plans.

Systems Perspective 9.3: Benchmark vs. reality: The hero run tax
Benchmark results, including MLPerf submissions, describe performance under a specified workload and measurement protocol. A carefully tuned result is a useful reference, but its operating conditions may differ from those of a production service. Capacity planning requires comparing those conditions with the intended deployment rather than treating the reported throughput as an unconditional capacity guarantee.

Production capacity must account for costs that the reference measurement does not already include. Metrics collection, logging, checkpointing, and health monitoring can consume resources; contention and hardware variability can add further delays. Their combined effect must be measured without counting costs already present in the benchmark a second time. As a planning scenario, if useful throughput is 10–20 percent below the rate that implied 30 days of training, the same work takes approximately 33.3–37.5 days. This is a sensitivity calculation, not a universal production penalty.

The measurement contract makes optimization results comparable across the node and fleet boundaries. A trace dominated by intermediate transfers or repeated host launches motivates fusion and replay; dominant weight traffic motivates precision reduction. The following mechanisms each change one part of that measured budget, and their value must be checked against the same workload, quality criterion, and service objective.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A 70B parameter model achieves 65% MFU on an isolated 8-GPU node. When scaled across a 128-GPU cluster connected via InfiniBand, the training step time increases such that cluster-wide MFU drops to 48%. What is the Scaling Tax incurred by this distributed deployment?

    1. 17.0% Scaling Tax, calculated as the direct difference (\(65\% - 48\%\))
    2. 35.4% Scaling Tax, calculated as the ratio of unutilized compute (\(1 - 0.48 / 0.65\))
    3. 52.0% Scaling Tax, representing the unutilized fraction of the 128 GPUs
    4. Approximately 26.2% Scaling Tax (\(1 - \text{Fleet MFU} / \text{Local MFU} = 1 - 0.48 / 0.65\)), representing the relative efficiency loss compared with the single-node baseline

  2. In a synchronous data-parallel cluster of 1,000 GPUs, explain why one gray-failure node that delivers 10% less processing throughput can reduce the entire fleet’s useful throughput by roughly 10% when its work determines every step’s critical path.

  3. Synchronized clocks record the same collective in otherwise matched runs. In the baseline, every rank enters at 8 ms and all finish at 10 ms. In case A, all but one rank enter at 8 ms, the last enters at 12 ms, and all finish at 14 ms. In case B, every rank enters at 8 ms and all finish at 14 ms. What interval accounts for the extra completion time in each case, and what measurement and targeted experiment would help distinguish the causes?

  4. Which tiered testing strategy is specifically designed to catch nonlinear scaling collapses (such as exhausted InfiniBand credit buffers or garbage collection pauses) before deploying a code change to a 1,000-GPU cluster?

    1. Small-scale canaries on 8 and 64 GPUs to map the scaling efficiency curve and identify curve-bending regressions before full-fleet deployment
    2. Single-GPU unit tests running with Python debug assertions enabled
    3. Synthetic micro-benchmarks that test only CPU RAM bandwidth
    4. Static code linting to count the number of matrix multiplications in the model

See Answers →

9.8 Operator Fusion and Kernel Engineering

Consider the simple sequence of operations \(\FusionOutputTensor= \text{LayerNorm}(\text{GELU}(\FusionInputTensor\WeightMatrix+ \BiasVector))\). Separate kernels write and reread intermediate tensors through global memory, creating traffic that can dominate the element-wise and normalization work. Operator fusion combines compatible operations so that intermediate values remain in registers or shared memory. The opportunity is to remove transfers and launches; whether the entire sequence fits profitably into one kernel depends on its reduction dependencies and on-chip resource requirements.

Systems Perspective 9.4: Analogy: The short-order cook
Imagine a kitchen where one chef chops vegetables, puts them in the fridge (HBM), then another chef takes them out to boil them, puts them back in the fridge, and a third chef takes them out to plate them. This is unfused execution: the bottleneck is not the cooking but the constant walking to the fridge.

Operator fusion assigns the recipe to a single chef who keeps the ingredients on their cutting board (SRAM/Registers) and performs all three steps consecutively without ever returning to the fridge until the final dish is ready.

9.8.1 The kernel launch problem

Each GPU kernel launch involves overhead: the CPU must prepare launch parameters, dispatch to the GPU command queue, and the GPU must schedule thread blocks across its streaming multiprocessors (SMs). For a small element-wise operation on an accelerator such as an H100, this overhead runs to several microseconds per launch, a time during which a memory-bound kernel might have already completed its useful work. When a transformer layer comprises dozens of small operations (add, multiply, normalize, activate), the cumulative launch overhead becomes significant. The exposed share depends on how the framework dispatches. In eager execution a Python loop and the framework dispatcher submit each kernel in turn, but not every launch traverses the Python global interpreter lock (GIL), the lock that serializes Python bytecode execution; asynchronous runtime threads, compiled regions, and native extensions can submit work outside that path.

Each unfused kernel also materializes an output in global memory. For the sequence \(\FusionOutputTensor= \text{LayerNorm}(\text{GELU}(\FusionInputTensor\WeightMatrix+ \BiasVector))\), a simplified traffic model assumes these reads and writes reach HBM; cache reuse can reduce the actual off-chip traffic. The GEMM kernel reads \(\FusionInputTensor\) and \(\WeightMatrix\) from HBM, computes \(\FusionInputTensor\WeightMatrix+ \BiasVector\), and writes the intermediate result \(\FusionIntermediateTensor_1\) back to memory. The GELU kernel then reads \(\FusionIntermediateTensor_1\), evaluates \(\text{GELU}(\FusionIntermediateTensor_1)\), and writes a second intermediate tensor \(\FusionIntermediateTensor_2\). Finally, the LayerNorm kernel reads \(\FusionIntermediateTensor_2\), applies normalization, and writes the final output \(\FusionOutputTensor\).

Intermediate tensors \(\FusionIntermediateTensor_1\) and \(\FusionIntermediateTensor_2\) each occupy the same memory as the output \(\FusionOutputTensor\). For the Llama 2 70B shape (hidden dimension 8192) at a batch of 2048 tokens in FP16, each intermediate tensor is 33.6 MB. The unfused execution materializes 67.1 MB of intermediate tensors, corresponding to 134.2 MB of HBM traffic if each write and reread reaches that boundary. A fused kernel can avoid these transfers by keeping the needed tiles of \(\FusionIntermediateTensor_1\) and \(\FusionIntermediateTensor_2\) in registers or shared memory within the SM. Figure 4 illustrates this idealized contrast; it does not require the complete intermediate tensors to fit on chip at once.

By retaining intermediates within registers and shared memory, the fused pipeline in figure 4 eliminates four one-way transfers between the SM and HBM (the red write and read passes for \(\FusionIntermediateTensor_1\) and \(\FusionIntermediateTensor_2\)). Including one read each of \(\FusionInputTensor\) and \(\WeightMatrix\) and one write of \(\FusionOutputTensor\), the stated dimensions yield a 1.7× byte reduction, ignoring the small bias and normalization vectors. Cache reuse, tile reloads, and workspace can change actual traffic.

Launch overhead creates a second opportunity. Suppose an eager transformer decoder layer issues 30 separate kernels at 5–10 μs per launch. The host then spends 150–300 μs on dispatch. The same layer’s bandwidth-only GPU time in the batch-one 70B example is about 65 μs, making exposed submission time a plausible limiter. Host dispatch can overlap GPU execution, so these totals alone do not establish the length or placement of launch gaps. A launch-bound trace must show that submission leaves useful device work waiting. Fusion reduces the number of launches, while graph replay (section 9.8.3) reduces repeated submission overhead for the kernels that remain.

Fusion nevertheless has a resource ceiling. A larger fused kernel keeps more live values in registers and often reserves more shared memory per thread block. Either demand can reduce the number of resident warps, lowering occupancy and the latency-hiding capacity of the SM; excessive register demand can also spill values into local memory backed by the cache hierarchy or HBM. Fusion is profitable only when the avoided launches and HBM intermediates outweigh this loss of occupancy, spill traffic, and any duplicated computation. Kernel evaluation must therefore compare measured HBM bytes, register and shared-memory use, achieved occupancy, spill transactions, and end-to-end time rather than treating fewer launches as sufficient evidence.

9.8.2 Fusion categories

Fusion is profitable when the HBM traffic it removes is worth the kernel complexity it introduces. The three common categories differ by that trade-off: how much data movement they eliminate, how much synchronization they require, and how often a compiler can apply them automatically.

Element-wise fusion sits at the low-complexity end: consecutive element-wise operations (add, multiply, activation functions) combine into a single kernel. For pure operations with compatible iteration spaces, fusion can preserve the same per-element dependencies. Aliasing, side effects, multiple consumers, and numerical requirements can constrain legality or profitability. Deep learning frameworks commonly perform element-wise fusion automatically for supported patterns.

When the sequence includes a reduction, the fusion decision becomes more constrained. Reduction fusion combines an element-wise operation with a subsequent reduction (such as summing elements for a loss function, or computing mean and variance for layer normalization). Threads can use warp-level shuffle instructions or shared memory to combine partial results within a block. Reductions that span blocks may need additional synchronization or an intermediate buffer. When the reduction and its producer fit a compatible schedule, fusion can avoid writing and rereading the prenormalization tensor; that saving depends on the reduction shape and kernel design.

Operator-specific fusion uses custom kernels designed for a particular sequence of operations, such as fused attention or fused GEMM-bias-activation. The kernel architect must reason about data flow, shared memory allocation, and thread scheduling simultaneously. This specialization can remove an intermediate representation that dominates memory demand: FlashAttention, for example, avoids materializing the quadratic attention workspace and keeps the online softmax state linear in sequence length.

To estimate the opportunity, consider each category applied to a transformer layer with hidden dimension 8192, a batch of 2048 tokens, and FP16 values. Fusing a bias-GELU-dropout chain can eliminate two intermediate tensors of 33.6 MB each, avoiding 134.2 MB of traffic per layer under the same HBM accounting assumptions. Across 80 layers, the modeled saving is about 10.7 GB per forward pass. Reduction fusion targets prenormalization tensors and intermediate statistics when their dependencies permit it. Operator-specific attention fusion removes the quadratic score and probability matrices, making it especially valuable when those matrices dominate memory demand. These savings cannot simply be added when candidate fusions eliminate overlapping intermediates. The resulting end-to-end speedup must be measured because remaining traffic and kernel resource use can change the bottleneck.

9.8.3 CUDA graphs for launch overhead amortization

An orthogonal technique for reducing the overhead term in the iron law is CUDA graphs.2 While operator fusion combines multiple operations into fewer kernels, CUDA Graphs amortize repeated CPU submission of a stable kernel sequence.

2 CUDA graphs: Graph capture records a sequence of GPU work and its dependencies for later replay (NVIDIA Corporation 2026). Replay requires a capture-compatible operation graph and valid referenced storage; it does not require numerically identical results. Dynamic control flow, shapes, or allocations can instead require graph variants, graph updates, or uncaptured regions. Repetitive decode shapes are often easier to capture than heterogeneous prefill shapes.

NVIDIA Corporation. 2026. CUDA Graphs: CUDA Programming Guide.

In standard PyTorch execution, each kernel launch requires the CPU to push a command to the GPU’s command queue, and section 9.8.1 put that cost at 150–300 μs per decoder layer. For the 80-layer 70B example, host-side kernel submission alone totals roughly 12–24 ms per forward pass, and whatever part of it the GPU cannot hide behind running kernels lands on the critical path. That total is several times the 5.2 ms bandwidth-bound decode step computed in notebook 9.1, which is why eager decode, submitted one kernel at a time, can be launch-bound before it is bandwidth-bound.

CUDA Graphs address this by recording a sequence of GPU operations (kernel launches and memory copies) into a replayable graph. The recording happens once during a warmup phase. On subsequent iterations, one replay request submits the recorded graph and avoids rebuilding the launch sequence operation by operation. Replay still incurs host, driver, graph-launch, synchronization, and device scheduling costs, so the saved fraction must be measured for the captured region.

The benefit can be substantial when short kernels leave visible CPU launch gaps, but it shrinks when long kernels or data movement already dominate wall time. The relevant comparison is the end-to-end captured region before and after replay, including warmup and any uncaptured work, rather than the number of launches alone.

The constraint is structural rather than numerical. A graph executable replays captured nodes and dependencies against referenced storage, while supported graph updates can change a bounded set of node parameters (NVIDIA Corporation 2026). Variable sequence shapes, changing batches, conditional expert routes, and allocation patterns may therefore require multiple graph variants or leave dynamic regions in ordinary stream execution. Decode often presents more reusable operation shapes than prefill, but that advantage depends on the runtime’s bucketing and capture strategy.

Operator fusion and CUDA Graphs attack different sources of exposed time: fusion removes kernels and intermediate traffic, while graph replay reduces repeated host submission for the kernels that remain. When a trace attributes a material fraction of end-to-end time to CPU launch gaps and the workload admits stable capture, replay becomes a candidate intervention; profiling after capture must confirm that the gap moved rather than assuming all noncompute overhead disappeared.

9.8.4 FlashAttention and tiled attention primitives

Standard self-attention computes \(\text{Softmax}(\AttentionQueryMatrix\AttentionKeyMatrix^T / \sqrt{\AttentionHeadDimension})\AttentionValuesMatrix\), where \(\AttentionQueryMatrix\), \(\AttentionKeyMatrix\), and \(\AttentionValuesMatrix\) are matrices of shape \([\text{sequence length}{\times}\text{head dimension}]\). The naive implementation materializes the full \(\SequenceLength{\times}\SequenceLength\) attention matrix \(\UnscaledAttentionScoreMatrix= \AttentionQueryMatrix\AttentionKeyMatrix^T\) in HBM, where \(\SequenceLength\) is the sequence length. For \(\SequenceLength= 8192\) and FP16 precision, this matrix alone consumes \(8192 \times 8192 \times 2 \approx 134\) MB per attention head, so the quadratic score tensors run to several gigabytes per layer. The simplified HBM-traffic accounting compares HBM-visible bytes for a 64-head Llama-style layer under explicit traffic assumptions.

FlashAttention (Dao et al. 2022) reformulates attention using tiling. Instead of materializing the full \(\SequenceLength{\times}\SequenceLength\) attention matrix, it processes \(\AttentionQueryMatrix\), \(\AttentionKeyMatrix\), and \(\AttentionValuesMatrix\) in small blocks that fit in on-chip SRAM. The algorithm loads tiles of \(\AttentionQueryMatrix\), \(\AttentionKeyMatrix\), and \(\AttentionValuesMatrix\), computes partial attention scores, and maintains running statistics (online softmax) to produce the exact result without ever storing the full attention matrix in HBM.

In this scenario, tiling cuts HBM traffic about 65\(\times\).

The reduction in HBM traffic can be dramatic. For a sequence length of 8192, 64 heads, and head dimension 128 in FP16, the simplified naive estimate writes and reads the score and probability matrices and moves \(\AttentionQueryMatrix\), \(\AttentionKeyMatrix\), \(\AttentionValuesMatrix\), and output once, totaling approximately 34.9 GB for the full layer, or 545.3 MB per head. The idealized FlashAttention lower bound counts one read of \(\AttentionQueryMatrix\), \(\AttentionKeyMatrix\), and \(\AttentionValuesMatrix\) and one output write, totaling approximately 536.9 MB for the full layer, or 8.4 MB per head. The resulting 65× ratio is a scenario comparison, not a claim about allocated memory or realized kernel traffic; actual traffic depends on tile reloads and the implementation schedule.

The key insight behind FlashAttention is the online softmax trick, which processes scores incrementally in a single pass and so makes tiling possible for an operation that appears to require global information. Standard softmax computes \(\text{softmax}(\SoftmaxRowScore_i) = e^{\SoftmaxRowScore_i} / \sum_j e^{\SoftmaxRowScore_j}\), but for numerical stability it first subtracts the global maximum: \(\text{softmax}(\SoftmaxRowScore_i) = e^{\SoftmaxRowScore_i - \SoftmaxRowMaximum} / \sum_j e^{\SoftmaxRowScore_j - \SoftmaxRowMaximum}\) where \(\SoftmaxRowMaximum= \max_j \SoftmaxRowScore_j\). Finding this global maximum seems to require seeing all scores first, which would force materializing the full \(\SequenceLength{\times}\SequenceLength\) matrix.

The online algorithm avoids this by maintaining running statistics that are updated incrementally as each tile is processed. When processing tile \(\ProcessedKeyTileIndex\), the algorithm executes four steps:

  1. Computes a local block of scores \({\OnlineSoftmaxScoreBlock}_t = \OnlineSoftmaxQueryBlock {\OnlineSoftmaxKeyTile}_t^T\).
  2. Updates the running maximum: \(\NewSoftmaxRunningMaximum= \max(\OldSoftmaxRunningMaximum, \max({\OnlineSoftmaxScoreBlock}_t))\).
  3. Rescales the previous running sum and output: multiply by \(e^{\OldSoftmaxRunningMaximum- \NewSoftmaxRunningMaximum}\) to correct for the updated maximum.
  4. Computes the local softmax contribution using \(\NewSoftmaxRunningMaximum\) and accumulates into the running output.

After processing all tiles, divide the accumulated weighted numerator by the running softmax denominator. This normalization yields the same mathematical attention result as the standard algorithm, up to floating-point roundoff. The rescaling step (step 3) is the critical innovation: it allows the algorithm to “fix up” previous partial results when a new tile reveals a larger maximum value. The algorithm is exact in the mathematical sense, but floating-point execution order can still change the last few bits relative to an unfused implementation.

The cost of tiling includes additional rescaling, reductions, and scheduling work. Avoiding HBM traffic can outweigh those costs when transfers are exposed on the critical path, but a memory-bound baseline does not make extra arithmetic free. Scalar throughput, reduction dependencies, register pressure, and occupancy can become the new limits. The kernel must therefore be evaluated on its measured traffic and complete execution time.

FlashAttention achieves this traffic reduction by staging matrix blocks across the memory hierarchy, as mapped in figure 5: instead of materializing the quadratic \(\SequenceLength{\times}\SequenceLength\) score matrix in off-chip HBM, the kernel streams \(\AttentionQueryMatrix\), \(\AttentionKeyMatrix\), and \(\AttentionValuesMatrix\) tiles into on-chip storage and evaluates softmax statistics online. Consider illustrative \(128{\times}128\) tiles. The input payload includes a block of \(\AttentionQueryMatrix\) (\(128 \times 128 \times 2 \approx\) 32.8 KB), a block of \(\AttentionKeyMatrix\) (\(128 \times 128 \times 2 \approx\) 32.8 KB), and a block of \(\AttentionValuesMatrix\) (32.8 KB), totaling approximately 98.3 KB. This input payload is smaller than the H100’s 228 KB of shared memory per SM, but it is not the complete kernel working set: accumulators, softmax state, and buffering also consume registers or shared memory. The tile score \(\AttentionScoreTile= \AttentionQueryMatrix_i \AttentionKeyMatrix_j^T\) is computed and consumed on chip; it is never materialized as a full HBM tensor. The illustrative schedule iterates over \(8192/128 =\) 64 column tiles for each of 64 row tiles, reloading tiles as required. The quadratic \(\SequenceLength{\times}\SequenceLength\) score and probability matrices are never materialized: the persistent per-head tensors are \(\AttentionQueryMatrix\), \(\AttentionKeyMatrix\), \(\AttentionValuesMatrix\), \(\AttentionHeadOutput\), and \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength)\) softmax statistics.

FlashAttention-2 (Dao 2023) further optimizes the algorithm for GPU architectures with many streaming multiprocessors by restructuring the parallelism pattern. The original FlashAttention parallelizes over batch and head dimensions, meaning each thread block handles one (batch, head) pair and iterates over the full sequence. FlashAttention-2 additionally parallelizes over the sequence dimension of the query matrix, distributing work across thread blocks more efficiently and achieving better occupancy. It also reduces the number of non-GEMM FLOPs by restructuring the rescaling operations and exploiting the asymmetry between the Q loop (outer) and K/V loop (inner).

Dao, Tri. 2023. “FlashAttention-2: Faster Attention with Better Parallelism and Work Partitioning.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2307.08691.

Newer FlashAttention-family kernels target Hopper-era hardware features such as FP8 Tensor Cores and the Tensor Memory Accelerator, a hardware path for asynchronous bulk tensor movement between HBM and shared memory. The systems principle is the same as the original algorithm: as the hardware exposes faster movement and lower-precision execution paths, the attention schedule must be rewritten to use those paths without materializing the quadratic workspace.

The original breakthrough made memory movement part of the algorithmic cost model. Attention still requires matrix multiplication, but reducing its arithmetic alone does not address the cost of materializing and rereading large intermediate tensors. FlashAttention instead trades a small amount of extra arithmetic, the online rescaling and backward-pass recomputation, for the elimination of quadratic HBM-resident state.

Example 9.2: The FlashAttention breakthrough
Scenario: Standard Transformer attention materializes a full \(\SequenceLength\times \SequenceLength\) attention score matrix in high-latency GPU HBM, creating a quadratic memory and bandwidth bottleneck as sequence length \(\SequenceLength\) grows (Dao et al. 2022).

Diagnosis: Materializing attention intermediates creates memory traffic that an arithmetic-only analysis misses. FlashAttention restructures attention via block-by-block tiling and online softmax, holding intermediate state on chip to compute attention without writing the \(\SequenceLength\times \SequenceLength\) matrix to global memory.

Systems lesson: FlashAttention demonstrates I/O-aware algorithm design: eliminating quadratic HBM intermediates can improve runtime even when backward execution recomputes values. The commonly quoted 2–4\(\times\) wall-clock speedup is not a general prediction for long-context workloads; measured gains depend on sequence length, hardware, the baseline kernel, and whether the timing covers attention alone or the complete model.

Dao, T., D. Y. Fu, S. Ermon, A. Rudra, and C. Ré. 2022. “FlashAttention: Fast and Memory-Efficient Exact Attention with IO-Awareness.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 35: 16344–59. https://doi.org/10.52202/068431-1189.

The 65× ratio measures traffic, the bytes moved through HBM. Eliminating the quadratic intermediate also frees capacity, the bytes that must be resident at once, and that second quantity grows differently with sequence length. Standard attention’s workspace rises as \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength^2)\) while FlashAttention’s rises as \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength)\), so the gap widens with every doubling of context (figure 6); the much larger ratios on that plot are capacity ratios and should not be read as traffic or speed. The freed capacity matters for serving because prefill and decode often share a replica. While a long prompt is being prefilled, its score workspace occupies HBM alongside the KV cache of every request already decoding, so removing that workspace leaves room for the scheduler to admit more concurrent requests.

FlashAttention reduces the memory wall within a single GPU by tiling across the SRAM-HBM boundary. For sequence lengths that exceed the memory capacity of a single GPU, the same tiling principle extends across multiple GPUs via Ring Attention (Liu et al. 2023). Distributing the sequence blocks across a ring of accelerators and overlapping communication with computation enables Ring Attention to handle context windows that would be impractical on single-GPU configurations.

Liu, Hao, Matei Zaharia, and Pieter Abbeel. 2023. “Ring Attention with Blockwise Transformers for Near-Infinite Context.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2310.01889, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2310.01889.

↩︎ Ring Attention’s rotation of key/value blocks around a ring of accelerators, and its overlap condition, are established in Context parallelism with ring attention versus Ulysses.

Checkpoint 9.2: FlashAttention mechanics

Verify understanding of memory-aware attention:

Self-Check: Question
  1. In FlashAttention’s online softmax algorithm, arrange the following steps in the exact order executed when processing tile \(t\) of key-value blocks in fast on-chip SRAM:
  1. Rescale previous running sum and output accumulator by multiplying by \(e^{m_{\text{old}} - m_{\text{new}}}\)
  2. Compute local block of attention scores \(A_t = Q_{\text{block}} K_t^T\)
  3. Compute local softmax probabilities with \(m_{\text{new}}\) and accumulate into running output
  4. Update the running maximum: \(m_{\text{new}} = \max(m_{\text{old}}, \max(A_t))\)

  1. Why are CUDA Graphs highly effective at accelerating the autoregressive decode phase of Large Language Models, but significantly more difficult to apply to the prompt prefill phase?

    1. CUDA Graph replay requires capture-compatible work and valid referenced storage; repetitive decode shapes often allow more reuse than variable-length prefill shapes
    2. CUDA Graphs only support integer quantization kernels and cannot record floating-point matrix multiplications used during prefill
    3. CUDA Graphs execute exclusively on CPU cores and cannot capture GPU kernels that exceed 1000 TFLOP/s
    4. CUDA Graphs require disaggregated serving network fabrics to replay kernel dispatches across multiple nodes

  2. Explain how tiled attention can improve runtime even when online softmax rescaling or backward recomputation adds arithmetic. Why do removed HBM transfers alone not establish a fixed wall-clock speedup?

  3. True or False: Wrapping an unfused PyTorch layer in a CUDA Graph automatically fuses adjacent element-wise kernels into a single GPU kernel to eliminate intermediate high-bandwidth memory (HBM) writes.

  4. Consider a bias-GELU-dropout chain applied to a tensor with hidden dimension 4096 and batch size 2048 in FP16. The unfused path launches three kernels and materializes two intermediate tensors of approximately 16.8 MB each. If a fused kernel can retain their values in registers or SRAM, how does fusion change memory traffic and execution?

    1. It increases total HBM traffic to 128 MB because the fused kernel must write debug checkpoints after each sub-operation
    2. It removes four intermediate transfers totaling approximately 67.1 MB and reduces three launches to one, while preserving the input read and final output write
    3. It converts the operation into a pure CPU task to bypass GPU shared memory allocation limits
    4. It eliminates the need to load the weight matrix \(W\) from HBM by synthesizing weights dynamically in registers

9.9 Precision Engineering

Fusion reduces the number of trips through HBM; precision engineering reduces the payload of each trip. Moving FP16 weights through HBM consumes twice as many bytes as FP8. For bandwidth-bound kernels, shrinking weights from 2 bytes to 1 byte can roughly halve weight-read traffic and increase effective memory bandwidth. The engineering decision is where numerical noise can be tolerated to reduce bandwidth pressure and where quality demands higher precision. The quantization techniques in this section apply reduced precision to efficient inference at scale.

↩︎ FP8 as a training-time format, with distinct roles for E4M3 and E5M2, is established in FP8 for distributed training.

9.9.1 Outliers and block-wise quantization

The first inference-side precision decision is how to shrink weights while preserving the information needed for model quality. INT8 uses the same payload width as FP8, while INT4 reduces that payload further; their numerical ranges and supported execution paths differ. LLM quantization must also account for outlier features. In the models studied by Dettmers et al. (2022), a small number of hidden dimensions (about 0.1 percent of features) exhibited activation magnitudes roughly 3–20\(\times\) larger than the rest. Uniform per-tensor quantization can either clip large values or widen the range to include them, reducing resolution for smaller values. Weight tensors face a related problem when a few large weights determine the scale for the entire tensor. Block-wise quantization limits that weight-side effect, while the runtime and calibration techniques that follow address activation outliers.

Dettmers, Tim, Mike Lewis, Younes Belkada, and Luke Zettlemoyer. 2022. “LLM.int8(): 8-Bit Matrix Multiplication for Transformers at Scale.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS), 30318–32. https://doi.org/10.52202/068431-2198.
Definition 9.3: Block-wise quantization

Block-wise quantization is an ML quantization scheme that partitions a weight tensor into nonoverlapping groups of \(\QuantizationBlockSize\) elements and assigns each group its own scale. An affine scheme uses \(\QuantizationScale_i = (\BlockRangeMaximum- \BlockRangeMinimum) / (2^\NumericBitWidth- 1)\) together with an offset or zero point; a symmetric scheme centers the range at zero and needs only scale metadata. The metadata examples below assume symmetric scale-only quantization.

  1. Significance: With block size \(\QuantizationBlockSize = 64\) and \(\NumericBitWidth= 4\) bits, weights compress from 16 bits to 4 bits (a 4\(\times\) memory reduction) while each FP16 scale adds \(16/64 = 0.25\) bits per weight. This yields an effective bit-width of \(4.25\) bits per weight: 6.25 percent overhead relative to the INT4 payload, or about 1.6 percent of the original FP16 weight size.
  2. Distinction: Unlike per-tensor quantization, which applies a single scale across the entire weight matrix and forces that scale to accommodate outlier values at the cost of wasting precision on the majority of near-zero weights, block-wise quantization contains outlier damage within individual blocks, preventing a single extreme value from degrading quantization fidelity for the whole tensor.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that block size is a free hyperparameter. Smaller blocks (\(\QuantizationBlockSize = 32\)) can improve local range resolution but double the scale metadata overhead vs. \(\QuantizationBlockSize = 64\). At \(\QuantizationBlockSize = 16\), the scale adds 1 bit per weight: 25 percent overhead relative to the INT4 payload, or 6.25 percent of the original FP16 weight size. The effect on model quality and kernel speed must be evaluated rather than inferred from block size alone.

The metadata price stays small across formats. An INT8 scheme with one FP32 scale per 128 weights adds \(32/128 = 0.25\) bits per weight, about 3 percent of its payload, so block-wise scaling isolates outliers without the bandwidth penalty of a higher-precision format. Figure 7 isolates this dynamic-range protection across an eight-weight sample containing a single outlier (\(58.7\)). Under per-tensor scaling (left), the outlier dictates a coarse scale factor (\(\QuantizationScale\approx 0.462\)) that forces six of the seven small weights to round to zero. Partitioning the tensor into four-element blocks (right) confines that coarse scale to the second block, allowing the uncorrupted first block to adopt a fine scale (\(\QuantizationScale_1 \approx 0.00165\)) that preserves full INT8 resolution.

Block-wise scaling contains weight outliers inside the stored format. Activation outliers, and the rounding error that low-bit weights accumulate, call for methods that act at other stages. The four methods below all work post-training, without retraining the model. GPTQ, AWQ, and SmoothQuant prepare the model offline from a small calibration set, while LLM.int8() acts at runtime; section 9.9.2 takes up the alternative of training with quantization when post-training quality falls short. LLM.int8() handles outliers at runtime by decomposing a matrix multiplication into two parts: selected outlier dimensions processed in FP16 and the remaining dimensions processed in INT8. A magnitude threshold identifies the outlier dimensions, and the two matrix products are combined to produce the output. This mixed path preserves higher precision where uniform quantization would introduce disproportionate error. Its quality benefit must be weighed against the runtime cost of identifying outliers and executing the separate paths.

Generative Pre-trained Transformer Quantization (GPTQ) (Frantar et al. 2023) moves error compensation into weight calibration. It uses second-order information, an estimate from calibration activations of how sensitive the layer’s output is to each weight, to guide layer-wise reconstruction, adjusting remaining unquantized weights to compensate for errors introduced by weights already quantized. The result can support low-bit representations, including INT4, without treating each weight’s error independently. Its quality depends on the model, bit width, grouping, and calibration data; the reconstruction objective is a proxy for the eventual task-level quality criterion.

Frantar, Elias, Saleh Ashkboos, Torsten Hoefler, and Dan Alistarh. 2023. “GPTQ: Accurate Post-Training Quantization for Generative Pre-Trained Transformers.” In CoRR, abs/2210.17323. https://doi.org/10.48550/ARXIV.2210.17323.
Lin, Ji, Jiaming Tang, Haotian Tang, Shang Yang, Wei-Ming Chen, Wei-Chen Wang, Guangxuan Xiao, Xingyu Dang, Chuang Gan, and Song Han. 2024. “AWQ: Activation-Aware Weight Quantization for on-Device LLM Compression and Acceleration.” Proceedings of Machine Learning and Systems 6: 87–100.

Activation-Aware Weight Quantization (AWQ) (Lin et al. 2024) uses activation magnitudes to guide weight scaling rather than GPTQ-style second-order reconstruction. Weights associated with large activations can contribute disproportionately to output error. AWQ protects these salient weights through channel scaling before group quantization, using calibration activations to choose the scales. This provides another route to low-bit weight storage; comparison with GPTQ requires the same model, bit budget, calibration conditions, and held-out quality evaluation.

SmoothQuant (Xiao et al. 2023) redistributes quantization difficulty between activations and weights before inference. Its calibration exploits activation outliers that concentrate in particular channels. A per-channel scaling transformation divides activations by a smoothing factor and multiplies the corresponding weights by that factor, preserving the matrix product before rounding. Reducing activation range can make the weights harder to quantize, so the smoothing choice balances the two errors. This enables W8A8 (weight-8-bit, activation-8-bit) execution with compatible INT8 kernels; quality and speed still depend on calibration and the target implementation.

Xiao, Guangxuan, Ji Lin, Mickaël Seznec, Hao Wu, Julien Demouth, and Song Han. 2023. “SmoothQuant: Accurate and Efficient Post-Training Quantization for Large Language Models.” International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), 38087–99.

These techniques place the outlier-handling cost in different stages: runtime decomposition, weight calibration, or a preprocessing transformation. The choice must satisfy both the quality budget and the execution path available on the target hardware. An INT4 weight representation can save HBM traffic even when a GPU kernel dequantizes it for FP16 arithmetic; an INT8 representation can also use integer arithmetic when a compatible kernel and device support it. The later weight-only and weight-activation comparison separates these bandwidth and compute benefits.

Each of these methods trades some calibration or runtime cost for bandwidth, and at the extreme end of the deployment spectrum quantization moves from an optimization to a physical necessity. A mobile or federated deployment provides that limiting case. When the device has only a few gigabytes of memory shared with the rest of the phone, or far less below that, precision is no longer a tuning knob after the model is chosen; it is part of the feasibility test.

Lighthouse 9.1: Archetype C (Federated MobileNet): Precision as feasibility
For Archetype C (Federated MobileNet), smartphone-class devices with power budgets of roughly 2–10 W, about 3 W sustained with brief bursts toward 10 W, precision is part of the feasibility test: the model’s weights, activations, and any local training state must fit beside the rest of the phone’s workload, and INT8 or INT4 weights often decide whether an on-device model fits at all. Microcontroller-class sensors sit at an extreme below Archetype C, where a live FP16 working set that exceeds a 256 KB SRAM budget cannot reside on the device at all; smaller models or weights streamed from nonvolatile storage require a different accounting. Binary neural networks and 1-bit quantization push this to the extreme, representing weights as single bits (+1/-1). While this trades significant accuracy, it reduces the weight memory footprint by 16\(\times\) relative to FP16 (32\(\times\) relative to FP32). These storage ratios exclude metadata and activations; device energy savings require a supported execution path and measurement of the complete workload.

9.9.2 Post-training vs. quantization-aware training

When a post-training recipe misses the quality budget, the precision decision shifts from calibration to training cost. The trade-off between Post-Training Quantization (PTQ) and Quantization-Aware Training (QAT) centers on the balance between engineering effort and model fidelity. PTQ starts from trained weights and avoids a gradient-based retraining loop. Methods such as GPTQ and AWQ use calibration activations to guide weight quantization, although they optimize different criteria. Their cost depends on model size, calibration data, and implementation. Reducing precision further can increase quality loss, so a recipe that works at INT8 does not establish that INT4 or INT3 will meet the same requirement. Evaluation must cover the intended tasks and input distribution, using held-out data rather than the calibration set alone.

When PTQ fails to meet quality thresholds, QAT offers another way to adapt the model to the target precision. Simulating low-precision rounding during the forward pass and approximating gradients during the backward pass via the straight-through estimator (STE)3 allows the network to adjust its weights in response to quantization error. The training recipe must match the intended inference representation closely enough for that adaptation to transfer, and the resulting model still needs to pass the quality gate.

3 Straight-through estimator (STE): Rounding has zero gradient almost everywhere, so backpropagation through a quantized layer would otherwise stall. The STE, discussed by Bengio et al. (2013) for hard stochastic neurons, passes the upstream gradient through the rounding step as if rounding had not happened. It is a heuristic that works well in QAT practice, not a convergence guarantee.

Bengio, Yoshua, Nicholas Léonard, and Aaron Courville. 2013. “Estimating or Propagating Gradients Through Stochastic Neurons for Conditional Computation.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1308.3432.

QAT introduces training costs: forward and backward passes, trainable state, and data suitable for adapting the model. The hardware and runtime requirements depend on which parameters are updated and how long training continues. Adapter-based quantized fine-tuning can reduce trainable state by freezing a quantized base model and updating small low-rank adapter matrices, pairs of thin matrices whose product is added to a frozen weight matrix so that only the thin pair is trained. It can improve task performance under that representation, but using a quantized base does not by itself make the procedure QAT for the final deployed model. Merging or requantizing adapters can change the numerical path, so quality must be checked after the intended deployment conversion.

In many deployment environments, a practical workflow follows a two-stage approach: deploy with PTQ first when it meets quality requirements, then apply QAT or adapter-based quantized fine-tuning if the PTQ model fails at the target precision. This sequence minimizes engineering effort while preserving the option of higher quality when needed.

9.9.3 Weight-only vs. weight-activation quantization

The final precision choice is whether the optimization should stop at the weights or include activations as well. Weight-only quantization, as used by GPTQ and AWQ, stores weights at reduced precision while retaining higher-precision activations. In a common INT4 implementation, the kernel dequantizes weights to FP16 during the GEMM and performs matrix arithmetic on FP16 Tensor Cores. The storage format therefore reduces weight capacity and read traffic without implying INT4 arithmetic throughput. This is a useful candidate for weight-dominated, small-batch decode, provided the saved traffic outweighs unpacking and dequantization costs.

Weight-activation quantization (SmoothQuant, FP8 training) reduces both weights and activations to lower precision, enabling the GEMM to execute using lower-precision arithmetic (INT8 Tensor Cores, FP8 Tensor Cores). This provides both bandwidth and compute benefits but is more challenging to implement without quality degradation, because activation distributions are more dynamic and harder to quantize than weight distributions.

The roofline placement determines which benefit is exposed, which gives a working rule. At small batch sizes, where decode streams weights below the ridge, weight-only INT4 attacks the binding term by cutting weight bytes, and leaving the arithmetic in FP16 costs little because arithmetic is not the binding term. At large batch sizes or in prefill, where execution approaches the compute ceiling, weight-activation FP8 raises that ceiling, which weight-only formats cannot do. Neither format has a universal advantage in speed per unit of quality loss. Hardware support, matrix shapes, cache traffic, and calibration quality all affect the comparison. A serving system can use different formats at different operating points, but each configuration must be evaluated against the same quality criterion and its own latency and throughput requirements.

The same precision problem extends to the KV cache, which determines whether weight savings become larger batches. In decode, weights may be compressed aggressively while per-request cache state still grows with sequence length, so a precision change that leaves the cache untouched may fail to move the serving bottleneck. Numerical compression reduces the bytes stored per cached key or value, while GQA, met earlier as a source of decode-attention reuse, reduces how many key-value heads must be cached for each layer. GQA is a model-architecture choice, not a precision switch that can be applied unchanged to any checkpoint. Both mechanisms matter because the scheduler admits requests against the combined memory footprint of weights plus active cache state. The KV cache capacity calculation isolates the local capacity effect, holding serving policy fixed and excluding quantization metadata, runtime workspace, and admission headroom. Its batch sizes are capacity bounds under those assumptions.

↪ KV cache quantization and grouped query attention as serving-memory techniques are developed in KV cache compression and architectural optimization.

Napkin Math 9.3: The precision dividend
Problem: A 70B parameter model is served on 8\(\times\) H100 GPUs. The model weights in FP16 consume 140 GB (17.5 GB per GPU). KV cache at FP16 consumes 1.34 GB per request. How does quantizing weights to INT4 and KV cache to INT8 change the maximum batch size?

Math:

Step 1: Baseline capacity in FP16. Before optimization, weights consume 17.5 GB per GPU, leaving 85.9 GB minus 17.5 GB = 68.4 GB per GPU for the KV cache. With an FP16 KV cache requiring 1.34 GB total per request (approximately 0.17 GB per GPU), the system supports a maximum batch size of approximately 407 requests.

Step 2: Optimized capacity with INT4 weights and INT8 KV cache. Quantizing weights to INT4 compresses the per-GPU footprint by 4/16 to 4.4 GB, expanding available cache memory to 85.9 GB minus 4.4 GB = 81.5 GB per GPU. Compressing the KV cache to INT8 simultaneously reduces the per-request footprint to approximately 0.67 GB total (0.08 GB per GPU). Dividing the expanded cache capacity by the halved footprint yields a maximum batch size of approximately 971 requests.

Systems insight: Precision engineering changes serving economics by enabling larger batch sizes. Larger batches amortize the fixed cost of weight loading, shifting operations from memory-bound toward compute-bound. The calculated capacity gain is 2.4×; converting that headroom into throughput requires measuring the larger batch against compute, cache-read bandwidth, and latency limits.

Precision engineering reduces the payload needed to represent values; operator fusion can eliminate transfers of intermediate values altogether. Their combined memory savings can make a configuration feasible even when either technique alone is insufficient. For example, FlashAttention avoids quadratic attention intermediates, while INT8 KV cache compression reduces the remaining cache payload relative to FP16. Their latency gains need not multiply: conversion costs and a shift to a different bottleneck can limit the combined speedup. Because these patterns recur across stable layer structures, the next question is when a compiler can apply the transformations across the whole model graph.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Large Language Models exhibit emergent activation outlier features where \(\sim 0.1\%\) of channels have magnitudes \(3–20\times\) larger than normal. How does SmoothQuant enable efficient uniform INT8 quantization (W8A8) across both weights and activations without suffering catastrophic quality loss?

    1. SmoothQuant drops all outlier channels from the model entirely, using low-rank distillation to compensate for removed hidden dimensions
    2. SmoothQuant splits the matrix multiplication at runtime into an FP16 branch for outliers and an INT8 branch for standard features
    3. SmoothQuant applies a per-channel scaling factor that divides activations by \(s\) and multiplies weights by \(s\), migrating quantization difficulty from dynamic activations to static weights prior to inference
    4. SmoothQuant computes an exact Hessian inverse across all layers during training using the straight-through estimator

  2. Contrast weight-only quantization (e.g. AWQ INT4) with weight-activation quantization (e.g. SmoothQuant W8A8 or FP8) in terms of target serving operational regime (batch size) and hardware execution units used.

  3. True or False: In block-wise INT4 quantization, reducing the group block size \(G_{\text{block}}\) from 64 to 16 can improve local range resolution but quadruples the scale-factor metadata overhead from 0.25 bits/weight (6.25% overhead on INT4) to 1.0 bit/weight (25% overhead on INT4).

  4. A 70B model uses 17.5 GB of weights per GPU on eight 80 GB GPUs. Ignoring runtime reserves and quantization metadata, INT4 weights use approximately 4.4 GB per GPU and INT8 KV cache halves the per-token cache footprint. Why does the KV capacity gain combine two distinct effects rather than equal the fourfold weight reduction?

    1. Because INT4 weights bypass the GPU memory hierarchy and execute directly from CPU system RAM
    2. Because quantizing the KV cache converts autoregressive decoding into non-autoregressive parallel generation
    3. Because INT8 KV cache pages eliminate the need for GPU virtual address translation tables in PagedAttention
    4. Because reducing weights from 17.5 GB to 4.4 GB expands available KV cache space from 62.5 GB to 75.6 GB per GPU, which, when multiplied by halving the per-request INT8 KV cache footprint, multiplicatively expands total admitted batch capacity
