9.10 Graph Compilation

After precision and fusion expose repeatable optimization patterns, graph compilation asks whether those patterns are stable enough for the compiler to own. Manually authoring fused CUDA kernels for every layer permutation across rapidly evolving architectures is an impractical burden for human developers. A graph compiler analyzes the model’s computational graph and generates optimized, hardware-aware machine instructions, transforming high-level PyTorch code into specialized kernels when shape stability and replay volume justify the compilation cost.

Systems Perspective 9.5: Hardware-software co-design
Compilation also determines where values live and which execution units perform the work. A target-specific backend chooses matrix tiles, places intermediates within the memory hierarchy, and maps operations to supported arithmetic units. Different accelerator backends make different choices because their arrays, instruction sets, and storage resources differ. These mappings implement the model’s numerical contract; any precision changes or reordered reductions must remain within that contract. Hardware-aware compilation therefore joins algorithmic structure to the resource limits established by profiling.

9.10.1 The compilation pipeline

A graph compiler transforms high-level model definitions into optimized hardware instructions across four progressive tiers: graph capture, graph-level optimization, target-aware operator fusion, and code generation. The transformer feed-forward network block in figure 8 traces one lowering through PyTorch’s compiler, torch.compile, whose three components section 9.10.2 introduces. Its capture front end, TorchDynamo, records the subgraph; a decomposition step (AOTAutograd) exposes GEMM-bias primitives; and its code generator, TorchInductor, groups the first GEMM with GELU and selects a fused epilogue alongside separate GEMM and normalization kernels. The four-operation, three-kernel path is one illustrative outcome, and the trace shows the counts a real model and backend produce.

In the graph capture stage, the compiler constructs a representation of operations and tensor dependencies. The illustrated FFN block forms a directed acyclic graph with four primary nodes and associated parameter tensors. Capturing a Python execution path may specialize the graph to assumptions that must be checked on later calls. Symbolic dimensions can accommodate changing tensor sizes, but do not by themselves represent arbitrary data-dependent branches or loops. Those require supported control-flow constructs, separate captured regions, or execution outside the compiled region.

During graph-level optimization, the compiler applies algebraic simplifications and operation rewriting. It identifies that the bias addition in the first Linear layer is a candidate for folding into the matrix multiplication kernel. It also recognizes that the GELU activation is an element-wise operation that depends only on the output of the first Linear. Exposing these dependencies prepares the graph for fusion and kernel selection. A smaller graph alone does not establish a speedup, because its nodes may represent operations with very different costs.

The operator fusion pass identifies compatible operations that can share a kernel and avoid intermediate transfers. For the FFN block, the compiler can fuse the GELU activation into the tail of the first Linear kernel when the GEMM supports that epilogue. The second Linear lies between GELU and LayerNorm, so those two operations are not an adjacent fusion pair. Instead of materializing the intermediate result of the first Linear and rereading it for GELU, the fused epilogue consumes the values on chip. The HBM saving depends on tensor sizes and cache behavior, while profitability also depends on the fused kernel’s resource use.

The memory planning pass determines when to allocate and free tensors. Without optimization, a transformer might allocate separate buffers for every intermediate activation. The compiler analyzes tensor lifetimes, recognizing that, absent a residual connection, the input to the first Linear is dead once that Linear has consumed it, and reuses the same physical memory addresses. For inference and other forward-only workloads, this buffer reuse can turn peak temporary memory from the sum of many layer-local buffers into the maximum live working set. Training activations that must be saved for the backward pass require checkpointing or rematerialization; ordinary buffer reuse cannot make those saved tensors disappear. Memory planning also interacts with operator fusion: fusing two operations eliminates the intermediate tensor between them, which both removes the HBM traffic and removes the memory allocation. The compiler must reason about both effects jointly to make profitable decisions.

The kernel selection pass maps each fused operation to an available implementation. For the first Linear-GELU, the compiler may select a cuBLAS or CUTLASS GEMM with a compatible activation epilogue; the second Linear remains a GEMM, and LayerNorm requires reduction and normalization work. The illustrated path reduces 4 kernels to 3 by eliminating one intermediate materialization. Other shapes or backends may choose different boundaries, including separate kernels when fusion is unavailable or unprofitable.

9.10.2 torch.compile

For workloads already written in PyTorch, torch.compile is the least disruptive compiler intervention. It tries to capture enough static graph to fuse memory-bound regions while preserving PyTorch’s dynamic Python execution model. It operates through three components: TorchDynamo for graph capture, AOTAutograd for graph-level preparation, and TorchInductor for code generation. AOTAutograd lowers the captured graph into primitive tensor operations, the decomposition stage of figure 8, for inference and training alike; for training it also constructs the backward graph ahead of time so the backward pass can be compiled too.

TorchDynamo operates at the Python bytecode level,4 a design choice that distinguishes it from earlier tracing approaches. torch.jit.trace records tensor operations executed on example inputs, while torch.fx symbolic tracing executes with proxy values. Each has restrictions on the Python behavior it can represent. TorchDynamo intercepts Python frame evaluation to capture supported tensor computation. Python constructs it cannot capture, including some data-dependent control flow and unsupported operations, can cause a graph break. In the default configuration, execution may return to Python and resume capture in a later region. Requiring a single full graph instead makes an unsupported break an error. Larger captured regions create more optimization opportunities, but preserving program behavior takes precedence over capture size.

4 TorchDynamo bytecode interception: CPython’s frame-evaluation hook (PEP 523, introduced in Python 3.6) lets TorchDynamo intercept Python execution and capture supported tensor operations. Capture still depends on supported program behavior and guards that validate specialization assumptions. Its integration with CPython makes interpreter-version compatibility part of the deployment contract.

TorchInductor lowers the captured graph into generated kernels and calls to existing libraries. Common paths include Triton for GPU kernels and C++/OpenMP for CPU kernels. Triton provides a Python-like language that exposes tiling and fusion while its compiler maps the specified work onto GPU execution resources. TorchInductor can fuse supported element-wise patterns and select implementations using heuristics or autotuning, depending on the operation and configuration. These choices make the generated code and its measured behavior the evidence for which optimizations occurred.

A minimal example illustrates the usage:

import torch

def transformer_block(x, w1, w2, ln_weight, ln_bias):
    """Unfused transformer FFN block."""
    h = x @ w1  # Linear projection
    h = torch.nn.functional.gelu(h)  # Activation
    h = h @ w2  # Output projection
    # Layer normalization
    mean = h.mean(dim=-1, keepdim=True)
    var = h.var(dim=-1, keepdim=True, unbiased=False)
    h = (h - mean) / torch.sqrt(var + 1e-5)
    h = h * ln_weight + ln_bias
    return h

# Compile the function—TorchDynamo traces, TorchInductor optimizes
compiled_block = torch.compile(transformer_block)

# First call triggers compilation; subsequent calls use compiled code
output = compiled_block(x, w1, w2, ln_weight, ln_bias)

In this example, torch.compile exposes opportunities to fuse GELU with compatible surrounding work and to combine normalization steps. The actual kernel boundaries depend on shapes, backend support, and compiler decisions. Model code remains standard PyTorch; inspecting the generated kernels and comparing warmed execution against the eager baseline establishes what the compiler achieved.

9.10.3 XLA and TPU optimization

XLA pays for performance with static structure. Used as the backend for JAX and TensorFlow, it generates the high-level optimizer intermediate representation that targets multiple backends, including Google Tensor Processing Units (TPUs), NVIDIA GPUs, and CPUs. Within a selected compilation region, XLA can optimize across operations and layers rather than preserving eager execution boundaries. Whole-program compilation describes the scope available to those optimizations; it does not require every host-side activity in an application to enter one graph.

That view also supports compiler-directed partitioning through General and Scalable Parallelization for ML Computation Graphs (GSPMD), where SPMD denotes the single program, multiple data execution model. Sharding annotations describe how tensors are distributed, and the compiler propagates those choices through the graph and inserts communication such as AllReduce or AllGather. PyTorch interfaces such as DistributedDataParallel or FullyShardedDataParallel expose another way to express distributed execution; they are not the only available interfaces. The important distinction is which partitioning and communication decisions the programmer specifies and which the compiler derives. Compiler assistance reduces implementation work, but the resulting communication volume and layout still require evaluation.

For TPU hardware, XLA maps matrix multiplication onto systolic arrays and chooses layouts and schedules suited to the target generation. Hardware units of \(128{\times}128\), as used in several TPU generations, motivate alignment and padding choices; array dimensions are generation-specific. Padding can improve hardware mapping while adding arithmetic, so useful model throughput remains the relevant outcome. Comparing a tuned TPU run with a less-tuned GPU run does not isolate a compiler benefit: workload, precision, topology, and optimization effort must also be controlled.

The trade-off for XLA’s performance is compilation latency and rigidity. Compilation cost depends on the size and structure of the compiled region in either stack. In JAX, new argument shapes, dtypes, or static argument values can require a new specialization; previously compiled signatures can reuse cached executables. This makes XLA excellent for steady-state production workloads where the graph is static and the model runs for days or weeks, but challenging for research environments involving dynamic shapes or rapid experimental iteration. The choice between torch.compile and XLA often follows the hardware and software stack: NVIDIA GPU workflows commonly start from PyTorch, while TPU workflows commonly use JAX or TensorFlow with XLA.

9.10.4 TensorRT inference optimization

NVIDIA TensorRT specializes an inference graph for a target device and a declared range of inputs. The absence of a backward pass lets it plan tensor lifetimes without retaining activations for gradients, while the inference graph exposes opportunities for fusion and kernel selection. Quantization is a separate choice within that workflow. A quantized model may arrive with explicit scales and conversion operations; calibration on representative data can supply scales when the selected quantization path requires them. Calibration is therefore not a mandatory step for every TensorRT engine.

Mixed-precision quantization requires deciding which tensors and operations can use reduced precision without exceeding the quality budget. Representative calibration data helps select ranges, but does not prove that every layer tolerates the same format. Explicit quantization encodes the intended conversions in the graph, and TensorRT optimizes within that numerical contract. The input graph must also represent inference behavior, with training-time dropout and running-statistic updates disabled. Shape profiles and tensor lifetimes then guide kernel specialization and memory planning.

TensorRT uses kernel autotuning to compare available implementations, called tactics, under the builder’s settings and resource constraints. The search depends on the operation and shape profile; timing caches can reuse prior results rather than benchmarking every candidate again. The selected tactics form part of the TensorRT engine’s specialization to its deployment. That specialization can improve performance, but its advantage over another compiler must be established on the same workload and quality contract, including shapes away from the tuning point.

The trade-off for TensorRT’s aggressive optimization is reduced flexibility and high compilation cost. Build time depends on model size, tactic search, and hardware. By default, an engine targets a particular hardware and shape profile; compatibility modes can broaden supported hardware, and dynamic-shape profiles admit a declared range. Changes outside the engine’s supported contract require a new build. This makes TensorRT a common fit for stable, high-volume production deployments, while torch.compile is often a better fit for development and lower-volume services where rapid iteration matters more than extracting the last percentage of throughput.

9.10.5 The Triton language

Between hand-written CUDA and fully automated graph compilers sits Triton,5 a Python-based language for writing GPU kernels. Triton occupies a middle ground: the programmer specifies the algorithm (tiling strategy, fusion pattern) while Triton handles low-level concerns (thread block scheduling, memory coalescing, shared memory management).

5 Triton: Introduced by Tillet et al. (2019) as an intermediate language and compiler for tiled neural-network computations, and later developed at OpenAI; the key design decision was making the tile, not the thread, the fundamental programming abstraction. This choice directly mirrors the tiling strategy of FlashAttention: the programmer reasons about blocks of data that fit in SRAM, and the compiler maps those blocks to GPU threads and shared memory. This abstraction reduces exposure to low-level CUDA concerns such as warp divergence, bank conflicts, and coalescing while preserving control over the memory hierarchy decisions that determine ML kernel performance.

Tillet, Philippe, H. T. Kung, and David Cox. 2019. “Triton: An Intermediate Language and Compiler for Tiled Neural Network Computations.” Proceedings of the 3rd ACM SIGPLAN International Workshop on Machine Learning and Programming Languages, 10–19. https://doi.org/10.1145/3315508.3329973.

A tile-level GELU kernel illustrates the programming model. Even this single activation is a small fusion, because the tanh approximation chains a cube, a multiply-add, a tanh, and a final scale that eager code written operation by operation would run as separate kernels with intermediate writes:

import triton
import triton.language as tl

@triton.jit
def fused_gelu_kernel(
    input_ptr,
    output_ptr,
    n_elements,
    BLOCK_SIZE: tl.constexpr,
):
    # Each program instance handles BLOCK_SIZE elements
    pid = tl.program_id(0)
    offsets = pid * BLOCK_SIZE + tl.arange(0, BLOCK_SIZE)
    mask = offsets < n_elements

    # Load input tile from HBM into registers
    x = tl.load(input_ptr + offsets, mask=mask)

    # Fused GELU computation (tanh approximation)
    # GELU(x) = 0.5 * x * (1 + tanh(sqrt(2/pi) * (x + 0.044715 *
    # x^3)))
    x_cubed = x * x * x
    inner = 0.7978845608 * (x + 0.044715 * x_cubed)
    gelu = 0.5 * x * (1.0 + tl.math.tanh(inner))

    # Store result back to HBM
    tl.store(output_ptr + offsets, gelu, mask=mask)

The programmer thinks in terms of tiles (BLOCK_SIZE elements), not individual threads. Triton compiles this to Parallel Thread Execution (PTX), NVIDIA’s virtual instruction set, and from there to streaming assembly (SASS), the native machine code of a specific GPU generation, handling thread-to-data mapping, memory coalescing, and register allocation automatically. This makes it feasible for ML engineers (rather than GPU specialists) to write custom fused kernels when the automatic compiler misses a fusion opportunity.

The next step extends the same pattern across operators. For y = LayerNorm(GELU(x)), separate GELU and LayerNorm kernels write and then reread the GELU output. A fused implementation can avoid that round trip when the normalization reduction and its temporary state fit the selected tile schedule. Actual HBM traffic depends on the reduction implementation, cache reuse, and register pressure; the runtime benefit must be measured rather than equated directly with the byte reduction.

The performance-engineering consequence is that Triton turns many custom fusion opportunities into compiler output. When torch.compile identifies a fusion opportunity that requires a custom kernel, TorchInductor automatically generates Triton code for the fused operation. Many fusion benefits therefore come through a single torch.compile call, without hand-written Triton code. For advanced cases where the compiler’s heuristics miss the bottleneck, hand-written Triton kernels provide a middle ground between the accessibility of PyTorch and the performance of hand-tuned CUDA.

9.10.6 Compilation modes and backends

Because most fusion and replay benefits reach PyTorch workloads through torch.compile, its mode setting is usually the first compilation decision a deployment makes. Mode selection is an amortization decision, since additional search is useful only when repeated execution recovers its cost. The default mode provides a baseline for comparing compilation time and warmed performance. The reduce-overhead mode targets eligible launch-bound regions and can use CUDA Graphs (section 9.8.3) to reduce repeated submission, sometimes at additional memory cost. The max-autotune mode expands the search over supported kernel implementations; it does not guarantee that every operation is tuned or that the resulting graph is faster for every input. Compare build time, memory demand, and execution across the expected shape distribution before choosing a mode.

Backend choice follows the same constraint: the more stable and deployment-specific the workload, the more specialization it can support. TorchInductor combines generated kernels with library calls. Export through torch.export produces a graph with an explicit contract, but deployment runtimes require compatible conversion paths and supported operators. TensorRT integrations and Open Neural Network Exchange (ONNX) Runtime use their respective import workflows; an exported graph is not automatically interchangeable among them. Correctness, shape coverage, and measured performance must be checked after conversion.

The value of the longest search, max-autotune, can be priced from a profile breakdown of where a decode step spends its time. A single-GPU budget again keeps collectives out of the accounting, so the scenario uses a 13B model on one H100.

Napkin Math 9.4: The compilation dividend
Problem: Consider an illustrative batch-one inference budget for a 13B-parameter model stored in INT4, with FP16 arithmetic after dequantization. Assume the eager implementation processes 120 tokens/s on a single H100. The constructed time budget assigns 35 percent of step time to element-wise kernels (LayerNorm, GELU, residual additions) and 15 percent to launch overhead. These are scenario inputs, not measured performance; the INT4 weight footprint avoids implying a physically impossible full-FP16 streaming rate. Applying torch.compile with the max-autotune mode, estimate the new throughput.

Math:

Step 1: Identify the addressable overhead. Element-wise kernels (35 percent) and launch overhead (15 percent) total 50 percent of execution time. The scenario assumes that torch.compile reduces element-wise time by 70 percent through fusion and removes most exposed launch overhead. These assumed reductions define a candidate outcome to verify with profiling.

Step 2: Estimate postcompilation time. Starting from an original time per token of \(1 / 120 = 8.33\,\text{ms}\), compilation leaves the GEMM time unchanged at \(8.33 \times 0.50 = 4.17\,\text{ms}\). Fusion reduces element-wise execution time by 70 percent to \(8.33 \times 0.35 \times 0.30 = 0.87\,\text{ms}\), while launch optimizations eliminate 80 percent of launch overhead down to \(8.33 \times 0.15 \times 0.20 = 0.25\,\text{ms}\). Summing these components yields a new time per token of \(4.17 + 0.87 + 0.25 = 5.29\,\text{ms}\), or approximately 189 tokens/s.

Systems insight: Under these assumptions, torch.compile yields a 1.58× modeled speedup without changing GEMM time. The GEMM then accounts for 79 percent of the step, making it the next interval to inspect. Its measured compute, bandwidth, and shape efficiency determine whether precision, batching, kernel selection, or another intervention can shorten it.

9.10.7 Compilation overhead and trade-offs

Compilation is worthwhile when repeated executions recover the build and warmup cost before the model or its execution shapes change. Build costs span orders of magnitude, from seconds or minutes of torch.compile warmup to tens of minutes for a TensorRT engine of a 70B model, so the break-even replay count differs just as widely. Long-running training jobs and high-volume services offer many reuses; short-lived model versions and heterogeneous requests may not. The compilation dividend above makes the break-even concrete.

In that scenario, compilation saves about 3.0 ms per generated token. A max-autotune search of about 15 min, the mode the dividend assumed, is repaid after roughly 300,000 tokens, or about 26 min of continuous single-stream generation at the compiled rate. A default-mode build of about 1 min would need only about 20,000 tokens if it captured the same saving. Repayment depends on the number of executions that reuse the artifact, not simply on how many days the model remains deployed. Frequent recompilation or low demand can prevent the longer search from paying back. Table 11 summarizes how the common compilation paths trade build cost against shape flexibility.

Graph breaks, the capture interruptions introduced by unsupported program behavior, divide the regions over which a compiler can fuse operators and plan memory. Many small regions can therefore retain the launch and transfer costs that motivated compilation. Refactoring unsupported control flow can enlarge those regions, but the maintenance cost belongs in the optimization decision.

Shape specialization creates a related trade-off. Kernels specialized for a narrow shape range can exploit more fixed information, while symbolic dimensions or a broader supported range accommodate variable batches and sequence lengths. The appropriate choice follows the production shape distribution and replay frequency, which also determine how much autotuning effort is justified.

Graph compilation applies supported rewrites and hardware mappings across captured regions. Its scope depends on the transformations it implements and the numerical contract it must preserve. Larger changes to model execution, such as proposing several tokens before verification or activating only selected experts, introduce additional algorithmic choices. Their benefits and correctness conditions must be understood before a compiler can implement them effectively.

Self-Check: Question
  1. Arrange the following stages of the deep learning graph compilation pipeline in their standard logical order of execution:
  1. Operator Fusion Pass (identifying fusible subgraphs to eliminate intermediate HBM round-trips)
  2. Graph Capture (tracing Python code to construct a Directed Acyclic Graph of tensor operations)
  3. Kernel Selection and Code Generation (generating and autotuning backend Triton/CUDA machine code)
  4. Memory Planning Pass (analyzing tensor lifetimes and scheduling physical buffer reuse)
  5. Graph-Level Optimization (applying algebraic simplifications and constant folding)

  1. How does TorchDynamo in PyTorch 2.0 capture computation graphs from standard Python code, and what is the systems impact when it encounters an unsupported dynamic construct (such as data-dependent Python control flow)?

    1. TorchDynamo intercepts CPython frame evaluation at the bytecode level (via PEP 523); when encountering untraceable Python constructs, it inserts a Graph Break that splits the trace into separate subgraphs, preventing fusion across the break boundary
    2. TorchDynamo compiles Python source text into static C++ before execution; when encountering dynamic control flow, it aborts the process with a fatal runtime exception
    3. TorchDynamo executes an abstract syntax tree rewriter that replaces all Python loops with CUDA thread blocks, ignoring data-dependent conditions
    4. TorchDynamo runs exclusively ahead-of-time, forcing all dynamic variables to fixed constants during model export

  2. Assume compilation removes 80% of launch overhead and reduces element-wise time by 70%, leaving GEMM time unchanged. Calculate the new time and throughput, and identify the next interval to inspect.

  3. In torch.compile, the compilation mode that expands the search over supported kernel implementations, trading additional build work for potential warmed-execution gains, is called ____.

  4. How does XLA optimization within a selected compiled region differ from eager execution boundaries, and what specialization trade-off must a deployment account for?

    1. XLA generates Python bytecode on every forward pass, trading compilation speed for execution latency
    2. XLA optimizes across a selected compiled region and can insert GSPMD communication; new signatures can require specialization, while cached signatures can reuse executables
    3. XLA executes only on CPU hosts, whereas TorchDynamo compiles directly to TPU hardware
    4. XLA requires dynamic shapes and cannot optimize static tensor dimensions

9.11 Algorithmic Performance Transformations

Fusion, precision engineering, and graph compilation optimize the same dense computation by moving fewer bytes, using narrower formats, or letting compilers reorganize kernels. Algorithmic transformations change the work itself. Speculative decoding attacks the memory-bound decode loop by trying to accept multiple tokens per target-model pass, while MoE attacks dense-model cost by activating only the experts a token needs. Both techniques can improve the iron-law budget, but both can also create new bottlenecks if their extra control logic, communication, or imbalance exceeds the work they remove.

9.11.1 Speculative decoding

Verifying K draft tokens in one pass moves decode up the slope toward the ridge.

Leviathan, Yaniv, Matan Kalman, and Yossi Matias. 2023. “Fast Inference from Transformers via Speculative Decoding.” International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), 19274–86.
Chen, C., S. Borgeaud, G. Irving, J.-B. Lespiau, L. Sifre, and J. Jumper. 2023. “Accelerating Large Language Model Decoding with Speculative Sampling.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2302.01318.

Speculative decoding is a latency optimization that breaks the sequential bottleneck of autoregressive generation by using a smaller draft model to predict multiple tokens, which are then verified in parallel by the target model (Leviathan et al. 2023; Chen et al. 2023). The performance-engineering relevance is arithmetic intensity: a single target-model forward pass can process \(\SpeculativeDraftLength\) candidate tokens for roughly the same weight-streaming cost as one token, the same reuse that batching buys in equation 2 but drawn from one request’s future tokens, shifting the decode operating point from the memory-bound slope toward the roofline ridge.

Figure 9 aligns the execution timelines of standard and speculative generation along a shared time axis. Where standard decoding uses four separate target-model passes to generate four tokens, the smaller draft model proposes tokens \(\DraftedToken_1\) through \(\DraftedToken_4\) sequentially, and the target evaluates those positions in a single forward pass. In the illustrated round, three tokens (\(\DraftedToken_1\)–\(\DraftedToken_3\)) are accepted, while the first rejected token (\(\DraftedToken_4\)) is replaced by \(\ResampledReplacementToken\) via residual sampling. The shorter timeline assumes that drafting, verification, and correction together cost less than the target passes they replace.

The transformation is useful only when the draft model is accurate enough and cheap enough. If too many draft tokens are rejected, the target model still loads its weights but accepts little useful work. If the draft model is too expensive, its own sequential passes consume the time that one-pass verification saved. Batching changes the calculation as well: at large batch sizes the decode phase is already closer to compute-bound, so the marginal benefit of speculation shrinks. This chapter uses speculative decoding to show how an algorithm can alter the performance equation; Speculative decoding as a serving policy treats it as a serving policy with admission control and service level agreement (SLA) consequences.

Algorithm 1 makes the acceptance test precise: after the single target-model pass, each drafted token is accepted with probability \(\min(1, \TargetModelDistribution/\DraftModelDistribution)\), and a rejection resamples from the normalized positive residual \((\TargetModelDistribution- \DraftModelDistribution)_+\), so the emitted sequence is distributed exactly as the target model would have produced unaided. That correctness guarantee is what lets speculative decoding trade draft-model compute for latency without shifting the output distribution.

\(p_\theta(\cdot \mid \cdot)\)

The payoff can be stated in one quantity. Under a simplified model in which each drafted position is accepted independently with probability \(\DraftTokenAcceptanceProbability\), a round emits its accepted prefix plus one target-sampled token (the correction at the first rejection, or the extra token after full acceptance). Equation 3 gives the expected output of one verification round with \(\SpeculativeDraftLength\) drafted tokens:

\[ \mathbb{E}[\RoundEmittedTokenCount] = \sum_{\DraftPositionIndex=0}^{\SpeculativeDraftLength} \DraftTokenAcceptanceProbability^{\,\DraftPositionIndex} = \frac{1-\DraftTokenAcceptanceProbability^{\SpeculativeDraftLength+1}}{1-\DraftTokenAcceptanceProbability} \tag{3}\]

With \(\SpeculativeDraftLength=\) 5, an acceptance probability of 0.78 yields about 3.5 tokens per target pass, while an acceptance probability of 0.5 yields only about 2. The target model streams its weights once per round in both cases, so the amortized weight traffic per emitted token differs by nearly a factor of two before draft cost is even counted.

Speculative decoding pays off when the complete draft-and-verify round costs less per emitted token than ordinary target decoding. Rejections shorten the accepted prefix, but acceptance rate alone does not determine that cost: drafting, verification, cache management, and sampling all contribute. The serving system must therefore tune draft length and draft-model choice against measured latency and throughput under the same output-distribution requirement.

9.11.2 Mixture of experts

A mixture-of-experts architecture can increase total parameter capacity without activating every feed-forward parameter for every token (Fedus et al. 2022; Lepikhin et al. 2021). Sparse activation changes the relation between total parameters and per-token arithmetic, but it does not guarantee higher quality or lower latency than a dense model at a fixed resource budget. The comparison must hold model quality, batch and sequence distributions, hardware topology, and service objectives constant.

Fedus, William, Barret Zoph, and Noam Shazeer. 2022. “Switch Transformers: Scaling to Trillion Parameter Models with Simple and Efficient Sparsity.” Journal of Machine Learning Research 23 (120): 1–39.
Lepikhin, Dmitry, HyoukJoong Lee, Yuanzhong Xu, Dehao Chen, Orhan Firat, Yanping Huang, Maxim Krikun, Noam Shazeer, and Zhifeng Chen. 2021. “GShard: Scaling Giant Models with Conditional Computation and Automatic Sharding.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

A standard MoE transformer layer replaces the feed-forward network with multiple parallel “expert” FFNs and a lightweight Router (also called a gating network) that selects which experts process each token. That architecture shifts the performance problem from dense matrix throughput to routing, memory residency, and load balance. When experts are distributed across GPUs, expert parallelism places different experts on different devices, and an AllToAll exchange moves each token’s hidden state to its assigned expert before returning the result. The active-parameter savings are valuable only if that routing traffic and any expert imbalance remain smaller than the dense compute they replace.

A per-token estimate separates arithmetic savings from routing costs, and which saving counts depends on the regime. Because the recurring 70B model is dense, the estimate uses Mixtral 8x7B (Jiang et al. 2024), which routes each token to 2 of 8 experts, each holding about 176 million parameters per layer. In batched serving and prefill, where execution approaches the compute ceiling, the saving is arithmetic. Compared with evaluating every expert, routing skips about 2.1 GFLOP per token per layer, equivalent to 2.1 μs at H100 FP16 peak compute. The modeled dispatch-and-combine payload is 32.8 KB in FP16, and dividing by peak NVLink bandwidth gives 73 ns, roughly 29× less than the skipped compute. Bytes are therefore not the binding routing cost; the fixed cost of each exchange is. With the scenario’s assumed two-launch cost of 10 μs per layer, a layer repays its routing overhead only when it routes about 5 or more tokens at once, which batched serving and prefill supply easily. In batch-one decode, below the ridge, the saving is bytes instead. At FP16 each parameter costs two FLOPs and occupies two bytes, so the unselected experts’ weights amount to about as many gigabytes per layer as the skipped work has GFLOPs, and streaming that many bytes at HBM bandwidth takes far longer than the exchange’s microseconds of launch. Achieved AllToAll bandwidth, topology, and expert imbalance move both crossovers, and neither comparison sets MoE against a dense model of matched quality.

Jiang, A. Q., A. Sablayrolles, A. Roux, A. Mensch, B. Savary, C. Bamford, D. S. Chaplot, et al. 2024. “Mixtral of Experts.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2401.04088.

For performance engineering, the local question is whether active-parameter savings exceed the costs of routing and communication over the workload envelope. A complete evaluation documents the total expert pool, active experts per token, expert capacity limits, token routing skew, dispatch and combine communication volume, AllToAll exchange duration, dropped or rerouted tokens, and the achieved output quality alongside latency. Sparse activation improves the iron-law budget only when inactive experts stay off the critical path and routing does not create a larger communication or straggler term.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Why does speculative decoding accelerate autoregressive generation on modern GPUs without altering the target model’s output probability distribution?

    1. It forces the draft model to use the exact same weights as the target model through FP8 weight quantizing
    2. It skips attention computation for all tokens where the draft model confidence exceeds 50%
    3. It accepts candidate draft tokens with probability \(\min(1, p_\theta / q_\phi)\) and resamples rejected tokens from the positive residual \((p_\theta - q_\phi)_+\), ensuring strict mathematical equivalence while verifying \(K\) tokens in a single parallel target pass to amortize weight-streaming bandwidth
    4. It runs the draft model and target model on separate GPUs connected via PCIe, bypassing HBM bandwidth limits entirely

  2. Explain why speculative decoding provides substantial speedup at batch size 1 but exhibits diminishing or even negative returns as serving batch size increases (e.g. batch size 64).

  3. True or False: In a Mixture of Experts (MoE) architecture, scaling the number of experts from 8 to 256 is a cost-free capacity scaling knob because each token only activates top-\(k\) experts during inference.

  4. In a speculative decoding deployment, draft generation takes 4 ms and target verification takes 32 ms. Measurements show a mean of 3.5 emitted tokens per round, including correction or bonus tokens. What is the amortized time per emitted token and speedup over a 32 ms autoregressive baseline?

    1. Effective ITL is 36.0 ms/token, representing a \(0.89\times\) slowdown due to draft model overhead
    2. Effective ITL is 7.2 ms/token, representing a \(4.4\times\) speedup
    3. Effective ITL is 20.5 ms/token, representing a \(1.56\times\) speedup
    4. Approximately 10.3 ms per emitted token and a 3.1-fold amortized speedup

9.12 Overlap on the Device: Streams, Contention, and Tensor-Parallel Collectives

The recurring 70B replica spans eight GPUs, so every decode step contains collectives. In a tensor-parallel inference pass or a multi-GPU training step, an accelerator can finish local computation and then wait for an AllReduce to combine activations or gradients. Overlap shortens that exposed wait by running communication alongside independent computation on the same device. The collective algorithms and the layer-by-layer gradient schedules that create the opportunity belong to the communication layer; this section asks the device-level question of how much communication remains on the critical path once dependencies, stream ordering, and contention for the same SMs and memory bandwidth are accounted for.

↩︎ Layer-by-layer gradient overlap and bucket fusion are established in Communication-Computation Overlap.

Consider a 70B model with tensor parallelism across 8 H100 GPUs, using two AllReduce operations per layer in the forward pass, one after attention and one after the FFN. Approximate per-rank collective traffic as \(2 \times \HiddenDimension\times \BatchSize\times 2\) bytes at FP16 (where \(\HiddenDimension = 8192\) for Llama 2 70B and \(\BatchSize\) is batch size). At batch size 1, this gives \(2 \times 8192 \times 1 \times 2 =\) 32.8 KB per AllReduce. Dividing by 450 GB/s of per-direction NVLink bandwidth yields an ideal transfer term of 72.8 ns. The assumed launch overhead of 5 μs is roughly 70× larger. At batch size 1, the collective is therefore dominated by fixed software and protocol cost rather than bandwidth, and a faster link would barely shorten it. Peak link bandwidth alone poorly predicts small-message collective latency; launch, protocol, and synchronization costs must be measured.

At batch size 64, the modeled traffic per AllReduce grows to \(2 \times 8192 \times 64 \times 2 =\) 2.1 MB, giving a bandwidth-only term of 4.7 μs. Adding the assumed launch overhead gives 9.7 μs before other collective costs. The scenario estimates local execution from weight streaming: each GPU reads its 219 MB share of a layer’s FP16 weights in 65 μs at peak HBM bandwidth, then assigns half that time, 33 μs, to each interval between AllReduces. This modeled interval is about 3.4× the communication estimate, so at batch 64 a window exists in duration. Whether the schedule can use it is a dependency question.

The duration comparison gives an upper bound on the overlap opportunity. A consumer cannot use an activation before its reduction completes, so a tensor-parallel schedule also needs independent work or a legal chunking scheme to fill that interval. Total compute time alone does not prove that the communication can be hidden. Training gradient synchronization supplies the contrasting case, for two reasons. Its payload is the whole gradient tensor rather than one activation vector, so the collective is bandwidth-bound and the overlap question becomes quantitative. Its dependencies are also looser, because completed gradient buckets can communicate while later backward kernels compute other gradients. The next calculation therefore switches from activation exchanges to ring AllReduce over the gradient tensor.

9.12.1 Quantifying the overlap opportunity

The potential benefit of communication-computation overlap depends on the relative magnitudes of communication and computation time, which vary dramatically across system configurations.

Consider an 8-billion-parameter model trained on 8 H100 GPUs within a single node connected by NVLink at 900 GB/s aggregate bidirectional bandwidth (450 GB/s per direction). Assume data-parallel replicas with optimizer state offloaded so that each device can hold its weights, gradients, and activation working set. The timing window below includes forward, backward, and gradient synchronization only; optimizer updates and offload transfers require additional accounting. The gradient tensor contains 16.1 GB of FP16 values. Per the ring AllReduce analysis in Ring AllReduce, synchronization transfers \(2 \times (\DeviceCount-1)/\DeviceCount=\) 1.75× the gradient volume, taking approximately 62.5 ms at NVLink bandwidth. The forward pass computation takes 83.1 ms and the backward pass computation (at 40 percent MFU) takes approximately 166.3 ms.

Without overlap, the modeled timing window requires 311.9 ms (83.1 ms + 166.3 ms + 62.5 ms). Under ideal overlap, with gradient buckets ready early enough and no resource interference, the AllReduce is fully hidden behind the backward pass, reducing that window to 249.4 ms (83.1 ms + 166.3 ms), a 1.25× improvement. Sufficient independent backward work provides the time in which communication can be hidden. The separate normalized scenario in figure 10 holds computation at 100 units while increasing assumed communication cost as GPU count rises from 8 to 1,024. Its hidden fraction falls from roughly 90 percent to roughly 18 percent, and the exposed communication makes the modeled window roughly five times longer. These are constructed operating points, and the payload need not grow for the window to close. Slower effective communication, whether from topology, contention, or a lower-bandwidth link such as PCIe Gen5 at 64 GB/s per direction instead of NVLink at 450 GB/s, can exhaust the overlap window even when the payload is unchanged.

9.12.2 CUDA streams and asynchronous execution

The mechanism enabling overlap on NVIDIA GPUs is CUDA streams. A CUDA stream is an ordered sequence of GPU operations (kernel launches, memory copies, NCCL collectives) that execute sequentially within the stream but can execute concurrently with operations in other streams. The application maintains two distinct streams: a compute stream for matrix multiplications and element-wise kernels, and a communication stream for NCCL operations. The workflow proceeds by launching a compute kernel on the first stream and immediately triggering an asynchronous communication call on the second:

compute_stream = torch.cuda.Stream()
comm_stream = torch.cuda.Stream()
producer_stream = torch.cuda.current_stream()
# Inputs are ready on producer_stream; gradients do not alias A or B.
compute_stream.wait_stream(producer_stream)
comm_stream.wait_stream(producer_stream)
A.record_stream(compute_stream)
B.record_stream(compute_stream)
gradients.record_stream(comm_stream)

with torch.cuda.stream(compute_stream):
    output = torch.matmul(A, B)  # Non-blocking compute

with torch.cuda.stream(comm_stream):
    work = dist.all_reduce(gradients, async_op=True)
    work.wait()  # Establish completion on the current communication stream

torch.cuda.synchronize()  # Wait for both to complete

Separate streams make concurrent execution possible, but do not reserve independent hardware resources. A GEMM and an NCCL operation can compete for SM execution capacity, memory bandwidth, and cache while the interconnect carries data. Their allocation across SMs depends on the kernels, collective implementation, and launch configuration. A fixed count of communication blocks therefore does not imply an equal fractional loss of compute throughput. Engineers must evaluate any slowdown in concurrent computation against the communication time removed from the critical path; temporal overlap on a timeline alone does not establish a net speedup.

Effective overlap requires both timely submission and correct dependencies. For data-parallel training, DDP’s bucketed gradient reduction already provides both, launching each ready bucket’s AllReduce in a consistent order across ranks while later backward kernels run. Code that schedules collectives on its own streams must provide the same guarantees explicitly.

Custom side streams require explicit reasoning about readiness and storage lifetime. PyTorch enqueues operations on the current stream, which is the default stream unless another has been selected. A producer-consumer dependency can be enforced with event.record() / event.wait() or an appropriate stream wait; without that ordering, a consumer may read an unfinished tensor. For copies, non_blocking=True can avoid a host-side wait where supported, but does not by itself guarantee device-side overlap. Memory type, transfer direction, and dependencies still matter. An explicit record_event() can mark producer completion, while allocator stream tracking or equivalent lifetime management prevents storage reuse before side-stream consumers finish. Readiness and lifetime are separate obligations, and both must hold before interpreting the timing as a valid optimization.

Fusion, precision, compilation, speculative decoding, MoE, and communication overlap each predict a different change in the execution trace. A shorter kernel, fewer bytes, or overlapping streams counts as an improvement only if the end-to-end measurement confirms useful work completed sooner under the same constraints. The optimization playbook combines these mechanisms into experiments ordered by the bottleneck that the current trace exposes.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Under the exposed-time formulation of communication-computation overlap (\(T_{\text{step}} = \max(T_{\text{compute}}, T_{\text{comm}}) + T_{\text{sync}}\)), what physical condition is required to achieve 100% communication hiding (zero exposed communication time)?

    1. \(T_{\text{comm}} \leq T_{\text{compute}}\), a necessary duration condition; full hiding additionally requires timely independent work and tolerable resource interference
    2. \(T_{\text{comm}} = 0\), requiring all distributed GPUs to share a single physical HBM memory controller
    3. \(T_{\text{compute}} = 0\), requiring the GPU to execute collective operations without running backward pass gradients
    4. \(T_{\text{comm}} \ge 2 \times T_{\text{compute}}\), ensuring communication has priority over SM scheduling

  2. Explain how concurrent GEMM and NCCL streams can contend for an accelerator’s SMs and memory bandwidth. Under what measured condition is a reduction in local compute throughput worthwhile?

  3. In PyTorch DistributedDataParallel (DDP), arrange the following events in the exact sequence that implements asynchronous gradient communication overlap during training:

  1. Main compute stream continues backward pass execution on earlier layers concurrently with network transfer
  2. Autograd backward pass computes parameter gradients for a layer
  3. Event barrier synchronizes the communication stream with the compute stream before optimizer step
  4. Autograd backward hook triggers asynchronous AllReduce on the parameter bucket in a dedicated NCCL stream
  1. True or False: If a side-stream collective reads a tensor before its producing kernel completes, missing producer-consumer ordering can cause a data race even though each stream executes its own operations in order.

9.13 Optimization Playbook for Large Language Models

Consider an unoptimized 70-billion parameter PyTorch model deployed to serve interactive requests under a strict per-token latency objective. The optimization sequence begins with baseline end-to-end measurement, followed by roofline classification to isolate the primary bottleneck. A memory-bound decode path and a launch-bound path require different initial interventions. The playbook therefore orders experiments by the exposed time each can remove, with a new measurement after every change; the worked deployment below illustrates one such sequence.

9.13.1 The diagnostic sequence

Optimization begins by measuring the whole workload before touching individual kernels. End-to-end throughput, an Nsight Systems trace, and MFU from section 9.7 identify where execution falls short of its target. Roofline placement then estimates the compute and bandwidth limits for dominant kernels, while counters and dependency traces test which cost is exposed. For memory-bound, compute-bound, and communication-bound workloads, table 12 maps the diagnosis to candidate interventions. The engineer selects an intervention targeting the measured limiter, applies it, and reprofiles before attempting subsequent optimizations; classifying a system by generic workload category without trace evidence risks optimizing an unexposed cost.

The order matters. The triage of figure 1 screens data-loading stalls, host preprocessing, and communication overhead before it classifies kernel regimes, because a kernel intervention cannot help an accelerator that is waiting for work. The diagnostic sequence then closes by applying and verifying the selected intervention: implement the highest-impact optimization, reprofile, confirm the improvement, and restart from roofline classification with the new profile, since the bottleneck may have shifted.

9.13.2 Combining techniques

Production systems combine techniques because no single intervention covers every term in the iron law. An optimized language-model kernel path may combine tiled attention, lower-precision weight or cache representations, graph compilation, and replay. The durable question is which intervention changes measured bytes, operations, or launch time at the active bottleneck; named frameworks are implementations of those mechanisms rather than the organizing principle.

The serving layer adds admission, cache-state, routing, batching, and SLO controls. This chapter retains only their physical inputs: how batch and sequence shape change arithmetic intensity, how speculative verification changes exposed work, and how precision or kernel structure changes memory capacity and execution time. The distinction prevents a favorable local kernel result from being mistaken for a serving-policy result at a named offered load.

The speedups from these techniques are not additive; they interact in ways that demand careful sequencing. For instance, INT4 reduces the weight payload relative to FP16 by 4\(\times\) before metadata, while leaving other traffic unchanged. That reduction may shift weight-dominated execution toward a compute limit. Further cache compression could still help capacity or attention traffic, but its latency benefit must be measured at the new operating point. The profile-optimize-verify loop is essential because each intervention changes which cost remains exposed.

The interaction between optimizations creates a dependency graph that the performance engineer must navigate. Some combinations are synergistic: tiled attention removes intermediates, and lower-precision cache storage frees memory for larger batches. Other combinations overlap: a compiler mode may already use graph capture for eligible regions, so an additional capture layer may save little while complicating memory management. Still other combinations conflict: speculative decoding often benefits low-batch, memory-bound decode, whereas larger batches consume the compute headroom that makes verification inexpensive. Only an end-to-end measurement under the contract in table 9 can classify a combination as synergistic, redundant, or conflicting for the target workload.

A practical heuristic is to try a low-cost intervention that addresses the measured bottleneck before adding new algorithmic machinery. In the batch-one 70B scenario that follows, precision comes first because the weight-reading GEMM interval is the largest item in the budget. When stable-shape execution instead exposes launch overhead, torch.compile is a candidate; when weight capacity or traffic dominates, INT4 or FP8 weights may address the limit. Calibration prepares the representation, while held-out evaluation establishes whether its output quality is acceptable. FlashAttention-family kernels target attention traffic when they support the model’s attention variant and precision. INT8 cache entries halve the FP16 cache payload before metadata, potentially admitting larger batches. Speculative decoding is another candidate when verification can use spare compute, with added costs for drafting and serving integration. Implementation effort varies with the existing stack, so this sequence is a hypothesis to test rather than a universal ranking.

This candidate sequence favors lower implementation cost when multiple interventions address the same bottleneck. A measured capacity or bandwidth limit takes precedence over that convenience, and reprofiling can change the order or eliminate a later step entirely.

Checkpoint 9.3: Optimization strategy

Test the ability to design an optimization plan:

9.13.3 Optimization of a 70B LLM serving pipeline

Consider a 70B-parameter model on a node of eight H100 GPUs connected by NVLink. This constructed example illustrates budget accounting, not benchmark results. The scenario assumes batch size one, a fixed context length, and a decode step that emits one token. All intervals are exposed, nonoverlapping contributions on the same request critical path. The illustrative objective is a decode step below 50 ms; prefill latency, quality, and loaded throughput require separate measurements before deployment.

Baseline measurement

The deployment uses FP16 weights and tensor parallelism across 8 GPUs. Its weights occupy 140 GB, or approximately 17.5 GB per GPU. The maximum batch size also depends on cache capacity and serving policy; it cannot be inferred from weight storage alone.

The assumed baseline decode budget is 85 ms, about 16× the bandwidth-only step of 5.2 ms computed in notebook 9.1. That gap, not the weight-streaming floor, is what eager dispatch, unfused kernels, and exposed synchronization must explain; the intervals below apportion the whole budget among those causes. The budget assumes a runtime that already hides part of host submission behind running kernels, so its exposed launch and runtime interval of 10.2 ms sits below the 12–24 ms that one-kernel-at-a-time submission would cost per forward pass (section 9.8.3). Table 13 keeps every round on that same absolute-time boundary. In an actual Nsight Systems trace, overlapping work must not be counted twice when constructing this budget.

Round 1: Precision engineering

The first experiment targets weight-read traffic. INT4 quantization reduces the raw weight representation relative to FP16, although scale metadata, dequantization, and kernel choice affect runtime. Assume that quality validation succeeds and that exposed GEMM time falls by 45 percent, with all other intervals unchanged. The resulting step takes 69.7 ms, still above the objective. A large local reduction has removed only the corresponding fraction of the baseline’s GEMM interval.

INT8 KV cache compression is a separate candidate. The capacity calculation in section 9.9.3 estimates its storage benefit, but that benefit does not establish an attention speedup. Applying it requires another quality check and a new profile; the first-round column grants it no unmeasured latency benefit.

Round 2: Fusion, fused decode attention, and graph replay

The second experiment targets exposed attention, element-wise work, and launch gaps. Assume a validated fused decode-attention kernel, the single-pass kernel over the KV cache described in section 9.5.2 rather than prefill-style tiling, reduces the attention interval by 25 percent, fusion reduces element-wise work to 3.4 ms, and graph replay reduces launch and runtime overhead to 2 ms. These are scenario assumptions, not guaranteed effects of any kernel library or of torch.compile. In practice, each intervention would be tested separately before the combined configuration is retained.

The total becomes 49.6 ms, meeting the illustrative batch-one decode objective while still sitting about 9× above the bandwidth-only floor, so the objective is met with most of the original gap still in place. Communication remains 10.2 ms, unchanged in absolute time even though its share of the shorter step grows. Spread across the 160 AllReduces of an 80-layer step, that interval implies about 64 μs of exposed time per collective, against roughly 5 μs of launch and transfer in the estimate of section 9.12, or about 0.8 ms per step. That estimate covered only launch and transfer, leaving out the protocol and synchronization costs it flagged for measurement; in this constructed budget, those unmeasured costs account for the difference. A trace that attributed them would make communication the next experiment. Communication’s larger share of the step reflects the shorter total, not slower collectives. This result says nothing yet about time to first token or throughput under concurrent arrivals.

Round 3: Speculative decoding

Speculation offers a different way to amortize target-model execution. Assume a draft model proposes \(\SpeculativeDraftLength=\) 5 tokens in 4 ms, target verification takes the round-two decode duration, and each candidate is independently accepted with probability \(\DraftTokenAcceptanceProbability=\) 0.78. Verification cost and acceptance are workload assumptions that must be measured for a real draft/target pair.

Applying equation 3, the scenario emits 3.52 tokens per round on average. Dividing draft-plus-verification time by that expectation gives 15.22 ms per emitted token, an amortized 3.26-fold improvement over the round-two duration. Tokens arrive in blocks; this average does not establish the longest visible pause or an inter-token latency percentile.

The gain belongs to batch one. Larger batches consume the spare compute that made verification cheap, and locating the batch size at which speculation stops paying requires measuring the same budget at several batch sizes, the measurement the speculative-decoding pitfall in section 9.14 calls for.

Case study synthesis

The budget exposes bottleneck displacement without attributing a system improvement to an isolated kernel speedup. Precision changes one interval; fusion and replay change others; speculation changes the amount of output per round. Their benefits must be composed on a shared accounting boundary, with quality preserved.

This sequence produces candidate execution configurations for the serving fleet. To accept one for production, the engineer must replace the illustrative intervals with measurements, preserve the request-length distribution and offered load, and verify latency and completed throughput together. Memory capacity can permit a larger batch, but neither that capacity nor a faster batch-one path demonstrates that the service meets every objective simultaneously.

Self-Check: Question

  1. In the constructed batch-one 70B decode budget, order the three optimization experiments: (1) Operator fusion and graph replay, (2) Weight precision reduction, (3) Speculative decoding.

  2. Why does the constructed batch-one 70B decode example test INT4 weights before speculative decoding or operator fusion?

    1. Because the GPU driver disables CUDA Graphs and compiler optimizations unless weights are formatted in INT4
    2. The assumed profile identifies weight traffic as the first target; reducing its exposed GEMM interval provides a consistent baseline for subsequent experiments
    3. Because speculative decoding cannot mathematically operate on FP16 models
    4. Because operator fusion only works on models with fewer than 10 billion parameters

  3. Why must a serving controller measure the batch-size crossover for speculative decoding rather than infer a universal cutoff from a batch-one decode budget?

  4. An 85 ms decode step has exposed intervals of 34 ms GEMM, 20.4 ms attention, 10.2 ms element-wise work, 10.2 ms AllReduce, and 10.2 ms launch/runtime overhead. Reducing only GEMM time by 45% leaves 69.7 ms. Why does this still miss a 50 ms objective?

    1. The InfiniBand network cable was disconnected during the benchmark run
    2. The GPU ran out of power and throttled clock frequency to zero
    3. The unchanged non-GEMM intervals still total 51 ms, so reducing GEMM alone by the assumed amount cannot reach the objective
    4. INT4 quantization caused the model to produce infinite repetitive token loops

9.14 Fallacies and Pitfalls

An engineering team upgrades an inference cluster from A100 GPUs to H100 GPUs, expecting a 3\(\times\) latency reduction based on peak arithmetic specifications, only to find that small-batch decode accelerates merely by the ratio of the two memory bandwidths. The trap is pervasive: assuming that peak arithmetic throughput dictates inference latency when the workload is bound by memory bandwidth.

Fallacy: More FLOP/s means faster inference.

Weight-dominated, small-batch decode often has a memory-bandwidth limit. A GPU with 2\(\times\) the peak FLOP/s and unchanged memory bandwidth leaves that roofline bound unchanged. Actual inference performance also depends on achieved bandwidth, cache behavior, kernel efficiency, launch overhead, and communication. Equal advertised bandwidth therefore does not guarantee equal throughput, just as higher peak FLOP/s does not guarantee faster decode. Hardware comparisons must use the intended workload and its measured bottleneck.

Pitfall: Planning FP8 adoption as an automatic training-time halving.

On the chapter’s H100 example, FP8 offers twice the FP16 peak arithmetic rate and halves the payload of values converted from FP16. Those are opportunities, not a prediction that the full training step becomes twice as fast. Conversion and scaling add work, some tensors retain higher precision, and launch or communication costs may remain exposed. A communication-bound step can still benefit from shorter exposed computation, but its unchanged communication floor limits the gain. The trace must establish which intervals the precision change actually shortens.

Fallacy: The largest kernel is the whole performance problem.

The profiler detective in Example 9.1 illustrates this pitfall. Engineers naturally focus on the single largest kernel, which is often the GEMM in a transformer layer. When the GEMM is already near-optimal, however, the remaining performance budget is distributed across dozens of smaller operations: normalization, activation, attention scoring, KV cache management, and kernel launch overhead. Collectively, these “small” operations can consume more than half of total execution time. Graph compilation and systematic fusion address this long tail more effectively than further GEMM optimization.

Pitfall: Applying speculative decoding without considering batch dynamics.

Speculative decoding can reduce latency in a measured low-batch, memory-bound regime when the draft model is cheap, its proposals achieve a high acceptance rate, and the target has spare compute for batched verification. Verification is never free; its exposed cost depends on draft length, acceptance, batch and sequence distributions, and the target kernel’s operating point. As batching consumes compute headroom, the same configuration can cross from a gain to a loss, while variable accepted lengths introduce additional scheduler work. This chapter establishes how to measure that crossover for a fixed workload and machine; applying the resulting curve under changing offered load and service objectives is a matter of admission policy.

Fallacy: MoE expert count is a free scaling knob.

Increasing the number of experts in an MoE model can increase total parameters without proportionally increasing active per-token arithmetic, which resembles a free scaling knob. More experts still increase parameter memory and placement pressure, and a distributed placement exposes dispatch and combine communication. The realized cost depends on experts selected per token, token-to-expert skew, expert capacity, topology, and batching; no universal expert-count threshold determines when the design loses. The optimization is justified only when a matched-quality comparison shows that active-compute savings exceed routing, imbalance, and communication costs over the intended workload envelope.

Pitfall: Using graph compilers as a substitute for kernel analysis.

Graph compilers remain limited by their implemented transformations, cost models, and fusion heuristics. FlashAttention illustrates why an engineer must understand the algorithm and memory hierarchy even when a compiler generates the final kernels. Speculative decoding and MoE routing likewise introduce choices about verification, quality, and communication that ordinary fusion does not settle. Inspecting generated execution and evaluating those choices remain necessary; successful compilation alone does not establish that the workload is well optimized.

Fallacy: The lowest supported precision is always the best precision.

Aggressive quantization (INT4 weights, INT4 KV cache, FP8 activations) can degrade model quality in ways that are difficult to detect with standard benchmarks but visible to users. Perplexity on a held-out dataset may change by less than 1 percent, but the model may produce subtly worse responses for edge cases, rare languages, or complex reasoning tasks. The disciplined approach is targeted quantization: evaluating per-layer sensitivity under representative workloads and restricting lower bit-widths to robust tensors. KV caches, intermediate activations, endpoint layers, and attention logits all require empirical evaluation rather than a speculative sensitivity ranking. Calibration on a representative dataset, followed by evaluation on diverse quality benchmarks, is essential before deploying any quantized model to production.

Pitfall: Measuring throughput without measuring quality.

A model serving system that generates 200 tokens/second is not twice as good as one generating 100 tokens/second if the first system achieves that throughput by using INT4 quantization that degrades answer quality by 15 percent. Performance metrics must always be reported alongside quality metrics. The correct optimization target is the Pareto frontier of throughput vs. quality, not throughput alone.

Fallacy: A single profiling run is sufficient to characterize performance.

ML system performance can change over time. Thermal or power limits can reduce sustained clock speeds, changing allocation patterns can increase memory fragmentation, and cluster traffic can alter network contention. These effects are workload- and configuration-dependent rather than inevitable penalties of a fixed size. Cold-start behavior also differs from warmed execution. Reliable characterization measures the relevant phases separately and samples sustained operation often enough to capture variability that affects the service or training objective.

Pitfall: Optimizing for average case while ignoring tail latency.

A serving system might report mean inter-token latency of 30 ms and p99 inter-token latency of 500 ms over the same measurement window and request mix. The mean hides the slow intervals, while p99 describes a percentile rather than a worst-case bound. Traces should test whether those intervals arise from host pauses, allocation stalls, collective delays, or queueing. Preallocated buffers, graph replay, or scheduling changes help only when they address the observed cause. Measure the resulting latency distribution at the same offered load and verify that improving one request class does not make another miss its target.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A team upgrades their LLM serving cluster from NVIDIA A100 to H100 GPUs, expecting a \(3\times\) reduction in generation latency based on the H100’s \(3\times\) higher peak FP16 TFLOP/s rating. However, batch-1 autoregressive decode latency improves by only ~15%. What fundamental performance engineering fallacy explains this result?

    1. The fallacy that H100 GPUs cannot execute FP16 operations without INT4 emulation
    2. The fallacy that Python cannot run on Hopper-architecture accelerators
    3. The fallacy that PyTorch profiler traces disable GPU Tensor Cores during inference
    4. The fallacy that peak arithmetic alone predicts decode latency; low-intensity weight streaming has a bandwidth ceiling, and other exposed costs can limit the observed gain

  2. Explain why evaluating LLM serving performance purely on average inter-token latency can mask severe production failures, and describe two specific systems engineering techniques used to optimize P99 tail latency.

  3. True or False: Upgrading a distributed training workload from FP16 to FP8 precision automatically halves total step time across any multi-node GPU cluster.

  4. Why does successful graph compilation not establish that a workload has exploited algorithmic choices such as tiled online attention or draft-and-verify speculative decoding?

    1. Graph compilers apply implemented transformations and supported kernels; compilation alone does not settle algorithm selection, draft-verification correctness, or performance
    2. Graph compilers are restricted by hardware law to only optimize CPU assembly code
    3. Graph compilers only support models with fewer than 100,000 parameters
    4. Graph compilers disable shared memory allocations on NVIDIA GPUs

9.15 Summary

Performance engineering closes the gap between a model’s theoretical efficiency and its hardware implementation by systematically optimizing the terms of the single-machine iron law (equation 1), which governs total execution time \(\Time\) by data movement (\(\DataVolume / \Bandwidth\)), compute execution (\(\Operations/ (\PeakRate \cdot \EfficiencyHw)\)), and the overhead term (\(\Latency\)). On modern accelerators like the NVIDIA H100, where the FP16 ridge point of the roofline model sits at 295 FLOP/byte, small-batch autoregressive decode is often bound by the memory wall (\(\DataVolume / \Bandwidth\)).

Fusion and precision act on data movement (\(\DataVolume/\Bandwidth\)) through different mechanisms. Operator fusion avoids intermediate HBM transfers, while FlashAttention tiles attention into SRAM without materializing the quadratic \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength^2)\) score and probability matrices. Its persistent workspace grows as \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength)\), but actual HBM traffic includes tile reloads. The simplified 8K sequence example gives a 65× traffic ratio under its idealized accounting assumptions. Lower-precision weights and cache state reduce payload size \(\DataVolume\) and can expand feasible batch capacity.

The two parts of the overhead term (\(\Latency\)) call for different interventions. For host dispatch, graph compilers fuse eligible operations and plan memory, while CUDA Graphs amortize host submission for stable captured regions. Exposed communication, which the Fleet Law separates as \(\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)\), shrinks through communication-computation overlap only when independent work is ready and resource interference does not erase the gain.

Algorithmic transformations change the work itself, and which term they shrink depends on the regime. Speculative decoding obtains multiple emitted tokens from one target-model verification pass, amortizing one weight stream over several tokens and so shrinking the data term per emitted token in memory-bound decode. Mixture-of-experts routing limits active per-token FLOPs \(\Operations\) while expanding parameter capacity, shrinking the compute term in batched execution and the weight bytes read in batch-one decode. Their benefit depends on whether draft work, routing, communication, and imbalance cost less than the work they remove.

The case study in section 9.13.3 combines these mechanisms as a sequence of hypotheses, each requiring a new profile. Precision savings can enable larger batches, which change arithmetic intensity and the opportunity for speculation. The useful result is a measured execution envelope at a stated workload and quality level; separate low-batch latency and batched-throughput measurements do not establish simultaneous service-objective compliance. That envelope, a replica’s measured capacity at a stated workload mix and latency target, is also the quantity the fleet orchestrator uses to size and place serving replicas.

↩︎ The serving replica’s measured capacity as the fleet orchestrator’s unit of placement is established in Serving Capacity in the Shared Fleet.

In the Fleet Law’s terms (equation 1.3), the unsharded local-execution reference \(\UnshardedLocalExecutionTime\) includes data movement, computation, and host dispatch. Fusion, precision, compilation, and algorithmic change shorten the distributed local-execution term \(\UnshardedLocalExecutionTime/\DeviceCount\), while communication-computation overlap enlarges \(\OverlappedTime\). The chapter therefore spends its effort on the Compute (\(\OperationalTaxonomyDimension_1\)) axis and its co-design boundary with Communication, and those gains reach the fleet only when the Communication and Coordination terms do not absorb them.

Key Takeaways: Match the software to the silicon
  • Bytes often bind small-batch decode: The chapter’s weight-streaming example sits below the FP16 ridge point of 295 FLOP/byte. Larger batches and prefill can change that regime, so the active workload determines whether reducing HBM traffic or improving compute execution helps.
  • Locality and replay address different costs: Operator fusion and FlashAttention avoid intermediate transfers; CUDA Graphs reduce repeated host submission. The 8K attention example gives a 65× ratio under simplified traffic assumptions, while actual traffic and speedup depend on the implementation.
  • Precision buys bandwidth with risk: FP8, INT8, and INT4 increase effective bandwidth and batch capacity only when outliers, scale factors, and quality checks are managed. Quantization is a systems contract between numerical format, kernel implementation, serving memory, and acceptable model behavior.
  • Algorithms change the workload’s operating point: Speculative decoding and mixture-of-experts change the work performed per emitted token, but add draft, routing, AllToAll, and load-balancing costs. Savings count only when useful work completes sooner under the same quality and service constraints.
  • Reprofiling tests whether gains survive: Bottleneck displacement makes each optimization a new experiment. Compare the same workload, timing boundary, quality criterion, and offered load before and after a change; kernel gains become fleet gains only when the complete execution path improves.

Hardware is sold by its theoretical peak (\(\PeakRate\)) but paid for by the sustained throughput it achieves under memory bandwidth (\(\Bandwidth\)) and software overhead (\(\Latency\)) constraints. The chapter turned the autoregressive bottleneck (principle 15) from a statement about weight streaming into a budget, in which batch-one decode has a bandwidth-only floor and the distance between a measured step and that floor is what dispatch, unfused kernels, and exposed communication must explain. The case study also showed the displacement of overhead (principle 13) inside a single replica, since shortening the compute intervals left the AllReduce time unchanged and enlarged its share of the step.

The working rule is to place a step against its floor before choosing a lever, then to measure again after each change, because the next limiter is often the cost that the last optimization left untouched. The hardware sets the ceiling; that loop of measurement and correction determines how closely production software approaches it.

What’s Next: From execution limits to serving policy
The measured execution envelope gives a serving controller the cost and capacity of each replica it can deploy. Serving runs for a model version’s whole operational lifetime, so that per-replica cost recurs with every request, and at high traffic it can outgrow the one-time cost of training (principle 16). The controller still must decide which requests to admit, where to route them, how much cache state to retain, and when to change batch size or add replicas as offered load varies. Those decisions determine whether gains from fusion, precision, compilation, and speculation become useful fleet throughput while preserving service objectives.

↪ Fleet-scale serving policies, cache retention, and SLO protection are developed in Inference at Scale.

    1. Because Tensor Cores are physically disabled during inference workloads
    2. Low arithmetic intensity makes bandwidth the tighter ideal ceiling; measurements must still identify the exposed cost before choosing fusion, precision, or launch-overhead reduction
    3. Because memory bandwidth is free and unlimited in modern cloud data centers
    4. Because compiler graph optimizations only work on memory access instructions
