9.16 Self-Check Answers

Self-Check: Answer

  1. A training step’s wall-clock time is decomposed via the iron law of ML performance (\(T = D_{\text{vol}}/\text{BW} + O/(R_{\text{peak}} \cdot \eta_{\text{hw}}) + L_{\text{lat}}\)) into 60% memory traffic (\(D_{\text{vol}}/\text{BW}\)), 30% compute execution (\(O/(R_{\text{peak}} \cdot \eta_{\text{hw}})\)), and 10% dispatch and latency overhead (\(L_{\text{lat}}\)). A hardware upgrade doubles the GPU’s peak dense FP16 compute (\(R_{\text{peak}}\)) while leaving memory bandwidth (\(\text{BW}\)) and software latency (\(L_{\text{lat}}\)) unchanged. What is the maximum theoretical speedup this upgrade can achieve on this workload?

    1. Approximately \(1.18\times\) speedup (a 15% reduction in step time), because doubling compute only reduces the 30% compute slice to 15% while the 70% non-compute time remains unchanged
    2. \(2.00\times\) speedup, because doubling peak compute throughput halves the overall execution time of matrix multiplication operations
    3. \(1.67\times\) speedup, because the 30% compute fraction and 10% latency fraction are both accelerated by the faster clock frequency
    4. \(1.43\times\) speedup, because the memory-bound portion is compressed by the faster arithmetic execution units

    Answer: The correct answer is A. Approximately \(1.18\times\) speedup (a 15% reduction in step time), because doubling compute only reduces the 30% compute slice to 15% while the 70% non-compute time remains unchanged. Under the iron law (\(T = D_{\text{vol}}/\text{BW} + O/(R_{\text{peak}} \cdot \eta_{\text{hw}}) + L_{\text{lat}}\)), doubling \(R_{\text{peak}}\) only affects the compute term, halving it from 0.30 to 0.15 of the baseline time. The new normalized time is \(0.60 + 0.15 + 0.10 = 0.85\), yielding a speedup of \(1.0 / 0.85 \approx 1.18\times\). Expecting a \(2.0\times\) speedup commits the classic Amdahl’s law fallacy by ignoring that 70% of execution is governed by memory bandwidth and latency, while the other choices incorrectly assume compute hardware improvements accelerate memory transfers or fixed software dispatch overhead.

    Learning Objective: Apply the iron law of ML performance to calculate the theoretical speedup of targeted hardware upgrades under Amdahl’s law constraints

  2. An operation has an arithmetic intensity of 200 FLOP/byte. On an NVIDIA V100 (ridge point \(\approx 139\text{ FLOP/byte}\)) and an H100 (ridge point \(\approx 295\text{ FLOP/byte}\)), how does the tighter roofline ceiling change across generations, and what is the primary systems implication for performance optimization?

    Answer: At fixed intensity, the tighter ideal ceiling changes from compute on V100 (200 > 139 FLOP/byte) to bandwidth on H100 (200 < 295 FLOP/byte). This identifies candidate limits, not an observed bottleneck. Measure kernel traffic and execution first; fusion, tiling, or precision reduction can raise the bandwidth ceiling, while dependencies, launch costs, or inefficient scheduling can keep either implementation below its roofline.

    Learning Objective: Analyze how generational hardware balance changes a workload’s tighter ideal ceiling and the evidence needed to diagnose execution.

  3. Order the following levels of the GPU memory hierarchy from nearest to the streaming multiprocessor arithmetic units to farthest: (1) L2 Cache, (2) Registers, (3) High-Bandwidth Memory (HBM), (4) Shared Memory (SRAM).

    Answer: The correct order is (2) Registers -> (4) Shared Memory (SRAM) -> (1) L2 Cache -> (3) High-Bandwidth Memory (HBM). Registers and shared memory provide local working storage, L2 serves reuse across SMs, and off-chip HBM supplies bulk capacity. Keeping reusable intermediates near compute avoids costly off-chip transfers; numerical energy comparisons additionally require a common transaction size and measurement boundary.

    Learning Objective: Order GPU storage tiers by proximity to compute and explain the cost of off-chip traffic.

  4. True or False: Arithmetic intensity is a fixed, intrinsic property of a neural network architecture that remains invariant regardless of batch size or kernel implementation choices.

    Answer: False. Arithmetic intensity (\(I = \text{FLOPs} / \text{bytes}\)) is an algorithmic and implementation-dependent property, not an invariant model constant. For instance, increasing the serving batch size amortizes model weight loading across more requests, raising arithmetic intensity linearly with batch size, while operator fusion increases arithmetic intensity by keeping intermediate activations in on-chip SRAM instead of round-tripping through HBM.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate the relationship between workload arithmetic intensity, implementation strategies, and batch dimensions

  5. What is the primary energy and performance consequence of repeatedly streaming intermediate tensors through off-chip HBM rather than retaining them in on-chip SRAM?

    1. Off-chip transfers bypass the L2 cache entirely, preventing the GPU memory management unit from performing virtual address translation
    2. Off-chip transfers consume memory-interface energy and can limit throughput through bandwidth and latency, whereas retaining reusable intermediates in SRAM reduces HBM traffic
    3. Streaming through HBM causes numerical drift and precision loss because DRAM capacitors lose charge over multi-nanosecond round-trips
    4. SRAM access draws higher total power because operating at higher clock frequencies increases dynamic capacitance dissipation beyond HBM interface PHYs

    Answer: The correct answer is B. Repeated off-chip transfers consume energy and bandwidth without adding useful arithmetic. Fusion and tiling can retain intermediates in SRAM and reduce that traffic, subject to local capacity and occupancy constraints. Neither numerical precision drift in DRAM nor failed address translation explains this bottleneck; transaction-size conventions are needed before comparing access energies quantitatively.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate the thermodynamic and latency consequences of off-chip HBM data movement versus on-chip SRAM caching

← Back to Questions

Self-Check: Answer

  1. In modern Large Language Model (LLM) serving systems, why do the prompt prefill phase and the token decode phase exhibit fundamentally different performance bottlenecks on GPU hardware?

    1. Prefill generates tokens sequentially one by one, making it memory-bound by weight reads, whereas decode processes all prompt tokens in parallel, making it compute-bound by Tensor Cores
    2. Prefill relies exclusively on integer arithmetic units, whereas decode requires floating-point matrix multiplication units
    3. Long-prompt prefill GEMMs reuse weights across prompt tokens and can have a compute ceiling, whereas weight-dominated single-request decode has low intensity and a bandwidth ceiling
    4. Prefill requires continuous network AllToAll collective exchanges between GPUs, whereas decode requires no inter-GPU communication

    Answer: The correct answer is C. Long-prompt prefill GEMMs can reuse weights across many prompt tokens, while single-request decode streams weights for one token. The simplified FP16 estimates place those components above and below the ridge, respectively. Neither estimate proves saturation or classifies every operator: actual traffic, kernel shapes, cache reads, dispatch, and communication require profiling. The phases use compatible arithmetic rather than exclusively different number types.

    Learning Objective: Compare the computational characteristics and primary hardware bottlenecks of the prefill and decode serving phases

  2. Explain how PagedAttention reduces KV-cache allocation waste compared with contiguous preallocation. What measurements determine whether the recovered capacity improves serving throughput?

    Answer: Contiguous preallocation reserves capacity for each request’s maximum length, leaving unused slots when sequences are shorter. PagedAttention allocates fixed-size blocks as sequences grow and reuses free blocks across requests, sharply reducing allocation waste, though a final partly filled block can remain. The recovered capacity permits larger batches; reported 2–4-fold throughput gains depend on the workload and latency constraint rather than following automatically from paging.

    Learning Objective: Explain how paged allocation reduces KV fragmentation and can expand batch capacity.

  3. The architectural pattern that decouples LLM inference by executing the compute-bound prompt prefill phase and the bandwidth-bound autoregressive token decode phase on separate, specialized hardware pools is known as ____ serving.

    Answer: disaggregated. Separate pools specialize for prompt processing and token generation; transferring the KV cache between them adds a communication cost that the specialization must repay.

    Learning Objective: Identify the architectural term for separating prefill and decode workloads across specialized hardware pools

  4. A 70B parameter LLM (140 GB FP16 weights) is served across an 8-GPU NVIDIA H100 node with tensor parallelism (TP=8). Each GPU holds 17.5 GB of weights and provides 3.35 TB/s of HBM bandwidth and 989 TFLOP/s peak FP16 compute. At batch size 1, what is the theoretical bandwidth-limited decode step time per token, and why is achieved compute utilization below 0.4%?

    1. Decode takes approximately 120 ms per token because NVLink latency serializes the 8 GPUs, consuming 99.6% of time in network barriers
    2. Decode takes approximately 0.5 ms per token because the 8 GPUs execute in parallel at peak FP16 compute throughput
    3. Decode takes approximately 24.0 ms per token because the GPU must reread the entire 140 GB model across the PCI-e bus on each step
    4. Decode takes approximately 5.2 ms per token (\(17.5\text{ GB} / 3.35\text{ TB/s}\)), and compute utilization is below 0.4% because the arithmetic intensity is only 1.0 FLOP/byte compared to the H100 ridge point of ~295 FLOP/byte

    Answer: The correct answer is D. Decode takes approximately 5.2 ms per token (\(17.5\text{ GB} / 3.35\text{ TB/s}\)), and compute utilization is below 0.4% because the arithmetic intensity is only 1.0 FLOP/byte compared to the H100 ridge point of ~295 FLOP/byte. At batch size 1 with TP=8, each GPU reads its 17.5 GB weight shard once per token: \(T_{\text{decode}} = 17.5\text{ GB} / 3.35\text{ TB/s} \approx 5.2\text{ ms}\). Each GPU executes \(2 \times 8.75 \times 10^9 = 1.75 \times 10^{10}\text{ FLOP}\), giving an arithmetic intensity of \(\frac{1.75 \times 10^{10}\text{ FLOP}}{1.75 \times 10^{10}\text{ bytes}} = 1.0\text{ FLOP/byte}\). The achieved compute rate is \(1.75 \times 10^{10} / 0.00522 \approx 3.35\text{ TFLOP/s}\), which is only \(3.35 / 989 \approx 0.34\%\) of the H100’s peak compute. The other options miscalculate memory transfer times, confuse single-GPU shards with full model weights, or postulate unrealistic PCIe and NVLink stalls.

    Learning Objective: Calculate the theoretical decode step time and hardware utilization of a tensor-parallel LLM deployment using roofline principles

← Back to Questions

Self-Check: Answer

  1. A production LLM serving decode step on an NVIDIA H100 reports a Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) of only 2.5%, but a Model Bandwidth Utilization (MBU) of 86%. How should the performance engineer interpret these diagnostic metrics?

    1. The GPU hardware is malfunctioning and must be power-cycled to clear thermal throttling
    2. The measurements are consistent with a bandwidth-limited path; low MFU alone is not evidence of poor implementation when MBU is high
    3. The low MFU indicates severe kernel launch latency on the CPU dispatcher that requires rewriting into CUDA Graphs
    4. The high MBU indicates excessive gradient checkpointing overhead that is saturating the PCIe bus

    Answer: The correct answer is B. High MBU and low MFU can be consistent with a memory-bound decode path rather than defective hardware or dominant launch gaps. Check FLOPs and bytes at the same boundary: 2.5% of 989 TFLOP/s divided by 86% of 3.35 TB/s implies about 8.6 FLOP/byte, not one. Further diagnosis should test redundant traffic and the request critical path before declaring the entire service optimal.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate the diagnostic roles of Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) and Model Bandwidth Utilization (MBU) across training and inference regimes

  2. Using the application-to-hardware profiling hierarchy, describe the drill-down an engineer should use to diagnose an LLM serving pipeline that misses its p99 latency objective. Explain where synchronized traces across ranks enter the investigation.

    Answer: Start with application metrics such as P99 first-token and inter-token latency. Use framework traces across ranks to localize scheduling, data-transfer, or collective delays. Inspect kernel timelines to distinguish launch gaps, late arrival, and serialized work. Finally, use hardware counters on the implicated kernel to test memory bandwidth, occupancy, and instruction mix. Distributed scope cuts across this application-to-hardware hierarchy; it is not a separate fixed level.

    Learning Objective: Synthesize a structured multi-level profiling diagnostic protocol to isolate performance bottlenecks

  3. If profiling confirms that a matrix operation uses scalar GPU arithmetic rather than Tensor Core instructions, the scalar units are called ____ Cores; tensor dimensions alone do not establish that execution path.

    Answer: CUDA. Kernel selection depends on datatype, shape, library, and hardware. Misalignment may reduce efficiency without universally forcing a scalar fallback; inspect the executed instruction mix.

    Learning Objective: Distinguish scalar CUDA execution from Tensor Core execution using profiling evidence.

  4. Why can enabling shape recording in a high-fidelity PyTorch profiler perturb tensor lifetimes and increase memory demand in a workload that runs stably without profiling?

    1. The profiler increases GPU clock frequency beyond thermal limits, triggering emergency memory shutdowns
    2. The profiler converts all FP16 tensors to FP64 double-precision matrices during recording
    3. Shape recording can retain tensor references, inhibit reference-count-dependent optimizations, and introduce extra copies
    4. The profiler overwrites the CUDA driver’s virtual memory page tables with TensorBoard logs

    Answer: The correct answer is C. Shape recording can retain tensor references and affect optimizations or copies, so the instrumented run may require more memory. Allocation-history recording instead records allocator events and has metadata overhead; it should not be assumed to keep every activation alive. Compare matched instrumented and uninstrumented runs rather than attributing the change to clock rates or automatic precision conversion.

    Learning Objective: Analyze the observer effect (Heisenberg effect) of profiling tools on memory allocation and system behavior

  5. True or False: In an NVIDIA Nsight Systems trace, observing that the CUDA API row on the CPU thread displays wide bars significantly larger than the corresponding CUDA HW kernel bars indicates that the GPU kernels are executing too slowly.

    Answer: False. Wide CPU API bars do not show that GPU kernels themselves are slow. They may reflect host launch overhead, synchronization, or blocking calls. Correlate them with GPU idle gaps and dependencies before diagnosing a host-submission bottleneck; graph replay helps only the overhead it actually removes.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate CPU-GPU interaction patterns and launch bottlenecks on Nsight Systems timeline traces

Self-Check: Answer

  1. A 70B parameter model achieves 65% MFU on an isolated 8-GPU node. When scaled across a 128-GPU cluster connected via InfiniBand, the training step time increases such that cluster-wide MFU drops to 48%. What is the Scaling Tax incurred by this distributed deployment?

    1. 17.0% Scaling Tax, calculated as the direct difference (\(65\% - 48\%\))
    2. 35.4% Scaling Tax, calculated as the ratio of unutilized compute (\(1 - 0.48 / 0.65\))
    3. 52.0% Scaling Tax, representing the unutilized fraction of the 128 GPUs
    4. Approximately 26.2% Scaling Tax (\(1 - \text{Fleet MFU} / \text{Local MFU} = 1 - 0.48 / 0.65\)), representing the relative efficiency loss compared with the single-node baseline

    Answer: The correct answer is D. The relative MFU loss is 1 - 0.48/0.65 ≈ 26.2%; the 17-point difference is an absolute percentage-point change. The ratio measures efficiency loss under the stated workload and accounting assumptions. It does not isolate communication or barriers: matched traces must distinguish those costs from workload shape, offload, kernel efficiency, and rank imbalance.

    Learning Objective: Calculate and interpret the distributed Scaling Tax from single-node and fleet-wide MFU metrics

  2. In a synchronous data-parallel cluster of 1,000 GPUs, explain why one gray-failure node that delivers 10% less processing throughput can reduce the entire fleet’s useful throughput by roughly 10% when its work determines every step’s critical path.

    Answer: All ranks must complete their required work before a synchronized step can advance. A rank delivering 90% of normal throughput takes 1/0.9 ≈ 1.111 times as long for the same work, so the fleet can be limited to 90% of its original throughput. The result assumes that this delay is exposed and dominates the critical path. A 10% increase in duration would instead imply about a 9.1% throughput loss.

    Learning Objective: Analyze how barrier synchronization amplifies single-node gray failures and stragglers into cluster-wide throughput collapses

  3. Synchronized clocks record the same collective in otherwise matched runs. In the baseline, every rank enters at 8 ms and all finish at 10 ms. In case A, all but one rank enter at 8 ms, the last enters at 12 ms, and all finish at 14 ms. In case B, every rank enters at 8 ms and all finish at 14 ms. What interval accounts for the extra completion time in each case, and what measurement and targeted experiment would help distinguish the causes?

    Answer: Case A adds 4 ms before the last rank enters; completion remains 2 ms after that entry. Trace the delayed rank’s preceding computation, input, and host work, then test the implicated delay with the collective configuration held fixed. Case B extends the interval after all ranks enter from 2 ms to 6 ms. Inspect collective progress and contention, then repeat with competing traffic isolated. The trace localizes the exposed delay but does not alone prove a network fault.

    Learning Objective: Diagnose supplied aligned-rank traces and justify measurements that distinguish late arrival from prolonged collective completion.

  4. Which tiered testing strategy is specifically designed to catch nonlinear scaling collapses (such as exhausted InfiniBand credit buffers or garbage collection pauses) before deploying a code change to a 1,000-GPU cluster?

    1. Small-scale canaries on 8 and 64 GPUs to map the scaling efficiency curve and identify curve-bending regressions before full-fleet deployment
    2. Single-GPU unit tests running with Python debug assertions enabled
    3. Synthetic micro-benchmarks that test only CPU RAM bandwidth
    4. Static code linting to count the number of matrix multiplications in the model

    Answer: The correct answer is A. Small-scale canaries on 8 and 64 GPUs to map the scaling efficiency curve and identify curve-bending regressions before full-fleet deployment. Scaling regressions are inherently distributed and nonlinear; changes that introduce negligible overhead on 1 GPU can trigger severe network buffer exhaustion or synchronization stalls at scale. Testing on intermediate multi-node canaries (8 and 64 GPUs) maps the scaling slope, catching efficiency anomalies before committing full-fleet resources. Single-GPU tests, static linters, and CPU micro-benchmarks cannot observe distributed collective and interconnect behaviors.

    Learning Objective: Design a tiered regression testing protocol to detect distributed scaling anomalies prior to full-fleet deployment

← Back to Questions

Self-Check: Answer
  1. **In FlashAttention’s online softmax algorithm, arrange the following steps in the exact order executed when processing tile \(t\) of key-value blocks in fast on-chip SRAM:
  1. Rescale previous running sum and output accumulator by multiplying by \(e^{m_{\text{old}} - m_{\text{new}}}\)
  2. Compute local block of attention scores \(A_t = Q_{\text{block}} K_t^T\)
  3. Compute local softmax probabilities with \(m_{\text{new}}\) and accumulate into running output
  4. Update the running maximum: \(m_{\text{new}} = \max(m_{\text{old}}, \max(A_t))\)**

Answer: The correct order is (2) Compute local block of attention scores \(A_t = Q_{\text{block}} K_t^T\) -> (4) Update the running maximum: \(m_{\text{new}} = \max(m_{\text{old}}, \max(A_t))\) -> (1) Rescale previous running sum and output accumulator by multiplying by \(e^{m_{\text{old}} - m_{\text{new}}}\) -> (3) Compute local softmax probabilities with \(m_{\text{new}}\) and accumulate into running output. The algorithm first computes the local tile scores, updates the running maximum to incorporate the new tile’s peak, rescales previous partial accumulators using the difference between old and new maxima, and finally accumulates the new tile’s softmax contribution.

Learning Objective: Apply the algorithmic sequence of online softmax tiling in FlashAttention to maintain numerical exactness in on-chip SRAM

  1. Why are CUDA Graphs highly effective at accelerating the autoregressive decode phase of Large Language Models, but significantly more difficult to apply to the prompt prefill phase?

    1. CUDA Graph replay requires capture-compatible work and valid referenced storage; repetitive decode shapes often allow more reuse than variable-length prefill shapes
    2. CUDA Graphs only support integer quantization kernels and cannot record floating-point matrix multiplications used during prefill
    3. CUDA Graphs execute exclusively on CPU cores and cannot capture GPU kernels that exceed 1000 TFLOP/s
    4. CUDA Graphs require disaggregated serving network fabrics to replay kernel dispatches across multiple nodes

    Answer: The correct answer is A. Stable captured work lets graph replay reduce repeated host launch overhead. Decode often admits such a repetitive execution region, while varying prefill shapes require bucketing, padding, updates, or separate captures. Capture also depends on memory-address and operation constraints; variable prompts do not make graph use universally impossible. Integer-only or CPU-only execution is not the explanation.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate the operational constraints and execution prerequisites of CUDA Graphs in LLM serving pipelines

  2. Explain how tiled attention can improve runtime even when online softmax rescaling or backward recomputation adds arithmetic. Why do removed HBM transfers alone not establish a fixed wall-clock speedup?

    Answer: Standard unfused attention materializes score and probability matrices in HBM. FlashAttention tiles the computation so these intermediates remain on chip. Extra rescaling arithmetic can be cheaper than the removed HBM traffic when data movement is the bottleneck. The additional work is not literally free or necessarily executed on Tensor Cores; the net gain depends on sequence length, kernel implementation, and the resulting resource limits.

    Learning Objective: Analyze the I/O-aware systems trade-off of trading extra arithmetic operations in SRAM to eliminate off-chip HBM traffic

  3. True or False: Wrapping an unfused PyTorch layer in a CUDA Graph automatically fuses adjacent element-wise kernels into a single GPU kernel to eliminate intermediate high-bandwidth memory (HBM) writes.

    Answer: False. Graph replay reduces repeated host submission for a captured kernel sequence; it does not itself fuse kernels or eliminate every launch and synchronization cost. Removing intermediate HBM transfers requires a supported fusion transformation or a custom kernel. The measured end-to-end saving depends on how much submission overhead was exposed.

    Learning Objective: Distinguish between launch-overhead elimination via CUDA Graphs and memory-traffic reduction via operator fusion

  4. Consider a bias-GELU-dropout chain applied to a tensor with hidden dimension 4096 and batch size 2048 in FP16. The unfused path launches three kernels and materializes two intermediate tensors of approximately 16.8 MB each. If a fused kernel can retain their values in registers or SRAM, how does fusion change memory traffic and execution?

    1. It increases total HBM traffic to 128 MB because the fused kernel must write debug checkpoints after each sub-operation
    2. It removes four intermediate transfers totaling approximately 67.1 MB and reduces three launches to one, while preserving the input read and final output write
    3. It converts the operation into a pure CPU task to bypass GPU shared memory allocation limits
    4. It eliminates the need to load the weight matrix \(W\) from HBM by synthesizing weights dynamically in registers

    Answer: The correct answer is B. Each of two intermediates contains 2048 × 4096 × 2 bytes, or about 16.8 MB. Writing and rereading both costs four such transfers, about 67.1 MB (64 MiB). Fusion removes those transfers when the intermediate values remain on chip. It does not eliminate required input reads or create model weights; register pressure and occupancy still constrain the implementation.

    Learning Objective: Calculate intermediate memory traffic savings and launch overhead reduction achieved through operator fusion

Self-Check: Answer

  1. Large Language Models exhibit emergent activation outlier features where \(\sim 0.1\%\) of channels have magnitudes \(3–20\times\) larger than normal. How does SmoothQuant enable efficient uniform INT8 quantization (W8A8) across both weights and activations without suffering catastrophic quality loss?

    1. SmoothQuant drops all outlier channels from the model entirely, using low-rank distillation to compensate for removed hidden dimensions
    2. SmoothQuant splits the matrix multiplication at runtime into an FP16 branch for outliers and an INT8 branch for standard features
    3. SmoothQuant applies a per-channel scaling factor that divides activations by \(s\) and multiplies weights by \(s\), migrating quantization difficulty from dynamic activations to static weights prior to inference
    4. SmoothQuant computes an exact Hessian inverse across all layers during training using the straight-through estimator

    Answer: The correct answer is C. SmoothQuant applies a per-channel scaling factor that divides activations by \(s\) and multiplies weights by \(s\), migrating quantization difficulty from dynamic activations to static weights prior to inference. Because activation outliers are concentrated in specific channels across all tokens, multiplying the weights by the per-channel scale and dividing the inputs by the same scale is mathematically equivalent. This smooths activation peaks at the cost of slightly increasing weight dynamic range, enabling uniform INT8 quantization on both weights and activations (W8A8) to leverage INT8 Tensor Cores. The runtime branch approach describes LLM.int8(), the Hessian inverse refers to GPTQ, and dropping channels is a destructive heuristic.

    Learning Objective: Compare mathematical strategies for mitigating activation outlier channels during low-bit quantization

  2. Contrast weight-only quantization (e.g. AWQ INT4) with weight-activation quantization (e.g. SmoothQuant W8A8 or FP8) in terms of target serving operational regime (batch size) and hardware execution units used.

    Answer: Weight-only INT4 quantization targets memory-bound inference at small batch sizes (e.g. batch size 1 decode), where the primary bottleneck is streaming weights from HBM; weights are dequantized on-the-fly to FP16 and executed on standard FP16 Tensor Cores. Weight-activation quantization (W8A8 or FP8) quantizes both operands, targeting compute-bound inference at large batch sizes or training, executing directly on INT8 or FP8 Tensor Cores to double arithmetic compute throughput in addition to reducing memory traffic.

    Learning Objective: Analyze the operational trade-offs and hardware utilization differences between weight-only and weight-activation quantization

  3. True or False: In block-wise INT4 quantization, reducing the group block size \(G_{\text{block}}\) from 64 to 16 can improve local range resolution but quadruples the scale-factor metadata overhead from 0.25 bits/weight (6.25% overhead on INT4) to 1.0 bit/weight (25% overhead on INT4).

    Answer: True. With block-wise quantization, each group of \(G_{\text{block}}\) weights shares one 16-bit FP16 scale factor. For \(G_{\text{block}} = 64\), the scale adds \(16/64 = 0.25\) bits per weight (a \(0.25/4 = 6.25\%\) overhead relative to 4-bit payload). For \(G_{\text{block}} = 16\), the scale adds \(16/16 = 1.0\) bit per weight (a \(1.0/4 = 25\%\) overhead relative to 4-bit payload), yielding an effective bit-width of 5.0 bits/weight.

    Learning Objective: Calculate the metadata overhead and effective bit-width scaling of block-wise quantization group sizes

  4. A 70B model uses 17.5 GB of weights per GPU on eight 80 GB GPUs. Ignoring runtime reserves and quantization metadata, INT4 weights use approximately 4.4 GB per GPU and INT8 KV cache halves the per-token cache footprint. Why does the KV capacity gain combine two distinct effects rather than equal the fourfold weight reduction?

    1. Because INT4 weights bypass the GPU memory hierarchy and execute directly from CPU system RAM
    2. Because quantizing the KV cache converts autoregressive decoding into non-autoregressive parallel generation
    3. Because INT8 KV cache pages eliminate the need for GPU virtual address translation tables in PagedAttention
    4. Because reducing weights from 17.5 GB to 4.4 GB expands available KV cache space from 62.5 GB to 75.6 GB per GPU, which, when multiplied by halving the per-request INT8 KV cache footprint, multiplicatively expands total admitted batch capacity

    Answer: The correct answer is D. The ideal KV budget grows from 62.5 GB to 75.6 GB, a factor of 75.6/62.5 ≈ 1.21. Halving bytes per cached token multiplies capacity by another two, yielding approximately 2.42 times the original token capacity, not more than four times. Runtime reserves, metadata, sequence lengths, and scheduler policy reduce the deployable batch limit; quantization does not remove autoregressive dependencies or page tables.

    Learning Objective: Synthesize how weight and KV-cache quantization interact multiplicatively to expand serving batch capacity

Self-Check: Answer
  1. **Arrange the following stages of the deep learning graph compilation pipeline in their standard logical order of execution:
  1. Operator Fusion Pass (identifying fusible subgraphs to eliminate intermediate HBM round-trips)
  2. Graph Capture (tracing Python code to construct a Directed Acyclic Graph of tensor operations)
  3. Kernel Selection and Code Generation (generating and autotuning backend Triton/CUDA machine code)
  4. Memory Planning Pass (analyzing tensor lifetimes and scheduling physical buffer reuse)
  5. Graph-Level Optimization (applying algebraic simplifications and constant folding)**

Answer: The correct order is (2) Graph Capture -> (5) Graph-Level Optimization -> (1) Operator Fusion Pass -> (4) Memory Planning Pass -> (3) Kernel Selection and Code Generation. The compiler first captures the computation into a DAG, performs device-agnostic algebraic simplifications, identifies adjacent operations to fuse, plans memory allocations across the simplified and fused graph, and finally lowers the optimized subgraphs into specialized machine code.

Learning Objective: Order the core transformation stages of a deep learning graph compilation pipeline

  1. How does TorchDynamo in PyTorch 2.0 capture computation graphs from standard Python code, and what is the systems impact when it encounters an unsupported dynamic construct (such as data-dependent Python control flow)?

    1. TorchDynamo intercepts CPython frame evaluation at the bytecode level (via PEP 523); when encountering untraceable Python constructs, it inserts a Graph Break that splits the trace into separate subgraphs, preventing fusion across the break boundary
    2. TorchDynamo compiles Python source text into static C++ before execution; when encountering dynamic control flow, it aborts the process with a fatal runtime exception
    3. TorchDynamo executes an abstract syntax tree rewriter that replaces all Python loops with CUDA thread blocks, ignoring data-dependent conditions
    4. TorchDynamo runs exclusively ahead-of-time, forcing all dynamic variables to fixed constants during model export

    Answer: The correct answer is A. TorchDynamo intercepts CPython frame evaluation at the bytecode level (via PEP 523); when encountering untraceable Python constructs, it inserts a Graph Break that splits the trace into separate subgraphs, preventing fusion across the break boundary. By hooking frame evaluation at the bytecode interpreter level, TorchDynamo captures graphs without requiring model code modifications. However, graph breaks divide execution into smaller compiled fragments separated by Python interpreter overhead, reducing the scope for cross-operator fusion and memory planning. The other choices incorrectly describe fatal exceptions, AST loop rewriting, or forced ahead-of-time constant evaluation.

    Learning Objective: Explain the bytecode interception mechanism of TorchDynamo and analyze the performance impact of graph breaks

  2. A constructed batch-one budget for a 13B model stored in INT4 with FP16 arithmetic after dequantization assigns 50% of step time to GEMMs (4.17 ms), 35% to element-wise operations (2.92 ms), and 15% to launch overhead (1.25 ms), approximately 8.33 ms per token (120 tokens/s). Assume compilation removes 80% of launch overhead and reduces element-wise time by 70%, leaving GEMM time unchanged. Calculate the new time and throughput, and identify the next interval to inspect.

    Answer: The remaining intervals are 4.17 ms GEMM, about 0.88 ms element-wise work, and 0.25 ms launch overhead, totaling approximately 5.30 ms, or 189 tokens/s. Rounded inputs account for the small difference in the displayed baseline sum. GEMM now occupies about 79% of the budget, making it the next interval to inspect; counters and traces must determine whether its exposed cost is compute, memory traffic, or implementation inefficiency.

    Learning Objective: Calculate a constructed compilation dividend and identify the next interval requiring diagnosis.

  3. In torch.compile, the compilation mode that expands the search over supported kernel implementations, trading additional build work for potential warmed-execution gains, is called ____.

    Answer: max-autotune. This mode benchmarks candidate implementations and retains a fast choice for the target workload and hardware, trading compilation work for subsequent execution efficiency.

    Learning Objective: Identify the torch.compile configuration mode responsible for hardware kernel autotuning

  4. How does XLA optimization within a selected compiled region differ from eager execution boundaries, and what specialization trade-off must a deployment account for?

    1. XLA generates Python bytecode on every forward pass, trading compilation speed for execution latency
    2. XLA optimizes across a selected compiled region and can insert GSPMD communication; new signatures can require specialization, while cached signatures can reuse executables
    3. XLA executes only on CPU hosts, whereas TorchDynamo compiles directly to TPU hardware
    4. XLA requires dynamic shapes and cannot optimize static tensor dimensions

    Answer: The correct answer is B. XLA can optimize across operations within a selected region and derive communication from sharding annotations. Compilation and specialization costs depend on that region and its signatures; previously compiled signatures can reuse cached executables. It need not capture all host activity, and it supports accelerator backends as well as CPUs. Measure build cost and reuse rather than assuming every shape change recompiles the entire application.

    Learning Objective: Analyze compiled-region scope, specialization cost, and artifact reuse.

Self-Check: Answer

  1. Why does speculative decoding accelerate autoregressive generation on modern GPUs without altering the target model’s output probability distribution?

    1. It forces the draft model to use the exact same weights as the target model through FP8 weight quantizing
    2. It skips attention computation for all tokens where the draft model confidence exceeds 50%
    3. It accepts candidate draft tokens with probability \(\min(1, p_\theta / q_\phi)\) and resamples rejected tokens from the positive residual \((p_\theta - q_\phi)_+\), ensuring strict mathematical equivalence while verifying \(K\) tokens in a single parallel target pass to amortize weight-streaming bandwidth
    4. It runs the draft model and target model on separate GPUs connected via PCIe, bypassing HBM bandwidth limits entirely

    Answer: The correct answer is C. It accepts candidate draft tokens with probability \(\min(1, p_\theta / q_\phi)\) and resamples rejected tokens from the positive residual \((p_\theta - q_\phi)_+\), ensuring strict mathematical equivalence while verifying \(K\) tokens in a single parallel target pass to amortize weight-streaming bandwidth. In memory-bound decode, loading target model weights from HBM once can verify \(K\) candidate tokens simultaneously in a single compute pass (\([K, d_{\text{model}}] \times [d_{\text{model}}, d_{\text{model}}]\)), effectively shifting the arithmetic intensity \(K\times\) rightward toward the roofline ridge. The acceptance-rejection math guarantees that the emitted token sequence matches the target model distribution \(p_\theta\) exactly. The other choices describe non-existent confidence thresholds, weight quantization confusion, or PCIe offloading.

    Learning Objective: Analyze how speculative decoding achieves latency speedups while preserving exact target model output distributions

  2. Explain why speculative decoding provides substantial speedup at batch size 1 but exhibits diminishing or even negative returns as serving batch size increases (e.g. batch size 64).

    Answer: At small batches, target verification can reuse one weight pass across several candidate positions while arithmetic units have spare capacity. Larger batches already amortize weight reads, so additional verification and draft work can erase that benefit. The crossover is workload-dependent: batch size 64 alone does not establish compute saturation. Measure accepted output per round, draft and verification costs, and loaded throughput before enabling speculation.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate the interaction between speculative decoding efficiency and serving batch size dynamics

  3. True or False: In a Mixture of Experts (MoE) architecture, scaling the number of experts from 8 to 256 is a cost-free capacity scaling knob because each token only activates top-\(k\) experts during inference.

    Answer: False. More experts increase total weight capacity even when each token activates a fixed number. Distributing them can add communication, routing imbalance, and latency, but transferred activation bytes do not necessarily grow with total expert count when token count, top-k, and representation size are fixed. Placement and the number of destinations determine the actual network cost.

    Learning Objective: Analyze the system-level memory, communication, and load balancing costs of scaling expert counts in MoE models

  4. In a speculative decoding deployment, draft generation takes 4 ms and target verification takes 32 ms. Measurements show a mean of 3.5 emitted tokens per round, including correction or bonus tokens. What is the amortized time per emitted token and speedup over a 32 ms autoregressive baseline?

    1. Effective ITL is 36.0 ms/token, representing a \(0.89\times\) slowdown due to draft model overhead
    2. Effective ITL is 7.2 ms/token, representing a \(4.4\times\) speedup
    3. Effective ITL is 20.5 ms/token, representing a \(1.56\times\) speedup
    4. Approximately 10.3 ms per emitted token and a 3.1-fold amortized speedup

    Answer: The correct answer is D. A round takes 4 + 32 = 36 ms and emits 3.5 tokens on average, so amortized time is 36/3.5 ≈ 10.29 ms per token and speedup is 32/10.29 ≈ 3.11. Dividing by all five candidate tokens would wrongly assume full acceptance. This average does not establish an inter-token latency percentile because outputs can arrive in blocks.

    Learning Objective: Calculate amortized time per emitted token from measured round cost and output yield.

Self-Check: Answer

  1. Under the exposed-time formulation of communication-computation overlap (\(T_{\text{step}} = \max(T_{\text{compute}}, T_{\text{comm}}) + T_{\text{sync}}\)), what physical condition is required to achieve 100% communication hiding (zero exposed communication time)?

    1. \(T_{\text{comm}} \leq T_{\text{compute}}\), a necessary duration condition; full hiding additionally requires timely independent work and tolerable resource interference
    2. \(T_{\text{comm}} = 0\), requiring all distributed GPUs to share a single physical HBM memory controller
    3. \(T_{\text{compute}} = 0\), requiring the GPU to execute collective operations without running backward pass gradients
    4. \(T_{\text{comm}} \ge 2 \times T_{\text{compute}}\), ensuring communication has priority over SM scheduling

    Answer: The correct answer is A. Communication duration must fit within the available independent-compute window. This duration condition is necessary, not sufficient: dependencies must make that work ready in time, and resource contention must not erase the gain. If communication outlasts the window, some remains exposed. Neither zero-time communication nor giving collectives scheduling priority establishes useful overlap.

    Learning Objective: Apply the exposed-time test to determine whether inter-device communication is fully hidden behind concurrent computation

  2. Explain how concurrent GEMM and NCCL streams can contend for an accelerator’s SMs and memory bandwidth. Under what measured condition is a reduction in local compute throughput worthwhile?

    Answer: Communication kernels can consume execution, cache, and bandwidth resources that the GEMM also needs. Temporal overlap can therefore slow local computation. The trade-off is worthwhile only when the communication time removed from the critical path exceeds the added slowdown and the end-to-end workload improves. A fixed SM reservation or universal percentage penalty cannot establish that condition.

    Learning Objective: Explain the physical resource contention mechanism of SM partitioning during concurrent compute and communication execution

  3. **In PyTorch DistributedDataParallel (DDP), arrange the following events in the exact sequence that implements asynchronous gradient communication overlap during training:

  1. Main compute stream continues backward pass execution on earlier layers concurrently with network transfer
  2. Autograd backward pass computes parameter gradients for a layer
  3. Event barrier synchronizes the communication stream with the compute stream before optimizer step
  4. Autograd backward hook triggers asynchronous AllReduce on the parameter bucket in a dedicated NCCL stream**

Answer: The correct order is (2) Autograd backward pass computes parameter gradients for a layer -> (4) Autograd backward hook triggers asynchronous AllReduce on the parameter bucket in a dedicated NCCL stream -> (1) Main compute stream continues backward pass execution on earlier layers concurrently with network transfer -> (3) Event barrier synchronizes the communication stream with the compute stream before optimizer step. As gradients are computed, hooks dispatch non-blocking AllReduces on a side stream while backward propagation continues on the main stream, synchronizing only at the end of the backward pass.

Learning Objective: Order the runtime sequence of PyTorch DDP bucketed gradient communication and backward computation overlap

  1. True or False: If a side-stream collective reads a tensor before its producing kernel completes, missing producer-consumer ordering can cause a data race even though each stream executes its own operations in order.

    Answer: True. Per-stream order does not establish order between a producer and consumer on different streams. An event wait, an appropriate stream wait, or equivalent ordering must establish readiness; storage lifetime must also remain valid. Concurrency is safe only after both obligations hold.

    Learning Objective: Analyze multi-stream CUDA synchronization hazards and data race conditions

Self-Check: Answer

  1. In the constructed batch-one 70B decode budget, order the three optimization experiments: (1) Operator fusion and graph replay, (2) Weight precision reduction, (3) Speculative decoding.

    Answer: The correct order is (2) Weight precision reduction -> (1) Operator fusion and graph replay -> (3) Speculative decoding. Precision first reduces the exposed GEMM interval. Fusion and replay then reduce attention, element-wise, and launch intervals. Speculation finally changes output per target pass. Each round uses explicit assumptions and a shared timing boundary; the sequence is not a universal optimization order.

    Learning Objective: Order the experiments in a constructed decode budget and explain their shared accounting boundary.

  2. Why does the constructed batch-one 70B decode example test INT4 weights before speculative decoding or operator fusion?

    1. Because the GPU driver disables CUDA Graphs and compiler optimizations unless weights are formatted in INT4
    2. The assumed profile identifies weight traffic as the first target; reducing its exposed GEMM interval provides a consistent baseline for subsequent experiments
    3. Because speculative decoding cannot mathematically operate on FP16 models
    4. Because operator fusion only works on models with fewer than 10 billion parameters

    Answer: The correct answer is B. The example assumes weight streaming is the first target and tests a 45% reduction in its exposed GEMM interval while leaving other intervals unchanged. It does not infer a batch expansion or simultaneous throughput gain from freed memory. Compiler restrictions, model-size limits, and an inability to speculate in FP16 are not the reason for this order.

    Learning Objective: Justify the architectural sequencing of optimization interventions in a production LLM serving pipeline

  3. Why must a serving controller measure the batch-size crossover for speculative decoding rather than infer a universal cutoff from a batch-one decode budget?

    Answer: Batching already shares weight reads across requests, reducing the spare compute that makes verification economical. Draft overhead, acceptance, cache capacity, and verification kernels determine where speculation stops improving useful service. The batch-one budget establishes no cutoff at 16 or any other batch size. Measure latency and loaded throughput for the draft-target pair under the intended request mix before selecting a policy.

    Learning Objective: Analyze the systems rationale for load-dependent adaptive speculative decoding policies

  4. An 85 ms decode step has exposed intervals of 34 ms GEMM, 20.4 ms attention, 10.2 ms element-wise work, 10.2 ms AllReduce, and 10.2 ms launch/runtime overhead. Reducing only GEMM time by 45% leaves 69.7 ms. Why does this still miss a 50 ms objective?

    1. The InfiniBand network cable was disconnected during the benchmark run
    2. The GPU ran out of power and throttled clock frequency to zero
    3. The unchanged non-GEMM intervals still total 51 ms, so reducing GEMM alone by the assumed amount cannot reach the objective
    4. INT4 quantization caused the model to produce infinite repetitive token loops

    Answer: The correct answer is C. GEMM becomes 34 × 0.55 = 18.7 ms, while all other exposed intervals remain 51 ms; the sum is 69.7 ms. The next experiment must address other critical-path costs. With attention at 15.3 ms, element-wise work at 3.4 ms, and launch/runtime overhead at 2 ms, the later constructed budget totals 49.6 ms; these are assumptions, not benchmark claims.

    Learning Objective: Analyze bottleneck displacement across iterative optimization rounds using timeline breakdown data

← Back to Questions

Self-Check: Answer

  1. A team upgrades their LLM serving cluster from NVIDIA A100 to H100 GPUs, expecting a \(3\times\) reduction in generation latency based on the H100’s \(3\times\) higher peak FP16 TFLOP/s rating. However, batch-1 autoregressive decode latency improves by only ~15%. What fundamental performance engineering fallacy explains this result?

    1. The fallacy that H100 GPUs cannot execute FP16 operations without INT4 emulation
    2. The fallacy that Python cannot run on Hopper-architecture accelerators
    3. The fallacy that PyTorch profiler traces disable GPU Tensor Cores during inference
    4. The fallacy that peak arithmetic alone predicts decode latency; low-intensity weight streaming has a bandwidth ceiling, and other exposed costs can limit the observed gain

    Answer: The correct answer is D. Peak arithmetic does not predict weight-dominated decode: its low intensity gives a bandwidth ceiling. A higher advertised bandwidth raises that ideal ceiling but does not establish the supplied 15% observed latency improvement. Matched traces must check achieved bandwidth, cache behavior, launch overhead, and communication; these unchanged or inefficient intervals can limit the realized gain.

    Learning Objective: Analyze the fallacy of using peak arithmetic FLOP/s ratings to project memory-bandwidth-bound inference performance

  2. Explain why evaluating LLM serving performance purely on average inter-token latency can mask severe production failures, and describe two specific systems engineering techniques used to optimize P99 tail latency.

    Answer: A mean of 30 ms can coexist with p99 of 500 ms for the same request mix; p99 is a percentile, not a worst-case bound. Traces must locate the slow intervals. Preallocated buffers can reduce allocation stalls, while graph replay can reduce exposed host-submission variation. Either helps only when it targets the measured cause; repeat latency measurements at the same offered load to confirm the benefit.

    Learning Objective: Explain the operational divergence between average latency and tail latency in serving systems and identify mitigation techniques

  3. True or False: Upgrading a distributed training workload from FP16 to FP8 precision automatically halves total step time across any multi-node GPU cluster.

    Answer: False. Higher FP8 peak throughput applies to supported arithmetic, not automatically to the entire step. Uncompressed collectives, host dispatch, and unaffected kernels can remain unchanged, while lower precision may also reduce some memory traffic. End-to-end speedup depends on which exposed intervals change and whether model quality remains acceptable.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate the limitations of low-precision arithmetic adoption under Amdahl’s law in distributed training

  4. Why does successful graph compilation not establish that a workload has exploited algorithmic choices such as tiled online attention or draft-and-verify speculative decoding?

    1. Graph compilers apply implemented transformations and supported kernels; compilation alone does not settle algorithm selection, draft-verification correctness, or performance
    2. Graph compilers are restricted by hardware law to only optimize CPU assembly code
    3. Graph compilers only support models with fewer than 100,000 parameters
    4. Graph compilers disable shared memory allocations on NVIDIA GPUs

    Answer: The correct answer is A. A compiler applies transformations it implements and evaluates with its cost model. It can select or generate a known fused attention kernel when supported, but successful capture alone does not establish that choice. Draft-and-verify execution also needs an explicit algorithmic and correctness contract. Inspect generated execution and measure the workload rather than treating compilation as proof of optimality.

    Learning Objective: Distinguish between the automated optimization scope of graph compilers and the algorithmic innovations required of performance engineers

← Back to Questions

Self-Check: Answer

  1. For memory-bound decode, identify how operator fusion, CUDA Graph replay, and speculative decoding change the execution budget. Contrast speculative decoding with MoE routing in a compute-bound regime. Why does each intervention still require end-to-end measurement?

    Answer: Fusion can remove intermediate HBM transfers and launches; graph replay reduces repeated host submission. In memory-bound decode, speculation amortizes target weight bytes over emitted tokens while adding draft and verification work. In compute-bound execution, MoE can reduce active arithmetic but adds routing and communication. None has a universal one-to-one mapping to a single cost term. Dependencies, quality, resource contention, and remaining exposed intervals determine the measured gain.

    Learning Objective: Analyze which execution costs an optimization changes under a stated regime and justify end-to-end verification.

  2. Summarize the concept of ‘optimization as bottleneck displacement’ (the waterbed problem) in performance engineering, and explain why continuous reprofiling is required after every optimization stage.

    Answer: Accelerating the dominant bottleneck in an ML system does not produce infinite speedup; once that term shrinks, non-optimized secondary operations (such as launch overhead, memory-bound LayerNorms, or communication) become the new binding limit. Continuous reprofiling after each intervention is required to verify that the targeted bottleneck actually decreased, measure the newly exposed limiting factor, and ensure engineering effort is directed only at the active bottleneck.

    Learning Objective: Synthesize the principle of bottleneck displacement and justify the iterative profiling feedback loop

  3. For weight-dominated, small-batch LLM decode below the H100 FP16 ridge point, what does the roofline bandwidth ceiling establish about optimization priorities?

    1. Because Tensor Cores are physically disabled during inference workloads
    2. Low arithmetic intensity makes bandwidth the tighter ideal ceiling; measurements must still identify the exposed cost before choosing fusion, precision, or launch-overhead reduction
    3. Because memory bandwidth is free and unlimited in modern cloud data centers
    4. Because compiler graph optimizations only work on memory access instructions

    Answer: The correct answer is B. Low intensity places the ideal ceiling on the bandwidth slope, so faster arithmetic alone does not raise that ceiling. It does not establish the observed critical-path cause: launch gaps, dependencies, occupancy, and communication can keep execution below it. Traces and counters determine whether bytes, dispatch, or another exposed interval should be targeted first.

    Learning Objective: Distinguish a roofline bandwidth ceiling from an observed critical-path bottleneck.
